Clinical Radiology Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Lobar airspace consolidation with preserved volume, typical of pneumococcal infection): Homogeneous opacity with air bronchograms that respects a fissure and does not shrink the lobe is airspace consolidation, classically pneumococcal. Bronchopneumonia is multifocal and patchy rather than confined to a lobe. Obstructive collapse would pull the fissure toward the opacity and reduce volume. Interstitial pneumonia gives reticular rather than airspace shadowing.
  2. The correct answer is D (Treat with antibiotics and repeat the chest radiograph after clinical recovery): Round pneumonia is a common childhood pattern of pneumococcal infection, because poorly developed collateral pathways keep the consolidation spherical. With a febrile child and typical features, treatment and follow-up radiography are appropriate. CT would expose the child to radiation unnecessarily. Bronchoscopy and PET belong to adult mass assessment and add no value here.
  3. The correct answer is A (Klebsiella pneumoniae causing expansile necrotising lobar pneumonia in a susceptible host): A bulging fissure indicates an expansile exudate, characteristic of Klebsiella in alcoholic patients, and low-attenuation regions suggest necrosis. Pneumococcus produces lobar consolidation but usually without bulging. Mycoplasma is patchy and interstitial. Pneumocystis requires immunosuppression and gives a diffuse perihilar pattern.
  4. The correct answer is D (Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia with secondary thin-walled cyst formation): Perihilar ground-glass change with subpleural sparing and thin-walled cysts in a profoundly immunosuppressed patient is classic Pneumocystis infection. Cytomegalovirus is uncommon and tends to nodules or consolidation. Alveolar proteinosis gives crazy-paving but does not fit the clinical context. Hypersensitivity pneumonitis requires an antigen exposure and shows nodules with air trapping.
  5. The correct answer is B (Post-primary tuberculosis reactivation in the apical and posterior segments): Reactivation tuberculosis favours the apical and posterior upper lobe segments and the superior segments of the lower lobes, with cavitation and volume loss. Primary disease tends to cause lymphadenopathy and consolidation anywhere, often in the lower or mid zones. Squamous carcinoma may cavitate but the clinical picture and site favour infection. Septic emboli are multiple and peripheral.
  6. The correct answer is D (Random distribution, consistent with haematogenous spread of infection): Uniform nodules with no relation to septa, fissures or secondary lobule structures define a random pattern, expected with haematogenous seeding in miliary tuberculosis or metastases. Perilymphatic nodules follow the pleura and bronchovascular bundles, as in sarcoid. Centrilobular nodules spare the pleural surface. Tree-in-bud reflects endobronchial spread rather than blood-borne disease.
  7. The correct answer is D (Aspergilloma (mycetoma) colonising a pre-existing post-tuberculous upper lobe cavity): A mobile intracavitary mass with a gravity-dependent air crescent in an old cavity is a fungus ball. Angioinvasive disease occurs in neutropenic patients and shows a halo sign. Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis shows central bronchiectasis and mucus impaction. An abscess has an air-fluid level and does not contain a mobile solid ball.
  8. The correct answer is A (Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis with central upper lobe bronchiectasis): Central bronchiectasis in the upper lobes with high-attenuation mucus in an asthmatic with eosinophilia and raised IgE is allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis. Cystic fibrosis causes more diffuse upper lobe predominant disease with peripheral involvement and no hyperattenuating mucus. Ciliary dyskinesia favours lower and middle lobes. Williams-Campbell is rare and unrelated to allergy.
  9. The correct answer is C (Halo sign suggesting angioinvasive fungal infection, so start antifungal therapy promptly): A nodule with a ground-glass halo in a prolonged neutropenic patient is angioinvasive aspergillosis until proved otherwise, and early antifungal treatment improves survival. The halo can reflect haemorrhage around infarct, but that is the mechanism, not a reason to delay therapy. Reversed halo describes a different pattern. Metastases would not explain the setting.
  10. The correct answer is D (Typical usual interstitial pneumonia pattern, consistent with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis): Basal subpleural predominant reticulation with honeycombing and traction bronchiectasis is a typical UIP pattern, and with a compatible clinical picture permits a diagnosis of idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis without biopsy. NSIP spares the subpleural lung and has more ground-glass opacity. Chronic hypersensitivity pneumonitis would show air trapping and upper zone features. An indeterminate category requires absent honeycombing.
  11. The correct answer is C (Fibrotic nonspecific interstitial pneumonia associated with systemic sclerosis): Subpleural sparing with basal ground-glass, reticulation and traction bronchiectasis in systemic sclerosis points to NSIP, the commonest interstitial pattern in that disease. UIP would show honeycombing reaching the pleura. Desquamative interstitial pneumonia is linked to smoking. Lymphocytic interstitial pneumonia is associated with Sjogren syndrome and shows cysts and nodules.
  12. The correct answer is B (Cryptogenic organising pneumonia demonstrating the reversed halo sign): Peripheral and peribronchovascular consolidation with a reversed halo in a patient unresponsive to antibiotics typifies organising pneumonia. Chronic eosinophilic pneumonia looks similar but needs eosinophilia and favours the upper zones. Mucormycosis can show a reversed halo but only in immunosuppressed or diabetic patients. Infarction is wedge-shaped with a pleural base.
  13. The correct answer is A (Subacute hypersensitivity pneumonitis with the three-density pattern of mosaic attenuation): Centrilobular ground-glass nodules with lobular air trapping give the three-density pattern, and the pigeon exposure supports subacute hypersensitivity pneumonitis. Respiratory bronchiolitis ILD needs a smoker and rarely shows lobular air trapping. Chronic fibrotic disease would show reticulation, traction bronchiectasis and honeycombing. Nonspecific interstitial pneumonia is basal and lacks nodules and air trapping.
  14. The correct answer is A (Sarcoidosis, with perilymphatic nodules developing along bronchovascular bundles and fissures): Symmetric bilateral hilar and right paratracheal adenopathy with erythema nodosum is the Lofgren presentation of sarcoidosis, and parenchymal disease follows a perilymphatic distribution. Lymphoma is usually asymmetric and the patient would be unwell. Tuberculosis is usually unilateral with different nodule patterns. Silicosis needs exposure.
  15. The correct answer is B (Serum alpha-1 antitrypsin level with phenotype or genotype testing): Early onset basal emphysema with a family history in a non-smoker points to alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, confirmed by serum level and phenotype or genotype. VEGF-D suits lymphangioleiomyomatosis, which gives diffuse thin-walled cysts rather than basal emphysema. FLCN testing suits Birt-Hogg-Dube, with lower zone cysts and pneumothoraces. Further CT would describe the disease but not name its cause.
  16. The correct answer is B (Bronchoarterial ratio greater than one with lack of bronchial tapering): Bronchiectasis is defined by bronchial dilatation, shown as a bronchoarterial ratio above one and failure of the airway to taper. Tree-in-bud and air trapping commonly accompany it but do not define it. Volume loss with crowding occurs in collapse and in bronchiectasis alike, so it does not establish the diagnosis.
  17. The correct answer is D (No routine follow-up is needed, as risk is low at this size): Fleischner 2017 advises no routine follow-up for solid nodules under 6 mm in low-risk patients, and this lifelong non-smoker has no risk features. An optional 12 month CT applies only to high-risk patients. Interval CT at 6 to 12 months applies to 6 to 8 mm nodules. PET has poor sensitivity below 8 mm.
  18. The correct answer is A (Referral for surgical resection or tissue diagnosis, as the persistent solid component exceeds 6 mm): A persistent part-solid nodule with a solid component of 6 mm or more is highly suspicious for invasive adenocarcinoma, and Fleischner recommends biopsy or resection. Surveillance schedules apply to pure ground-glass nodules or part-solid nodules with solid component under 6 mm. PET is unreliable in lesions dominated by ground-glass attenuation.
  19. The correct answer is D (Category 4A, with a three-month low-dose CT and consideration of PET-CT): At baseline, a solid nodule of 8 mm to under 15 mm is Lung-RADS 4A, managed with a three-month low-dose CT and optionally PET-CT if there is a solid component of 8 mm or more. Category 3 covers solid nodules of 6 to under 8 mm. Category 4B starts at 15 mm. Category 2 covers nodules under 6 mm.
  20. The correct answer is C (Pulmonary hamartoma, the commonest benign neoplasm of the lung): Fat within a stable lobulated lung nodule, with popcorn calcification, is virtually diagnostic of hamartoma. A granuloma has dense central or laminated calcification and no fat. A chondroid metastasis can calcify similarly but would not be stable and contains no fat. Adenocarcinoma has a spiculated margin, and internal fat argues against malignancy.
  21. The correct answer is C (Progression of adenocarcinoma in situ toward invasive adenocarcinoma with lepidic growth): A persistent ground-glass nodule that develops a solid component and bubble-like lucencies reflects progression from in situ to invasive adenocarcinoma. Organising pneumonia changes within weeks to months. Marginal zone lymphoma is usually consolidative with air bronchograms. Atypical adenomatous hyperplasia is typically under 5 mm, so a 22 mm lesion is not explained by it.
  22. The correct answer is A (Invasion of the brachial plexus roots above T1 or vertebral body involvement): For a superior sulcus tumour, MRI is best for showing invasion of the brachial plexus above T1, subclavian vessels and vertebral bodies, which define T4 disease and often preclude resection. First rib invasion alone is T3 and can be resected en bloc. Nodal and pleural findings matter but do not define local resectability in this way.
  23. The correct answer is A (N2a, as single-station ipsilateral mediastinal or subcarinal nodal involvement): The 9th edition subdivides N2 into N2a, a single ipsilateral mediastinal or subcarinal station, and N2b, multiple stations. The subcarinal station is mediastinal, so N1 is wrong. N3 requires contralateral mediastinal, hilar or supraclavicular disease. This subdivision carries prognostic differences and informs treatment planning.
  24. The correct answer is B (Small cell carcinoma, typically centrally located with bulky mediastinal nodal disease): A small primary with disproportionately bulky confluent mediastinal nodes causing SVC obstruction is the classic presentation of small cell carcinoma in a heavy smoker. Squamous carcinoma is central but usually has a larger primary and less nodal bulk. Lymphoma gives nodal bulk but not a hilar primary mass. Adenocarcinoma is usually peripheral.
  25. The correct answer is D (Empyema, demonstrated by the split pleura sign with enhancing pleural layers): Enhancing separated visceral and parietal pleura around a lentiform collection with obtuse angles is the split pleura sign of empyema, often with extrapleural fat thickening. A lung abscess is round with thick irregular walls and acute angles. Transudates lack pleural enhancement. Metastases produce nodular rather than smooth thickening.
  26. The correct answer is B (Malignant pleural mesothelioma related to prior asbestos exposure): Circumferential nodular pleural thickening over 1 cm with mediastinal pleural involvement and a shrunken hemithorax in an asbestos-exposed man is mesothelioma. Metastases can mimic it, but the exposure history favours mesothelioma. Pleural lymphoma is rare and usually shows prominent nodes. Benign diffuse thickening is smooth, tends to spare the mediastinal pleura and is not nodular.
  27. The correct answer is A (Asbestosis, a UIP-like fibrosis requiring significant exposure and a latency period): Basal subpleural fibrosis with honeycombing, pleural plaques and heavy asbestos exposure with a long latency is asbestosis, a UIP-like fibrosis. Idiopathic UIP looks identical but plaques and exposure make asbestos the better explanation. Nonspecific interstitial pneumonia spares the subpleural lung. Silicosis is upper zone nodular.
  28. The correct answer is B (Chronic simple silicosis with eggshell calcification of hilar lymph nodes): Upper zone small nodules with hilar adenopathy and eggshell nodal calcification in a sandblaster indicate silicosis. Sarcoidosis gives similar nodes and nodules but rarely eggshell calcification and has no silica exposure. Berylliosis needs beryllium exposure. Coal workers pneumoconiosis follows coal dust and tends to cause massive fibrosis.
  29. The correct answer is D (Progressive massive fibrosis, with a risk of cavitation and superimposed mycobacterial infection): Upper zone conglomerate masses in a coal worker are progressive massive fibrosis, which migrate toward the hila, can cavitate and predispose to tuberculosis. Caplan syndrome shows peripheral nodules in a patient with rheumatoid arthritis. Bilateral symmetrical masses in this setting are not carcinoma. Sarcoid masses are possible but the exposure history points to pneumoconiosis.
  30. The correct answer is A (Thymoma, associated with myasthenia gravis through antibodies to acetylcholine receptors): A solid calcified anterior mediastinal mass without invasion in an adult with fatigable weakness is a thymoma, which is associated with myasthenia gravis through acetylcholine receptor antibodies. Thymic carcinoma invades and metastasises. Teratoma contains fat and cysts in younger patients. Primary mediastinal B-cell lymphoma occurs in young women and is not linked to myasthenia.
  31. The correct answer is D (Mature cystic teratoma, a benign germ cell tumour): Fat, calcification resembling a tooth and a cystic component in an anterior mediastinal mass are diagnostic of mature teratoma, and normal markers support benignity. Seminoma is homogeneous without fat. Thymolipoma contains fat but not teeth or cystic elements. Non-seminomatous tumours have raised markers and aggressive features.
  32. The correct answer is D (Nodular sclerosing Hodgkin lymphoma involving the thymus and mediastinal nodes): A young adult with B symptoms, pruritus, a bulky anterior mediastinal mass and cervical and middle mediastinal nodes most likely has nodular sclerosing Hodgkin lymphoma. Primary mediastinal B-cell lymphoma tends to stay confined to the mediastinum. Seminoma is homogeneous and often has raised markers. T-lymphoblastic lymphoma is more rapid with effusion.
  33. The correct answer is B (Neuroblastoma arising from the sympathetic chain in the posterior mediastinum): A paravertebral tumour in a young child with stippled calcification and foraminal extension is a neuroblastoma. Ganglioneuroma is the benign mature counterpart and typically presents later. Neurenteric cysts accompany vertebral anomalies. Extramedullary haematopoiesis is bilateral in chronic haemolytic anaemia.
  34. The correct answer is C (Bronchogenic cyst, a foregut duplication cyst of the middle mediastinum): A smooth, non-enhancing, thin-walled cystic lesion in the subcarinal middle mediastinum is typical of a bronchogenic cyst. Necrotic nodes enhance at the rim. An oesophageal duplication cyst abuts the oesophageal wall. A pericardial cyst lies in the cardiophrenic angle.
  35. The correct answer is A (Right upper lobe collapse due to a central obstructing tumour (Golden S sign)): Elevation of the horizontal fissure with a central convex hilar bulge produces the Golden S sign, indicating right upper lobe collapse around a central mass. Middle lobe collapse obscures the right heart border. Consolidation preserves volume and does not move the fissure. Lower lobe collapse displaces the oblique fissure.
  36. The correct answer is D (Left lower lobe collapse, producing the sail sign behind the heart): A triangular retrocardiac opacity with loss of the medial hemidiaphragm outline and downward hilar displacement is typical of left lower lobe collapse. Left upper lobe collapse veils the hemithorax and shifts the hilum upward. Lower lobe consolidation shows air bronchograms without volume loss. A subpulmonic effusion has a lateral peak and no triangle.
  37. The correct answer is A (The lingula of the left upper lobe, adjacent to the left heart border): The silhouette sign says that an opacity touching a border of the same density obliterates that border. The left heart border abuts the lingula, so lingular disease removes it while the aortic knob and diaphragm remain visible. Posterior segment disease would erase the aortic arch. Lower lobe disease affects the diaphragm or descending aorta.
  38. The correct answer is D (Right middle lobe collapse, which may be caused by a narrow lobar bronchus): Middle lobe collapse obscures the right heart border and appears on the lateral film as a thin wedge between the minor and major fissures converging at the hilum. Consolidation keeps volume and shows air bronchograms. Lower lobe collapse obscures the diaphragm instead. A loculated fissural effusion is lens shaped and leaves the heart border visible.
  39. The correct answer is B (Nontuberculous mycobacterial infection with nodular bronchiectatic disease (Lady Windermere pattern)): Right middle lobe and lingular bronchiectasis with tree-in-bud nodules in an older thin woman with a nontuberculous acid-fast isolate is nodular bronchiectatic Mycobacterium avium complex disease. Allergic aspergillosis is central and upper lobe predominant. Cystic fibrosis presents in younger patients with diffuse disease. Aspiration favours dependent lower lobes.
  40. The correct answer is C (Anteromedial pneumothorax shown by the deep sulcus sign in a supine patient): In a supine patient pleural air collects anteriorly and medially, so the costophrenic sulcus appears deep and lucent, and the heart border becomes unusually crisp. An effusion would increase density. A bulla has a thin wall and lies within lung. A skin fold shows a line extending beyond the chest cavity with vessels visible beyond it.
  41. The correct answer is B (Lymphangitic carcinomatosis from lymphatic spread of metastatic breast cancer): Patchy asymmetric nodular septal and bronchovascular thickening with adenopathy in a patient with known breast cancer represents lymphatic spread of tumour. Oedema is symmetric and gravity dependent. Sarcoid can be similar but the clinical context favours metastasis. UIP shows architectural distortion and honeycombing.
  42. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary alveolar proteinosis showing the crazy-paving appearance on HRCT): Geographic crazy-paving ground-glass opacity with smooth septal thickening sharply demarcated from normal lung in an afebrile man points to pulmonary alveolar proteinosis, in which surfactant fills alveoli. Pneumocystis needs immunosuppression and gives a perihilar pattern. Cardiogenic oedema is gravity dependent with cardiac signs. Mucinous adenocarcinoma is consolidative and progressive.
  43. The correct answer is A (Pulmonary Langerhans cell histiocytosis, a smoking-related interstitial lung disease): Bizarre cysts and nodules sparing the costophrenic angles in a young smoker are typical of pulmonary Langerhans cell histiocytosis. Lymphangioleiomyomatosis affects women and shows uniform round cysts without sparing. Birt-Hogg-Dube cysts are basal and subpleural. Lymphocytic interstitial pneumonia is linked to Sjogren syndrome.
  44. The correct answer is C (Lymphangioleiomyomatosis associated with renal angiomyolipoma, linked to tuberous sclerosis): Diffuse uniform thin-walled cysts with chylous effusions and a fat-containing renal mass indicate lymphangioleiomyomatosis with angiomyolipoma, as in tuberous sclerosis. Langerhans disease produces bizarre cysts and nodules in smokers. Birt-Hogg-Dube is associated with chromophobe and oncocytic tumours. Lymphocytic pneumonia needs Sjogren features.
  45. The correct answer is A (Septic pulmonary emboli secondary to tricuspid valve endocarditis): Peripheral cavitating nodules, some wedge shaped with a feeding vessel sign, in an intravenous drug user with fever and a new tricuspid murmur indicate septic emboli from tricuspid endocarditis. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis needs sinus or renal disease. Bland infarcts do not cavitate and the patient is not febrile. Cavitating metastases have thick irregular walls and an oncological history.
  46. The correct answer is D (Identify the venous drainage and the origin of the anomalous systemic artery before resection): Sequestration is a segment of lung without normal bronchial communication supplied by a systemic artery, most often from the descending aorta. Surgeons must know the arterial origin, which can be large and bleed, and the venous drainage. Bronchoscopy would not demonstrate a normal connection by definition. PET is not indicated.
  47. The correct answer is B (Transcatheter embolisation of the feeding artery with coils or plugs): A pulmonary arteriovenous malformation with a feeding artery of 3 mm or more in suspected hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia is treated by transcatheter embolisation to prevent paradoxical embolism. Surgery is reserved for failed embolisation. Antibiotic prophylaxis is advised but does not treat the shunt. Echocardiography adds little before treatment.
  48. The correct answer is C (Ruptured pulmonary hydatid cyst showing the water-lily sign): A well-defined cyst with an air crescent in a person from a sheep-rearing region is a ruptured Echinococcus granulosus hydatid cyst, and the collapsed endocyst floating in fluid gives the water-lily sign. Aspergilloma occurs in a previous cavity with a mobile ball. A lung abscess has shaggy walls and surrounding consolidation. An infected bronchogenic cyst lacks endemic exposure.
  49. The correct answer is A (Swyer-James (MacLeod) syndrome from postinfective obliterative bronchiolitis in childhood): A unilateral small hyperlucent lung with a small hilum and air trapping after childhood bronchiolitis is Swyer-James syndrome. Pulmonary artery agenesis lacks air trapping. A pneumothorax shows a pleural line and a collapsed lung rather than volume loss with preserved lung markings. Congenital lobar overinflation enlarges the affected lobe.
  50. The correct answer is A (Tissue sampling of the nodule or nodes, since FDG uptake cannot separate infection from cancer): FDG uptake occurs in granulomatous infection as well as cancer, so in an endemic area with avid nodes tissue confirmation is needed before resection or mediastinal staging decisions. A high SUV is not diagnostic. Waiting three months delays potentially curable cancer. Empirical therapy without tissue risks missing carcinoma.
  51. The correct answer is D (Fibrinous septations or echogenic swirling material within the pleural fluid): Fibrinous septations and echogenic swirling material suggest an exudate with fibrin, typically a complicated parapneumonic effusion needing drainage. Anechoic fluid without structure is more often a transudate or simple effusion. Nodular pleural thickening suggests malignancy. A large anechoic effusion with atelectasis is simple in character and may be tapped for symptoms only.
  52. The correct answer is C (Rounded atelectasis associated with asbestos-related pleural disease and pleural thickening): A pleural-based rounded mass with the comet-tail sign, adjacent pleural thickening and volume loss in an asbestos-exposed patient is rounded atelectasis, a folded lung. Mesothelioma forms a nodular mass without curving vessels. Round pneumonia is a febrile consolidation with air bronchograms. Adenocarcinoma has spiculated margins and no comet tail.
  53. The correct answer is B (Air trapping on expiratory imaging in the same lung as the fibrosis): Mosaic attenuation with air trapping, along with mid and upper zone and peribronchovascular predominance, favours chronic hypersensitivity pneumonitis over idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Basal subpleural honeycombing and traction bronchiectasis occur in both diseases and do not discriminate. Reticulation without air trapping is nonspecific.
  54. The correct answer is C (Left upper lobe collapse with the luftsichel sign from hyperinflated superior segment of the lower lobe): A veil-like haze with elevated hilum and a lucent crescent around the aortic knob, the luftsichel sign, is typical of left upper lobe collapse because the hyperexpanded superior segment of the lower lobe fills the apex. Lower lobe collapse depresses the hilum. Lingular consolidation obscures the heart border. An effusion would push the mediastinum away.
  55. The correct answer is D (Cystic fibrosis, with bronchiectasis predominantly affecting the upper lobes): Upper lobe predominant bronchiectasis with mucus plugging, air trapping, failure to thrive and pancreatic insufficiency is cystic fibrosis. Primary ciliary dyskinesia favours the middle lobe, lingula and lower lobes. Allergic aspergillosis is central. Antibody deficiency causes lower lobe disease without pancreatic insufficiency.
  56. The correct answer is B (Radiation pneumonitis, defined by a geometric margin matching the treatment field): Consolidation with a straight margin that ignores fissures, arising months after breast radiotherapy, matches radiation pneumonitis conforming to the treatment portal. Radiation-associated organising pneumonia is tempting because it follows radiotherapy, but it spreads beyond the field and is migratory. Drug pneumonitis and viral pneumonitis are tempting with cough and low-grade fever, but both are bilateral and patchy rather than geometrically bounded.
  57. The correct answer is D (Idiopathic dilated cardiomyopathy with intramyocardial fibrosis and raised arrhythmic risk): Non-ischaemic dilated cardiomyopathy classically shows a linear mid-wall stripe in the septum, sparing the subendocardium, with normal coronary arteries. Infarct scar is subendocardial or transmural and follows a coronary territory. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy shows patchy enhancement in hypertrophied segments, with insertion point enhancement being nonspecific and with a thick septum. Amyloid causes diffuse subendocardial or transmural enhancement with a small, thick, stiff ventricle rather than dilatation.
  58. The correct answer is A (Markedly raised native T1 with expanded extracellular volume fraction): Cardiac amyloid expands the extracellular space, so native T1 and extracellular volume are both markedly elevated, and the abnormal gadolinium kinetics explain the nulling problem. Iron overload shortens T2 star and T1 instead. Regional T2 prolongation fits acute infarction or myocarditis. Normal native T1 would be unusual with diffuse enhancement and this clinical picture.
  59. The correct answer is D (Acute myocarditis with epicardial inflammatory injury of the inferolateral wall): Subepicardial enhancement with matching oedema in the lateral wall after a viral illness and normal coronary arteries is typical of acute myocarditis. Infarction would begin at the subendocardium and follow a vascular territory. Takotsubo usually shows apical or mid-ventricular dysfunction with oedema but little or no enhancement. Sarcoid favours basal septal mid-wall disease and nodal findings.
  60. The correct answer is D (Extensive scar on late gadolinium imaging, above fifteen percent of ventricular mass): Extensive scar, commonly defined as fifteen percent or more of left ventricular mass, is an established adverse marker for ventricular arrhythmia in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Systolic anterior motion explains the gradient but is not itself the strongest arrhythmic marker. Mild atrial dilatation is common. Hinge point enhancement is frequent and minor, so it does not carry the same weight.
  61. The correct answer is B (FDG PET after dietary preparation to show active myocardial inflammation): Patchy non-vascular enhancement, heart block and hilar nodes suggest cardiac sarcoidosis, and FDG PET after a high fat, low carbohydrate preparation shows whether inflammation is active and treatable. Stress perfusion MRI addresses ischaemia, which does not explain this distribution. Coronary CT and calcium scoring answer atherosclerosis questions, not inflammatory activity.
  62. The correct answer is C (Regional myocardial oedema on T2 imaging with absent or minimal late gadolinium enhancement): Stress-induced cardiomyopathy produces reversible wall motion abnormality beyond one coronary territory, with oedema but little scar. Dense transmural enhancement with microvascular obstruction points to a completed infarct. Inferolateral subepicardial enhancement is the myocarditis signature. Diffuse subendocardial enhancement with high native T1 suggests amyloid.
  63. The correct answer is A (Arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy with fibrofatty replacement of the right ventricular myocardium): Right ventricular dilatation, regional dyskinesia with aneurysmal pouches and mural enhancement with left bundle branch block tachycardia define arrhythmogenic cardiomyopathy. Ebstein anomaly would show displaced tricuspid leaflets and a large right atrium. Outflow tract tachycardia has a structurally normal heart. Chronic pulmonary hypertension would show hypertrophy and septal flattening, not focal aneurysms.
  64. The correct answer is C (Substantial viable myocardium remains, so contractile recovery is likely): The likelihood of functional recovery falls as enhancement extends across the wall, and segments with enhancement below fifty percent of thickness, especially under twenty-five percent, often improve after revascularisation. Transmural enhancement would predict poor recovery. MRI directly estimates viability. Microvascular obstruction appears as a dark core within enhanced tissue in the acute phase, not here.
  65. The correct answer is B (Microvascular obstruction, predicting larger infarct size, adverse remodelling and worse clinical outcome): A hypoenhancing core within a hyperenhanced infarct reflects capillary obstruction that prevents contrast entry, and it independently predicts larger final infarct size, remodelling and adverse events. Viable tissue would enhance less, not appear as a persistent core. Haemorrhage often accompanies it but does not erase prognostic weight. It is a real physiological finding rather than an artefact.
  66. The correct answer is C (Severe myocardial iron overload with high risk of heart failure, warranting intensified chelation): A T2 star below 10 ms indicates severe cardiac siderosis and carries a high short-term risk of heart failure and arrhythmia even when the ejection fraction is still normal. Normal values exceed 20 ms. Mild to moderate loading falls between 10 and 20 ms. Oedema prolongs T2 rather than shortening T2 star.
  67. The correct answer is C (Secundum atrial septal defect with left-to-right shunting and right heart volume loading): Right atrial and pulmonary arterial enlargement with plethora and a small aortic knuckle fits an atrial septal defect, because the shunt loads the right heart and reduces systemic output. Ductus causes a large aortic knuckle. A ventricular defect enlarges the left heart. Pulmonary stenosis would not give plethora.
  68. The correct answer is D (An Agatston score of 2,300 arbitrary units, above the sex-specific threshold for men): In low-flow, low-gradient disease, CT calcium scoring helps resolve discordance. Severe stenosis is likely above roughly 2,000 arbitrary units in men and 1,200 in women. A score of 900 is below both thresholds, 1,100 is under the male cut-off, and 400 suggests sclerosis or mild disease.
  69. The correct answer is C (Periodic MRI or ECG-gated CT of the whole thoracic aorta): Bicuspid valve disease is associated with ascending aortopathy, and echocardiography often visualises the mid ascending aorta poorly. Serial MRI or gated CT of the entire aorta is recommended for a dilated segment. Symptom-triggered imaging misses silent progression. Elective repair is usually considered around 55 mm, or lower with risk factors, not at 46 mm.
  70. The correct answer is D (Rheumatic mitral stenosis with left atrial dilatation and pulmonary venous hypertension): A normal-sized left ventricle with straightening of the left heart border, a double density and carinal splaying points to rheumatic mitral stenosis. Mixed mitral disease is tempting but would enlarge the left ventricle. A left atrial myxoma can mimic mitral obstruction, yet rheumatic history and the radiographic pattern favour stenosis. Chronic thromboembolic hypertension also causes haemoptysis but does not produce left atrial enlargement or venous diversion.
  71. The correct answer is C (Respiratory septal shift in early diastole, termed ventricular interdependence): Thickened or calcified pericardium with tubular ventricles suggests constriction, and respiratory septal shift on real-time cine reflects exaggerated ventricular interdependence. Restrictive cardiomyopathy usually shows biatrial enlargement and normal pericardium. Subendocardial enhancement with nulling problems suggests amyloid. Constriction preserves systolic function.
  72. The correct answer is A (Epicardial fat stripe sign, showing a moderate or large pericardial effusion): On the lateral film a soft tissue band separating the epicardial fat from the anterior mediastinal fat indicates fluid within the pericardial sac, which explains the globular heart. Epicardial lipomatosis widens fat but leaves no band. Anterior nodal disease is tempting in breast cancer but would not split the fat layers. Constrictive pericarditis is tempting after radiotherapy but usually gives a normal or small heart rather than a globular one, with or without calcification.
  73. The correct answer is C (Reassurance, with imaging follow-up only if symptoms arise or the lesion enlarges): A simple pericardial cyst has a thin imperceptible wall, water density, no enhancement and a typical right cardiophrenic location, and is benign. Observation is appropriate. Biopsy or resection is reserved for atypical or symptomatic lesions. Mediastinoscopy addresses nodal staging, not this finding.
  74. The correct answer is C (Congenital partial absence of the left pericardium with lung interposed between vessels): Lung tissue herniating between the aortic arch and pulmonary artery, with levoposition and a prominent left atrial appendage, indicates absence of the left pericardium. Pectus deformity displaces the heart but does not create a lucent band between vessels. Pulmonary agenesis has no lung to herniate. Constriction would show thickened calcified pericardium.
  75. The correct answer is A (Stanford type A dissection, treated by urgent open surgical repair of the ascending aorta): A flap beginning in the ascending aorta is Stanford type A, and the usual initial treatment is urgent open repair because of the risks of rupture, tamponade and coronary or valve involvement. Medical therapy alone is the pathway for uncomplicated type B disease. DeBakey type II is confined to the ascending aorta, whereas this flap reaches the iliac bifurcation. Descending stent grafting does not treat the ascending entry tear.
  76. The correct answer is C (The false lumen, shown by cobwebs and the beak sign at its leading edge): The false lumen is usually larger, enhances later, wraps around the true lumen, and shows cobwebs, which are residual strands of media, and the beak sign where dissection propagates. The true lumen is typically smaller, enhances early, and is often compressed. Options placing larger calibre or delayed filling in the true lumen reverse these rules.
  77. The correct answer is D (Acute intramural haematoma of the descending aorta as a variant of acute aortic syndrome): Hyperattenuating crescentic wall thickening on unenhanced images, without enhancement or a flap, defines intramural haematoma. A penetrating ulcer demonstrates a contrast-filled outpouching. Chronic thrombus is lower in attenuation and associated with aneurysm. Aortitis shows concentric, enhancing, often longer segment thickening in younger patients.
  78. The correct answer is B (Penetrating atherosclerotic ulcer with a localised intramural haematoma): A focal contrast-filled outpouching beyond calcified intima in a heavily atherosclerotic aorta, with adjacent crescentic haematoma, is a penetrating atherosclerotic ulcer. Pure intramural haematoma lacks the outpouching. A dissection with a thrombosed false lumen can show an ulcer-like projection but needs a flap. A true saccular aneurysm involves all wall layers and lacks a wall haematoma.
  79. The correct answer is B (Grade III, pseudoaneurysm, usually warranting urgent endovascular repair): The grading is I intimal tear, II intramural haematoma, III pseudoaneurysm, IV rupture. A focal contrast outpouching with disrupted intima and contained haematoma is a pseudoaneurysm, which is treated by thoracic endovascular repair in most centres. Grades I and II can be managed non-operatively, and rupture needs free extravasation.
  80. The correct answer is D (Both are displaced to the right of the midline by mediastinal haematoma): Haematoma around the aortic isthmus pushes the trachea and oesophagus, and therefore a nasogastric tube, to the right. Other signs are a widened mediastinum, a left apical cap, depression of the left main bronchus and loss of the aortopulmonary window. Leftward displacement is not expected with isthmic haematoma.
  81. The correct answer is A (Repeat the scan with ECG-gating to remove cardiac motion from the proximal aorta): Pulsation of the aortic root and proximal ascending aorta produces a pseudo-flap on non-gated studies, and a gated acquisition freezes motion, resolving the question. Surgery for an unconfirmed flap is premature. Delayed unenhanced imaging looks for wall haematoma but does not solve motion. More contrast does not correct temporal blur.
  82. The correct answer is C (Large vessel vasculitis affecting the aorta and its main branches in a young woman): Concentric mural thickening with a double ring appearance, long smooth stenoses of the aorta and branches, and a young woman as patient point to Takayasu arteritis. Fibromuscular dysplasia shows beading without mural enhancement. Atherosclerosis is eccentric and calcified. Rib compression is positional and focal, without arch wall thickening.
  83. The correct answer is B (Chronic thromboembolic pulmonary disease with organised thrombus and pulmonary hypertension): Eccentric, organised, wall-adherent thrombus with obtuse angles, webs, mosaic perfusion and bronchial artery hypertrophy characterise chronic thromboembolic disease. Acute embolus forms acute angles and a central filling defect, often with a polo mint sign. Veno-occlusive disease shows septal thickening and ground glass. Idiopathic arterial hypertension lacks thrombus.
  84. The correct answer is B (Ventilation perfusion scintigraphy, which avoids iodinated contrast and suits a clear radiograph): With renal impairment, a prior severe contrast reaction and a normal chest radiograph, a ventilation perfusion study is most suitable and likely to be interpretable. CT with premedication still delivers a contrast load. Catheter angiography is invasive and uses iodinated contrast. MR angiography has limited sensitivity for segmental emboli and gadolinium is undesirable at this eGFR.
  85. The correct answer is D (Pulmonary veno-occlusive disease, since vasodilator therapy may precipitate pulmonary oedema): Septal lines, ground glass nodules and lymphadenopathy in a patient with pulmonary hypertension and a normal left atrium suggest pulmonary veno-occlusive disease, where vasodilators can cause oedema, so lung transplant referral is key. Idiopathic arterial disease lacks these parenchymal signs. A normal left atrium argues against left heart failure. Sarcoid fibrosis would not give this pattern.
  86. The correct answer is C (CAD-RADS 3, with consideration of functional assessment by CT flow reserve or stress testing): CAD-RADS 3 covers 50 to 69 percent stenosis, for which functional assessment is considered. CAD-RADS 2 is 25 to 49 percent. CAD-RADS 4A is 70 to 99 percent in one or two vessels, and CAD-RADS 1 is 1 to 24 percent. A 60 percent lesion therefore falls in category 3.
  87. The correct answer is B (High-risk anomaly with an interarterial intramural course, linked to sudden death): Anomalous origin from the opposite sinus with an interarterial course, a slit-like ostium and an intramural segment is the high-risk variant, because compression during exercise causes ischaemia. Retroaortic and prepulmonic courses are usually benign. A fistula describes a connection to a chamber or vessel, not an origin abnormality.
  88. The correct answer is B (Assessment is limited for small stents, so invasive angiography or functional testing is preferred): Blooming and beam hardening from the metal struts make lumen assessment unreliable in stents under about 3 mm, and adjacent calcium worsens this. Invasive angiography or functional testing is therefore the better route. Optimised kernels and heart rate control improve larger stents but cannot rescue a 2.5 mm stent. MRI suffers susceptibility artefact, and a calcium score does not evaluate the lumen.
  89. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary infarction from distal wedging, managed by withdrawing the catheter into a proximal position): A catheter tip beyond the hilum can wedge in a peripheral branch, occlude it and cause infarction with a wedge-shaped opacity and haemoptysis, so withdrawal to a proximal position is correct. Pulmonary artery rupture is tempting because it also causes haemoptysis, but it is an acute haemorrhage rather than a wedge opacity. Septic emboli need bacteraemia, and pneumothorax relates to insertion.
  90. The correct answer is C (Extension raises the tip away from the carina, whereas flexion moves it towards the carina): The tube tip moves with the head: flexion advances it by roughly two centimetres towards the carina and extension withdraws it. The recommended tip position is about 5 cm above the carina with the neck neutral. A tip at 1 cm with flexion risks endobronchial intubation, and the direction is not reversed.
  91. The correct answer is A (Deep sternal wound infection with dehiscence and mediastinitis, confirmed by CT): Retrosternal gas is expected early after surgery but should decrease; an enlarging collection with wire fracture, sternal separation and fever indicates deep wound infection and mediastinitis, and CT defines the extent. Postpericardiotomy syndrome shows effusions without sternal change. Wire migration does not account for expanding gas and fever.
  92. The correct answer is C (Bronchopleural fistula with fluid spilling into the remaining lung): A sudden fall in the air-fluid level with salty sputum and new contralateral opacity indicates a bronchopleural fistula, with fluid spilling into the remaining lung. Empyema is tempting with fever but the level would not fall abruptly. Pulmonary oedema explains contralateral opacity but not the level change or salty fluid. Cardiac herniation shifts the mediastinum, usually with collapse, and does not cause expectoration.
  93. The correct answer is C (Deep sulcus sign, indicating anterior and basal pneumothorax on a supine radiograph): On a supine film free pleural air rises anteriorly and basally, deepening and lightening the costophrenic sulcus, which is the deep sulcus sign of pneumothorax. The continuous diaphragm sign indicates mediastinal air. A traumatic pneumatocele is a lung cavity, not a deep sulcus. A skin fold gives a line crossing the lung edge and does not deepen the sulcus.
  94. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary contusion, which usually begins to clear within three to five days): Contusion appears within hours as non-segmental opacity adjacent to chest wall trauma, often with a subpleural spared rim, and resolves over several days. Aspiration follows a dependent segmental distribution. Laceration produces a cavity or pneumatocele. Fat embolism has a delay of about one to three days and neurological and skin features.
  95. The correct answer is B (Collar sign with dependent viscera sign, the stomach lying against the posterior chest wall): A waist-like constriction of the stomach at the defect is the collar sign, and viscera lying against the posterior ribs with no lung behind them is the dependent viscera sign. The hump and band signs describe liver herniation through a right-sided tear. The other pairs misplace the stomach relative to the lung, which would argue against rupture.
  96. The correct answer is A (Complete main bronchus transection, shown by the fallen lung sign): When a main bronchus is completely transected, the lung falls away from the hilum, lying laterally and inferiorly, which is the fallen lung sign. This is accompanied by persistent air leak and pneumomediastinum. Tension pneumothorax shifts the mediastinum. Oesophageal injury and diaphragmatic hernia produce different findings.
  97. The correct answer is B (Thrombus within the aneurysm with distal embolisation to the hand and digital arteries): A cervical rib can cause repeated arterial compression, post-stenotic aneurysm and mural thrombus that embolises to the hand. Venous thrombosis characterises effort thrombosis, and neurogenic compression affects the lower trunk. Superior vena cava obstruction is a mediastinal problem. Assessment of the distal runoff is essential.
  98. The correct answer is C (MRI with sagittal and coronal T1 sequences, delineating the apical fat planes and nerve roots): MRI with multiplanar T1 imaging best shows invasion of the brachial plexus, subclavian vessels and vertebral foramina in a superior sulcus tumour. CT angiography shows vessels and bone but poorly shows the plexus. PET adds systemic staging with limited anatomical resolution. Supraclavicular ultrasound cannot image the apical mass or the roots.
  99. The correct answer is C (Congenital absence of the pectoralis major muscle, reducing soft tissue density over the lung): A missing anterior axillary fold with an ipsilateral hypoplastic hand indicates Poland syndrome, where absent pectoralis major lowers soft tissue density over a normal lung. Scoliosis can cause a rotated, asymmetrically lucent chest but does not explain the hand. Swyer-James lung is truly hyperlucent with small vessels. Thoracotomy needs a surgical history.
  100. The correct answer is D (Transverse chest diameter divided by the sternum to vertebra distance, with values above 3.25): The Haller index is the transverse inner chest diameter divided by the shortest distance from sternum to vertebral body, and values above 3.25 are the commonly used threshold for surgical consideration. An inverted ratio gives values below one. A threshold of 2.5 is close to the normal mean, and 4.5 is above the accepted cut-off.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Elastofibroma dorsi, a benign pseudotumour needing no biopsy when the appearance is typical): A subscapular, often bilateral lesion in an older woman, isodense to muscle with interspersed fat strands, is characteristic of elastofibroma dorsi, which needs no biopsy when typical. Sarcoma would be enhancing and destructive. A lipoma is uniformly fat. Metastasis would be unusual in this location.
  2. The correct answer is C (Left ventricular mural thrombus, which is avascular and appears dark on late imaging): A non-enhancing, avascular apical mass that is dark on late gadolinium imaging beside bright scar is a mural thrombus in an akinetic infarcted apex. Microvascular obstruction is also dark but lies within the infarct rather than forming a mass. Fibromas enhance brightly on late images, and metastases enhance on first pass perfusion.
  3. The correct answer is A (Atrial myxoma, the commonest primary cardiac tumour in adults, usually treated by surgical excision): A mobile lobulated left atrial mass on a short stalk from the septum near the fossa ovalis, enhancing heterogeneously and causing positional symptoms, is a myxoma. Thrombus does not enhance and favours the appendage. A fibroelastoma arises from a valve leaflet and is small. A sarcoma is broad-based and infiltrative rather than pedunculated.
  4. The correct answer is B (Transcatheter embolisation, and hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia as the associated condition): A nodule with a feeding artery and draining vein is a pulmonary arteriovenous malformation, and the stroke reflects paradoxical embolism through the right-to-left shunt. Embolisation of feeding vessels is the standard treatment, and most lesions are associated with hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia. Lobectomy is rarely needed. Observation is unsafe after a stroke, and Marfan syndrome does not cause these malformations.
  5. The correct answer is D (Azygos vein dilatation draining to the inferior vena cava via lumbar and hemiazygos veins): In superior vena cava obstruction the azygos system enlarges and carries blood to the inferior vena cava through the ascending lumbar and hemiazygos veins, bypassing the block. The azygos does not drain through the coronary sinus. The hemiazygos does not empty into the portal vein, and intercostal veins do not drain into the pulmonary veins.
  6. The correct answer is B (Pneumomediastinum, shown by the continuous diaphragm sign and displaced mediastinal pleura): The continuous diaphragm sign and air outlining the mediastinal pleura signify pneumomediastinum, here following an alveolar rupture dissecting along the bronchovascular sheaths, often called the Macklin effect. Pneumothorax shows a pleural line surrounded by lung. Pneumopericardium does not extend above the pericardial reflection. There is no volume loss for lobar collapse.
  7. The correct answer is D (Coronary ostial obstruction from displaced native leaflets after valve deployment): A coronary height below about 10 to 12 mm combined with shallow sinuses puts the left main ostium at risk of being covered by displaced leaflets, so CT planning measures coronary heights and sinus dimensions. Annular size guides prosthesis selection. Perforation, dissection and mitral injury are not predicted by these measurements.
  8. The correct answer is A (True thrombus, since slow flow artefact fills in on delayed imaging whereas thrombus does not): Slow flow in the appendage can mimic thrombus in the arterial phase, but a delayed acquisition allows contrast to fill the appendage, so a persistent defect confirms thrombus. Pectinate muscles enhance with the myocardium and are not persistent filling defects. A septal aneurysm does not lie in the appendage.
  9. The correct answer is D (Primary graft dysfunction from ischaemia-reperfusion injury, typically within seventy-two hours of surgery): Diffuse oedema-like opacity of the new graft in the first three days, without consolidation or cavitation, is primary graft dysfunction from ischaemia-reperfusion injury. Acute rejection is tempting but peaks later and shows perivascular change. Venous anastomotic obstruction causes graft oedema but needs a visible narrowing. Bacterial pneumonia is tempting but would consolidate.
  10. The correct answer is A (Tunnel length and septal thickness, including adjacent lipomatous hypertrophy of the interatrial septum): Device selection for PFO closure depends chiefly on tunnel length and septal thickness, including lipomatous hypertrophy, because these determine device seating and stability. The Eustachian valve can matter for catheter access but is secondary. Shunt grade informs the indication rather than the plan, and appendage sizing applies to appendage occlusion.
  11. The correct answer is A (Subclavian or aortic arch vessel injury, because of the high energy mechanism required): First rib fractures indicate high energy trauma, and brachiocephalic, subclavian and aortic arch injury should be excluded with CT angiography, particularly with mediastinal widening. Lower rib fractures are associated with splenic or hepatic injury and the twelfth rib with renal injury.
  12. The correct answer is C (Effort thrombosis of the subclavian vein from repetitive compression, known as Paget-Schroetter syndrome): Sudden arm swelling and cyanosis in a young rower, with venographic subclavian occlusion at the costoclavicular space, is effort thrombosis known as Paget-Schroetter syndrome. Catheter-related thrombosis needs an indwelling line. Superior vena cava syndrome affects the face and both arms. Hyperabduction syndrome compresses the vein transiently without a fixed occlusion.
  13. The correct answer is D (LR-5, because 20 mm or larger with hyperenhancement plus one more major feature is definite HCC): In LI-RADS v2018, a 20 mm or larger observation with non-rim arterial phase hyperenhancement and at least one additional major feature (washout, capsule or threshold growth) is LR-5. Washout is not mandatory at this size. LR-4 would apply with hyperenhancement alone at this size. LR-3 and LR-M do not fit, since there are no targetoid or other non-HCC malignant features.
  14. The correct answer is D (LR-4, because hyperenhancement alone at 20 mm or more is probably HCC): Non-rim arterial phase hyperenhancement in an observation of 20 mm or more with no additional major feature is LR-4. LR-5 requires washout, capsule or threshold growth in addition. LR-3 applies to smaller observations with the same feature. LR-2 is not appropriate for a hyperenhancing lesion of this size.
  15. The correct answer is B (LR-M, since targetoid appearances suggest malignancy that may be non-HCC, such as cholangiocarcinoma): Rim arterial enhancement, peripheral washout, delayed central enhancement and a targetoid diffusion pattern are LR-M features, meaning probably or definitely malignant but not specific for HCC. Intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma and combined tumours are the concern. LR-5 and LR-4 assume an HCC pattern, and LR-TIV needs enhancing soft tissue in a vein.
  16. The correct answer is B (LR-TIV, because enhancing soft tissue within a vein indicates tumour invasion regardless of mass category): LR-TIV is assigned when enhancing soft tissue is present in a vein, even if no discrete mass is seen. The expanded vessel, arterial enhancement and diffusion restriction separate tumour thrombus from bland thrombus. LR-5 would ignore the venous invasion. LR-M and LR-4 do not reflect the established LI-RADS rule.
  17. The correct answer is B (Focal nodular hyperplasia, as functioning hepatocytes with abnormal biliary drainage retain the agent): FNH contains normal hepatocytes and abnormal bile ductules, so it usually retains gadoxetate and is iso- or hyperintense on the hepatobiliary phase, with a T2 bright scar. Most adenomas lack OATP expression and appear hypointense in that phase. Fibrolamellar carcinoma has a T2 dark scar, and haemangioma does not retain hepatobiliary agent.
  18. The correct answer is D (HNF1A-inactivated adenoma, characterised by diffuse intralesional fat and a low risk of malignant transformation): Diffuse signal drop on opposed-phase imaging is the hallmark of the HNF1A-inactivated subtype, which has steatotic content and rarely transforms. Inflammatory adenomas show T2 bright atoll signs and persistent enhancement. Beta-catenin-activated adenomas are not fatty and carry the highest malignancy risk. A central scar with uptake belongs to FNH.
  19. The correct answer is A (Cavernous haemangioma, with enhancement that follows the blood pool across all phases): Discontinuous peripheral nodular enhancement that follows the aortic blood pool and fills in centripetally is typical of cavernous haemangioma. Focal nodular hyperplasia enhances homogeneously and early. Hypervascular metastases wash out rather than fill in. Cholangiocarcinoma shows irregular rim enhancement with delayed fibrous retention and does not track the blood pool.
  20. The correct answer is C (Fibrolamellar hepatocellular carcinoma, a tumour of young adults without underlying cirrhosis): A large calcified mass with a T2 hypointense, non-enhancing scar in a young non-cirrhotic patient suggests fibrolamellar carcinoma. FNH has a T2 bright scar that enhances late. Adenoma typically lacks a scar. Hepatoblastoma occurs in young children and nearly always raises alpha-fetoprotein.
  21. The correct answer is A (Gadoxetate-enhanced MRI with diffusion-weighted imaging and a 20 minute hepatobiliary phase): Gadoxetate hepatobiliary phase makes metastases conspicuous as non-enhancing defects against avidly enhancing parenchyma, and diffusion-weighted imaging adds sensitivity for sub-centimetre lesions. Extracellular gadolinium with diffusion-weighted imaging is a reasonable study but lacks the hepatobiliary phase. Repeat CT adds little to the equivocal staging study. Contrast ultrasound has limited coverage and is less sensitive for small deposits.
  22. The correct answer is C (Amoebic liver abscess, typically solitary and in the right lobe near the capsule): A solitary, round, right lobe abscess with a peripheral oedema halo in a traveller points to Entamoeba histolytica. Pyogenic abscesses are usually multiple or multiloculated and often gas-containing. Hydatid disease shows daughter cysts and membranes. A necrotic HCC would arise in cirrhosis.
  23. The correct answer is C (Echinococcal hydatid cyst in an active stage with daughter vesicles): Daughter cysts within a mother cyst and a floating membrane (water lily sign) are characteristic of hydatid disease, with exposure history supporting it. A mucinous cystic neoplasm has septa and nodules but not daughter vesicles. Amoebic abscess is clinically unwell and lacks daughter cysts. Cystic metastasis needs a known primary and lacks a detached membrane.
  24. The correct answer is B (Liver attenuation below 40 Hounsfield units, or at least 10 units lower than spleen): On unenhanced CT, normal liver is about 55 to 65 HU and slightly denser than spleen. A liver under 40 HU, or 10 HU or more below spleen, supports moderate to severe steatosis. A small difference of 5 HU is within the range of mild or no fat. Higher liver attenuation suggests iron or amiodarone, and thresholds do not transfer to contrast phases.
  25. The correct answer is D (Primary haemochromatosis, with parenchymal iron deposition sparing the reticuloendothelial system): In primary haemochromatosis, iron accumulates in hepatocytes and pancreas and spares spleen and marrow. Transfusional siderosis loads the reticuloendothelial system, darkening spleen and marrow. Alcoholic liver disease causes milder iron and does not darken the pancreas. Thalassaemia intermedia shows marrow expansion and usually involves other organs and the reticuloendothelial system.
  26. The correct answer is A (Intrahepatic comma-shaped venous collaterals with absent or thrombosed hepatic veins on Doppler ultrasound): Hepatic venous outflow obstruction produces caudate hypertrophy, because its veins drain directly into the IVC, and patchy enhancement. Absent or thrombosed hepatic veins with comma-shaped intrahepatic collaterals confirm Budd-Chiari syndrome. Portal vein thrombosis leaves the hepatic veins patent. Cardiac congestion dilates the hepatic veins. Arterial thrombosis causes infarcts.
  27. The correct answer is C (Intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma, a fibrous tumour that retains contrast in its centre): Delayed central enhancement reflects fibrous stroma, and capsular retraction with upstream duct dilatation points to intrahepatic cholangiocarcinoma. HCC washes out and typically arises in cirrhosis. Haemangioma follows blood pool with no retraction. An abscess has fever and a gas or fluid component.
  28. The correct answer is B (Type IV, with tumour involving the confluence and both secondary ducts): Bismuth-Corlette type IV involves the confluence plus both secondary radicles, or multifocal tumour, and is generally unresectable by standard resection. Type I stops below the confluence. Type II reaches the confluence only. Type III involves one secondary duct, a for right or b for left.
  29. The correct answer is C (Primary sclerosing cholangitis, with a markedly increased lifetime risk of cholangiocarcinoma): Beading, pruning and multifocal strictures with inflammatory bowel disease indicate PSC, which carries a high risk of cholangiocarcinoma rather than hepatocellular carcinoma. IgG4 cholangitis can mimic PSC but is not the typical disease with ulcerative colitis and responds to steroids. PBC affects small ducts, so the MRCP is usually normal.
  30. The correct answer is B (Primary biliary cholangitis, which targets small intrahepatic ducts and commonly causes reactive portal nodes): PBC affects small bile ducts, so MRCP is normal, and benign periportal lymphadenopathy is common. Positive antimitochondrial antibodies favour PBC over autoimmune hepatitis, which also causes reactive nodes. PSC would show duct strictures on MRCP. Lymphoma is possible but the serology, itch and normal ducts fit PBC better.
  31. The correct answer is B (Corticosteroid trial with repeat imaging to confirm response, after tissue sampling to exclude malignancy): Diffuse sausage-like enlargement with a capsule-like rim, bile duct thickening and raised IgG4 indicates autoimmune pancreatitis type 1, which responds rapidly to steroids. Malignancy still needs reasonable exclusion. Resection is unnecessary if the diagnosis holds. Stenting with brushings alone delays treatment, and watchful waiting leaves obstructive jaundice untreated.
  32. The correct answer is C (Non-visualisation of the gallbladder at 60 minutes with prompt excretion into the small bowel): In acute cholecystitis, cystic duct obstruction prevents tracer entering the gallbladder, so it is not seen at 60 minutes while bowel activity appears promptly. Absent bowel activity as well suggests common duct obstruction. Delayed bowel activity and low ejection fraction after cholecystokinin belong to other disorders where the gallbladder does fill.
  33. The correct answer is A (Adenomyomatosis, a benign hyperplastic condition with Rokitansky-Aschoff sinuses containing cholesterol or small calculi): Comet-tail artefacts from cholesterol crystals in Rokitansky-Aschoff sinuses with focal wall thickening are the signature of adenomyomatosis. Carcinoma shows a mass or irregular thickening with invasion. Xanthogranulomatous cholecystitis is clinically inflammatory with hypoechoic nodules. Chronic cholecystitis is diffuse and associated with stones.
  34. The correct answer is A (Mirizzi syndrome, where an impacted cystic duct stone compresses the common hepatic duct): Gallbladder neck stone with extrinsic common hepatic duct compression and a normal distal duct is Mirizzi syndrome. A distal stone dilates the whole tree down to the stone. A Klatskin tumour creates an irregular infiltrating stricture. Pancreatic head cancer produces double duct dilatation.
  35. The correct answer is C (Todani type I, with complete cyst excision and hepaticojejunostomy to reduce malignant risk): Fusiform dilatation of the extrahepatic duct is Todani type I, the most common form, and carries a risk of cholangiocarcinoma, so excision with Roux-en-Y reconstruction is standard. Type III is a choledochocele. Type V is intrahepatic (Caroli). Type II is a saccular diverticulum.
  36. The correct answer is C (Gallbladder adenocarcinoma presenting as a mass replacing the lumen with direct hepatic invasion): A mass replacing the gallbladder with liver invasion, stones and nodal disease points to gallbladder adenocarcinoma. Xanthogranulomatous cholecystitis can mimic it but does not usually cause nodal metastases. Mirizzi syndrome explains jaundice but not a mass with invasion. Cholangiocarcinoma arises in the liver and does not replace the gallbladder.
  37. The correct answer is C (At least 72 hours after symptom onset, when necrosis has fully declared on enhancement): Necrosis may be underestimated on early CT, so imaging after about 72 hours is best for assessing perfusion defects, when this changes management. Scanning at 24 to 48 hours or within 12 hours often misses evolving necrosis. Two weeks is later than needed for detecting necrosis, though walled-off collections take about four weeks to mature.
  38. The correct answer is D (Walled-off necrosis, a mature encapsulated collection of pancreatic or peripancreatic necrosis after four weeks): After four weeks, a necrotic collection with non-liquid debris and an enhancing wall is walled-off necrosis. An acute necrotic collection lacks a wall and occurs earlier. A pseudocyst is homogeneous fluid following interstitial pancreatitis. An acute peripancreatic fluid collection has no wall and no necrosis.
  39. The correct answer is D (Pseudocyst, defined by homogeneous fluid content after four weeks in the absence of necrosis): A pseudocyst is an encapsulated collection of fluid with minimal or no solid debris, occurring after four weeks following interstitial pancreatitis. Walled-off necrosis contains solid debris. Acute peripancreatic fluid collections occur earlier and lack a wall. Acute necrotic collections lack a wall and contain necrosis.
  40. The correct answer is C (Branch-duct IPMN with worrisome features, warranting endoscopic ultrasound for further characterisation): A cyst of 3 cm or more, or a main duct of 5 to 9 mm, is a worrisome feature in the 2017 Fukuoka guidelines, and endoscopic ultrasound is recommended. High-risk stigmata are jaundice, enhancing nodule of 5 mm or more and main duct of 10 mm or more. Communication with the duct favours IPMN.
  41. The correct answer is A (High-risk stigmata, for which surgical resection is generally recommended in a fit patient): A main duct of 10 mm or more, or obstructive jaundice from a cystic lesion in the head, are high-risk stigmata. Resection is generally advised for fit patients. Worrisome features such as a 5 to 9 mm duct prompt endoscopic ultrasound rather than direct surgery. The 2017 consensus has no indeterminate category for this picture, and delay is inappropriate.
  42. The correct answer is C (Serous cystadenoma, a benign lesion with a central scar and sunburst calcification): A microcystic honeycomb lesion with a central scar and sunburst calcification is classic for serous cystadenoma, which is benign and usually followed or ignored. Mucinous cystic neoplasm has few large locules and peripheral calcification. IPMN communicates with the duct. Solid pseudopapillary neoplasm affects young women.
  43. The correct answer is D (Mucinous cystic neoplasm, with ovarian-type stroma, arising in the body or tail of middle-aged women): A solitary macrocystic lesion with peripheral calcification in the tail of a middle-aged woman, with no duct communication, is typical for mucinous cystic neoplasm. Serous tumours are microcystic. IPMN communicates with the duct. A pseudocyst requires preceding pancreatitis and lacks septa.
  44. The correct answer is A (Solid pseudopapillary neoplasm, a low-grade tumour of young women with excellent outcome after resection): A large encapsulated solid and cystic mass with haemorrhage in a young woman is characteristic of solid pseudopapillary neoplasm. Neuroendocrine tumours are usually hypervascular and solid. Mucinous cystic neoplasm occurs in older women and lacks haemorrhagic solid components. Acinar cell carcinoma is typically seen in older men.
  45. The correct answer is D (Unresectable locally advanced, since arterial contact exceeds 180 degrees): Contact with the superior mesenteric artery exceeding 180 degrees is regarded as unresectable (locally advanced) in NCCN criteria, whereas contact of 180 degrees or less is borderline. Venous patency and lack of metastases do not alter arterial staging.
  46. The correct answer is A (Insulinoma, best problem-solved with endoscopic ultrasound when multiphase CT is equivocal): A small hypervascular lesion with hypoglycaemia suggests insulinoma, and endoscopic ultrasound is the most sensitive localising test for small tumours when multiphase CT is equivocal. Gallium-68 DOTATATE is less reliable because many insulinomas express few somatostatin receptors. Glucagonoma gives a different syndrome. Fluorodeoxyglucose PET is insensitive for well-differentiated neuroendocrine tumours.
  47. The correct answer is B (Metastases from renal cell carcinoma, which characteristically recur late and are hypervascular): Late hypervascular pancreatic masses in a patient with prior clear cell renal carcinoma suggest metastases, which often present years after nephrectomy and may be treated surgically. Ductal adenocarcinoma is hypoenhancing. An accessory spleen is a single small round nodule. Autoimmune pancreatitis is diffuse.
  48. The correct answer is C (Grade IV injury with a vascular injury, for which angioembolisation is appropriate in a stable patient): The 2018 AAST scale upgrades a contained parenchymal pseudoaneurysm or arteriovenous fistula (contrast blush) to grade IV. In a haemodynamically stable patient, angioembolisation is appropriate. Grading by laceration alone misses the vascular injury, and splenectomy is not preferred in a stable patient. Grade III with a vascular injury reflects the superseded scale.
  49. The correct answer is C (Splenic infarct caused by embolic occlusion of a segmental splenic artery branch): A peripheral wedge-shaped non-enhancing region in a patient with atrial fibrillation indicates embolic splenic infarction. Abscesses are round and often complicate infarcts. Lacerations follow trauma and are linear. Splenic vein thrombosis causes congestion and splenomegaly, not a sharply defined wedge defect.
  50. The correct answer is B (Gamna-Gandy bodies, from small haemorrhages with haemosiderin deposition in portal hypertension): Multiple small non-enhancing foci that bloom on gradient echo reflect haemosiderin from microhaemorrhages (siderotic nodules), classically in portal hypertension. Sarcoid nodules enhance mildly and are often hypointense on T2 without blooming. Melanoma metastases are T1 bright. Calcified granulomas can bloom but are few, and portal hypertension is the discriminating setting.
  51. The correct answer is B (Hepatic artery thrombosis, which risks biliary ischaemia and graft loss and requires urgent assessment): Absent hepatic arterial flow soon after transplant indicates hepatic artery thrombosis, the most common vascular complication and a cause of biliary ischaemia, abscess and graft failure. Portal and hepatic vein stenoses show their own Doppler findings. Rejection is diagnosed by biopsy.
  52. The correct answer is A (Ischaemic cholangiopathy, a non-anastomotic stricture pattern from compromised arterial supply to the bile ducts): The bile ducts depend on hepatic arterial supply, so thrombosis leads to non-anastomotic strictures, beading and bilomas, termed ischaemic cholangiopathy. Recurrent PSC takes years and needs the original indication. Ductopenic rejection thins ducts without bilomas. Casts and sludge produce filling defects rather than beaded strictures with bilomas.
  53. The correct answer is C (Post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder, linked to immunosuppression and Epstein-Barr virus infection): Nodal and extranodal masses with raised Epstein-Barr virus load in an immunosuppressed transplant recipient suggest PTLD. Recurrent HCC would usually show liver or distant disease with a different marker pattern. Mycobacterial infection can cause necrotic nodes but does not explain the viral load. Chronic rejection does not form a mass.
  54. The correct answer is D (About 20 minutes after injection, when hepatocyte uptake and biliary excretion are established): Gadoxetate is taken up by hepatocytes through OATP transporters and excreted in bile, and the hepatobiliary phase is acquired around 20 minutes later, longer in impaired liver function. Earlier phases are dynamic extracellular phases. A three hour delay is not used for this agent.
  55. The correct answer is C (Ultrasound every six months, with or without serum alpha-fetoprotein measurement): Surveillance in high-risk patients is ultrasound every six months, with or without alpha-fetoprotein, because it is inexpensive and safe. Multiphase CT and MRI are used for diagnosis of observations found on surveillance and are not routine screening tools. Twelve-month intervals miss tumours that grow in the interval between scans.
  56. The correct answer is D (Stopping the contraceptive pill and considering resection, since bleeding risk rises above 5 cm): Adenomas above 5 cm with haemorrhage carry a higher risk of further bleeding and malignant transformation, so hormone withdrawal plus consideration of resection is advised. Stopping the pill with observation alone is tempting because it is first-line for smaller lesions, but it undertreats a bleeding 6.5 cm adenoma. Embolisation or ablation while oestrogen continues leaves the stimulus for growth unchanged.
  57. The correct answer is C (Central dot sign, indicating Caroli disease with intrahepatic cystic duct dilatation): Saccular dilated intrahepatic ducts that contain enhancing portal vein branches give the central dot sign and are typical of Caroli disease, a congenital abnormality linked to cholangitis and stones. PSC shows beading. The double duct sign belongs to pancreatic head obstruction. The cluster sign is seen in abscess.
  58. The correct answer is B (Intrapancreatic accessory spleen, a benign splenule that follows normal splenic signal and enhancement): A small nodule near the tail that mirrors splenic attenuation and signal in every phase is a splenule, an accessory spleen. Neuroendocrine tumours differ from spleen on delayed phases. Hamartoma is within the spleen. A cystic neoplasm would not enhance like spleen.
  59. The correct answer is D (Benign haemangioma, as persistent high T2 signal and fill-in enhancement indicate a blood-filled lesion): A very bright, persistent T2 signal with discontinuous nodular enhancement and fill-in is typical for haemangioma even in a patient with cancer. Metastases lose signal on longer echo times and often show washout. Cysts do not enhance.
  60. The correct answer is B (Short symptom duration with marked weight loss in an older patient): Primary achalasia usually evolves over years and weight loss is modest, so a rapid course with major weight loss in an older patient points to an infiltrating gastro-oesophageal junction tumour that mimics it. Failed sphincter relaxation and a retained, dilated body are shared by both conditions. Tertiary contractions occur in early or vigorous achalasia and do not discriminate. Cross-sectional imaging or endoscopic ultrasound is then needed.
  61. The correct answer is C (Water-soluble iodinated contrast first, with dilute barium if no leak is seen): A water-soluble agent is used first because leaked barium provokes a severe mediastinal and pleural inflammatory reaction. If no leak is seen, thin barium is then given because water-soluble contrast can miss small tears. Hypertonic agents risk pulmonary oedema if aspirated, and insufflation at endoscopy can enlarge the defect.
  62. The correct answer is D (Endoscopic ultrasound with fine needle aspiration of suspicious regional nodes): Endoscopic ultrasound shows the wall layers, so it separates T1, T2 and T3 disease and allows needle sampling of adjacent nodes. PET is valuable for occult distant metastases but has limited local resolution. Thoracic MRI is not routine because of motion, and CT cannot reliably resolve wall layers.
  63. The correct answer is A (Scirrhous signet ring cell carcinoma infiltrating the whole gastric wall): A rigid, non-distensible stomach with effaced folds and layered wall enhancement is the pattern of scirrhous or signet ring carcinoma. Lymphoma usually remains pliable and often has bulky nodes. Menetrier disease thickens folds rather than effacing them, and a corrosive injury would carry a history of ingestion.
  64. The correct answer is C (Gastrointestinal stromal tumour of submucosal origin in the stomach): Large exophytic submucosal masses with necrosis and absent nodal spread are typical of stromal tumours. Adenocarcinoma generally infiltrates the wall and spreads to nodes. Lymphoma tends to be more homogeneous and nodal. A pseudocyst contains fluid and does not enhance centrally.
  65. The correct answer is C (A fibrous adhesive band following the earlier pelvic operation): A transition point in a patient with a previous pelvic operation and no obvious mass is most often due to an adhesive band. An internal hernia can mimic this but usually shows clustered, tethered loops with mesenteric vessel swirling. Crohn stricture and neoplasm are tempting because both narrow the lumen, but both usually leave a mural abnormality or a history that is not described here.
  66. The correct answer is B (Reduced bowel wall enhancement with mesenteric haziness and free fluid): Loss of wall enhancement, mesenteric haziness and free fluid reflect venous congestion and vascular compromise. A radial loop arrangement and the small bowel faeces sign describe the obstruction itself, and air-fluid levels occur in simple obstruction.
  67. The correct answer is B (Whirl of twisted mesenteric vessels with the caecum displaced to the left upper abdomen): Caecal volvulus twists the ileocolic mesentery, producing a whirl and displacing the mobile caecum to the left upper abdomen with a collapsed distal colon. Sigmoid volvulus and small bowel closed loop obstruction both show a whirl, which makes them tempting, but neither relocates the caecum. Intussusception gives a target appearance, not a gas-filled kidney-shaped viscus.
  68. The correct answer is A (Embolus from the left atrium lodging distal to the origin): Emboli typically lodge a few centimetres past the origin where the vessel narrows, often sparing the proximal jejunum. Atherosclerotic thrombosis is ostial, non-occlusive ischaemia has a patent artery, and dissection shows an intimal flap.
  69. The correct answer is C (Superior mesenteric venous thrombosis with congestive bowel wall oedema): Factor V Leiden predisposes to venous thrombosis, and a target-wall ileal segment with stranding, ascites and a congested mesenteric root indicates venous outflow obstruction. Arterial thrombosis and low flow ischaemia are tempting because they thicken bowel, but they lack the congested mesenteric pattern and the thrombophilic setting. Strangulated closed loop obstruction mimics the wall appearance but needs a transition point.
  70. The correct answer is A (Active inflammation, so medical therapy rather than surgery is likely to help): T2 hyperintensity, restricted diffusion, vascular engorgement and avid enhancement indicate active inflammation that can respond to medical treatment.
