Fibrotic strictures are low on T2 with weaker enhancement. Lymphoma would form a bulky mass, and infective ileitis does not account for a known long segment pattern.
  71. The correct answer is D (Refer for surgical resection or endoscopic dilatation of the stricture): A dilated upstream bowel with a low T2 wall and little inflammatory activity indicates a fixed fibrotic stricture. Drug escalation and steroids treat active inflammation, which is not present, and antibiotics address sepsis or abscess. Mechanical relief is therefore needed.
  72. The correct answer is A (Continuous colonic involvement extending proximally from the rectum without skip areas): Ulcerative colitis begins in the rectum and spreads in a continuous fashion, giving a lead-pipe colon with a widened presacral space. The patulous terminal ileum reflects backwash ileitis. Skip lesions, fistulae, cobblestoning and fat wrapping characterise Crohn disease.
  73. The correct answer is B (Pseudomembranous colitis caused by Clostridioides difficile overgrowth in the colon): Marked pancolitis with an accordion pattern of trapped contrast and ascites after antibiotics is characteristic of C. difficile infection. Ischaemia is typically segmental, CMV needs an immunocompromised host, and radiation colitis depends on prior pelvic therapy.
  74. The correct answer is C (Watershed territory between the superior and inferior mesenteric arterial supply): Segmental ischaemia at the splenic flexure after hypotension, with sparing of rectum and right colon, reflects the watershed between the superior and inferior mesenteric supplies. Occlusion of the inferior mesenteric artery or vein is tempting but would be expected to affect a longer left-sided segment and show a visible clot. A middle colic embolus would involve the transverse colon.
  75. The correct answer is B (Neutropenic enterocolitis involving the caecum and terminal ileum): Caecal and ileal wall thickening in a neutropenic patient with a normal appendix is typical of neutropenic enterocolitis. Appendicitis requires an abnormal appendix, Crohn disease has a chronic history, and right-sided diverticulitis would show diverticula with focal inflammation.
  76. The correct answer is B (A blind-ending tubular structure over 6 mm that does not compress): A non-compressible, blind-ending tube exceeding 6 mm in outer diameter is the key sign. Echogenic fat, free fluid and adenopathy can accompany other conditions such as mesenteric adenitis, and a compressible thin-walled appendix is normal.
  77. The correct answer is A (Non-contrast MRI of the abdomen and pelvis using fast sequences): After a non-diagnostic ultrasound in the second trimester, MRI without contrast offers good accuracy without fetal radiation. Low-dose CT is a reasonable fallback if MRI is unavailable, but it is not preferred. Repeating ultrasound rarely improves visualisation of a gravid, displaced appendix, and gadolinium is avoided in pregnancy because it adds risk without helping.
  78. The correct answer is A (Perforation of the appendix with a contained periappendiceal abscess): A calcified appendicolith with a gas-containing rim-enhancing collection beside the caecum indicates appendiceal perforation with abscess. Caecal diverticulitis, caecal carcinoma and Crohn disease can all create a pericaecal collection, which makes them tempting. None accounts for the appendicolith and dilated appendix, though a carcinoma should be considered in older patients at follow-up.
  79. The correct answer is D (Stage II, with percutaneous drainage in addition to antibiotics): A distant pelvic abscess is stage II, and collections over about 3 to 4 cm usually warrant image-guided drainage. Stage Ib is a small pericolic abscess, stage III is generalised purulent peritonitis, and stage Ia is a phlegmon without an abscess.
  80. The correct answer is A (Abrupt shouldering of a short segment with enlarged regional nodes): Abrupt transition, a short segment, and enlarged regional nodes favour a neoplasm. Diverticulitis gives long segment tapering, stranding greater than wall thickening, and preserved layers. Follow-up colonoscopy is advised when features are equivocal.
  81. The correct answer is C (Threatened or involved, favouring neoadjuvant chemoradiotherapy before resection): A tumour 1 mm from the mesorectal fascia is regarded as threatened or involved, and preoperative chemoradiotherapy aims to downstage and achieve a clear circumferential margin. Operating first with adjuvant treatment risks a positive margin. Short-course radiotherapy with immediate surgery is suited to a clear fascia, and a 1 mm margin is not clear.
  82. The correct answer is A (T2, with disease contained within the rectal muscle coat): An intact outer muscularis propria with no extramural spread places the tumour at T2. T3 requires extension beyond the muscularis, and T4 involves adjacent organs or peritoneum. Distinguishing T2 from T3 is the key MRI task, since stranding can be desmoplastic.
  83. The correct answer is C (T3c, with extramural depth between 5 and 15 mm): MRI subclassifies T3 by depth of invasion: a is under 1 mm, b is 1 to 5 mm, c is 5 to 15 mm, and d is over 15 mm. A depth of 7 mm falls in category c. Greater depth carries a poorer prognosis.
  84. The correct answer is A (Rigler sign, also called the double wall sign): Visibility of both sides of the bowel wall is Rigler sign, indicating free intraperitoneal gas. The football sign is a large oval gas collection, the cupola sign is gas beneath the central diaphragm, and the triangle sign is gas trapped between three loops.
  85. The correct answer is C (Transverse colon diameter exceeding 6 cm with loss of haustra): Toxic megacolon is defined by colonic dilatation, typically transverse colon over 6 cm, with a toxic clinical state and mucosal changes. The other options describe volvulus, small bowel obstruction and caecal thresholds in mechanical obstruction.
  86. The correct answer is B (Transmural bowel infarction needing urgent surgical assessment and resuscitation): Pneumatosis with portal venous gas plus poor enhancement and lactic acidosis indicates bowel necrosis. Benign cystic pneumatosis occurs in chronic disease with a well patient, and iatrogenic gas needs a procedural history.
  87. The correct answer is B (Pseudomyxoma peritonei from a ruptured mucinous appendiceal neoplasm): Septated mucinous ascites causing scalloping of visceral surfaces and an appendiceal source is typical of pseudomyxoma. Tuberculosis and ovarian carcinoma show peritoneal thickening or caking with more attenuating fluid, and cirrhotic ascites is simple and does not scallop.
  88. The correct answer is A (Primary epiploic appendagitis from torsion of a sigmoid fat appendage): An oval fatty lesion with a hyperattenuating ring and central vessel beside a normal colon is the hallmark of appendagitis, a self-limiting condition. Diverticulitis thickens the wall, omental infarction is larger and typically right-sided, and mesenteritis involves the mesenteric root.
  89. The correct answer is C (Mesenteric panniculitis, a fatty inflammatory process at the mesenteric root): The fat ring sign, with preserved fat around vessels and a pseudocapsule, characterises mesenteric panniculitis. Lymphoma commonly encases vessels, carcinoid shows calcified spiculated masses, and liposarcoma has a nodular solid component.
  90. The correct answer is C (Omental caking caused by confluent peritoneal metastatic deposits): Ascites, nodular peritoneal thickening and soft tissue replacing omental fat in a woman with a pelvic mass indicate omental caking from peritoneal metastases, commonly ovarian. Tuberculous peritonitis and mesothelioma give a similar peritoneal pattern, which makes them tempting, but neither fits a pelvic primary. Lymphoma usually adds bulky nodes rather than a pelvic mass.
  91. The correct answer is C (Spigelian hernia at the semilunar line of the abdominal wall): A defect through transversus abdominis and internal oblique lateral to the rectus, with external oblique intact, defines a Spigelian hernia at the semilunar line. Inguinal hernias are tempting because they are lower abdominal, but they pass through the inguinal canal rather than the lateral rectus border. Interstitial inguinal hernias arise from the inguinal canal, not at the semilunar line. Incisional hernias arise at a scar.
  92. The correct answer is D (Indirect hernia, a persistent patent processus vaginalis pathway): Entry through the deep ring along the cord, lateral to the inferior epigastric vessels, defines an indirect inguinal hernia. A direct hernia bulges medially through Hesselbach triangle, while femoral and obturator hernias pass through different openings.
  93. The correct answer is B (Femoral hernia with incarcerated ileum in the femoral canal): A sac below the inguinal ligament, lateral to the pubic tubercle and medial to the femoral vein, lies in the femoral canal. Femoral hernias are more frequent in older women and carry a high strangulation risk.
  94. The correct answer is D (Obturator hernia with a strangulated loop of ileum): Bowel deep to the pectineus within the obturator canal, with medial thigh pain from obturator nerve compression, indicates an obturator hernia. Femoral hernia is the common mimic in this region, but the sac lies anterior to the pectineus. Sciatic and perineal hernias are pelvic floor hernias at other sites.
  95. The correct answer is A (Reduced enhancement of the herniated bowel wall with surrounding fat stranding): Reduced wall enhancement with stranding indicates compromised blood supply. Sac fluid, upstream dilatation and a narrow neck occur in simple incarceration and do not distinguish ischaemic bowel.
  96. The correct answer is D (Draped aorta sign of a contained retroperitoneal rupture): The posterior wall losing its contour and draping over the vertebra with an adjacent haematoma indicates contained rupture. A hyperattenuating crescent suggests impending rupture, calcium displacement describes chronic enlargement, and thrombus does not lower the risk in this setting.
  97. The correct answer is D (Extrinsic compression by the median arcuate ligament crossing the aortic hiatus): A hooked narrowing from the superior surface that varies with respiration, in a young thin patient, is characteristic of ligament compression. Atherosclerosis and fibromuscular dysplasia are not variable with respiration and show different patterns.
  98. The correct answer is B (Reduced aortomesenteric angle after loss of retroperitoneal fat): Weight loss depletes the fat pad between the superior mesenteric artery and aorta, narrowing the angle and compressing the third duodenum, with relief on positional change. Annular pancreas and webs are fixed lesions, and Ladd bands typically present in infancy.
  99. The correct answer is B (Left renal vein entrapment between aorta and superior mesenteric artery): Compression of the left renal vein between aorta and superior mesenteric artery produces upstream distension and collateral gonadal veins, with haematuria and flank pain. Thrombosis shows luminal clot, a retroaortic vein is a variant behind the aorta, and tumour thrombus requires a renal mass.
  100. The correct answer is C (Active arterial extravasation, requiring urgent embolisation or surgical control): A contrast focus that grows on delayed images and mixes with blood is active bleeding. A pseudoaneurysm retains the shape and washes out, early phase heterogeneity resolves, and a fistula opacifies draining veins early.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Extraluminal gas bubbles adjacent to the thickened bowel wall): Extraluminal gas is a specific sign of hollow viscus rupture. Free fluid without solid organ injury, mesenteric haematoma and wall thickening raise suspicion but are also seen in contusion or vascular injury.
  2. The correct answer is C (MR cholangiopancreatography, ideally with secretin stimulation of the pancreas): MR cholangiopancreatography is the noninvasive test of choice for the main pancreatic duct, and secretin stimulation improves detection of leaks. ERCP delineates the duct but is invasive and reserved for therapy. Endoscopic ultrasound is operator dependent, and repeat CT often cannot show a duct disruption.
  3. The correct answer is B (Killian dehiscence, a weak area between thyropharyngeus and cricopharyngeus fibres): Zenker diverticulum is a pulsion pouch through Killian dehiscence, a weak area posteriorly between the oblique fibres of thyropharyngeus and the horizontal fibres of cricopharyngeus. Traction diverticula arise mid oesophagus and epiphrenic ones above the diaphragm.
  4. The correct answer is D (Eosinophilic oesophagitis producing a ringed or trachealised oesophageal appearance): Atopy, food impaction and multiple fixed rings with a narrowed segment point to eosinophilic oesophagitis. Peptic stricture is the main mimic, but it is short and distal. Candida oesophagitis gives plaques and a shaggy contour, and systemic sclerosis gives a dilated hypotonic oesophagus.
  5. The correct answer is B (Type III combined hernia with mesenteroaxial rotation of the stomach): A large combined hernia with stomach rotation and obstruction, presenting as the Borchardt triad of pain, retching without vomiting and failed tube passage, is a type III paraoesophageal hernia. Sliding hernias rarely twist, and type II and IV have different contents.
  6. The correct answer is B (Ileal neuroendocrine tumour with mesenteric and hepatic metastases): A calcified mesenteric mass with desmoplastic spokes tethering the ileum, plus hypervascular liver lesions, identifies an ileal neuroendocrine tumour. Adenocarcinoma rarely calcifies and GIST rarely tethers with desmoplasia. Sclerosing mesenteritis may fibrose the root but does not cause hepatic metastases.
  7. The correct answer is D (Class III, because thick or irregular enhancing walls or septa are present): Thick (4 mm or more) or irregular enhancing walls or septa without a measurable nodule define Class III in Bosniak 2019. Class IIF needs smooth minimal thickening (3 mm or less), and Class IV needs an enhancing nodule of 4 mm or more with obtuse margins or any size with acute margins. Class II lesions have hairline thin septa without measurable enhancement.
  8. The correct answer is C (Class IV, because an enhancing nodule of 4 mm or more with obtuse margins is present): In Bosniak 2019 a nodule is a convex enhancing protrusion; at 4 mm or more with obtuse margins it qualifies as Class IV, and with acute margins any size qualifies. Smooth thin septa elsewhere do not downgrade the lesion. Class IV masses are managed as probable malignancy, usually with surgery or ablation.
  9. The correct answer is B (Class II, a homogeneous hyperattenuating lesion under 3 cm with no follow-up needed): Homogeneous, well-marginated, non-enhancing lesions of 70 HU or more and under 3 cm are Class II (haemorrhagic or proteinaceous cysts). Absence of enhancement excludes solid tumour. Class I requires water attenuation, and surveillance is reserved for IIF.
  10. The correct answer is C (Clear cell carcinoma, the commonest subtype with marked early enhancement): Clear cell RCC is hypervascular, enhancing avidly in the corticomedullary phase with washout and frequent necrosis or haemorrhage. Papillary tumours enhance less than renal cortex, chromophobe tumours enhance homogeneously, and collecting duct tumours are infiltrative and poorly enhancing.
  11. The correct answer is D (Low T2 signal with weak, progressive enhancement below that of renal cortex): Papillary RCC is typically T2 hypointense and hypovascular, with enhancement lower than the renal cortex. Clear cell RCC is T2 bright, avidly enhancing, and may lose signal on chemical shift imaging. A pseudocapsule appears in many solid renal tumours and does not discriminate.
  12. The correct answer is A (Imaging cannot reliably exclude chromophobe carcinoma, so sampling or resection is usually advised): A central stellate scar and homogeneous enhancement suggest oncocytoma, but they also occur in chromophobe carcinoma and clear cell RCC. Because imaging is not reliable enough to exclude malignancy, biopsy or excision is normally recommended.
  13. The correct answer is C (Angiomyolipoma, a benign mesenchymal tumour containing fat, vessels and smooth muscle): Macroscopic fat (below minus 20 HU) in a renal mass without calcification is diagnostic of angiomyolipoma. RCC rarely contains fat, and when it does, calcification is usually present. Retroperitoneal liposarcoma displaces the kidney rather than arising within the parenchyma.
  14. The correct answer is A (Prophylactic embolisation or mTOR inhibitor therapy to reduce the risk of spontaneous bleeding): Haemorrhage (Wunderlich syndrome) risk rises with tumour size above about 4 cm and with intratumoural aneurysms over 5 mm, so prophylactic treatment is reasonable. Embolisation or mTOR inhibitors preserve renal function, which matters in tuberous sclerosis. Radical nephrectomy is not indicated for a benign tumour.
  15. The correct answer is A (Uric acid, radiolucent on radiography and about 200 to 450 HU on CT): Uric acid stones are pure radiolucent calculi on plain film but are visible on CT at roughly 200 to 450 HU. Calcium oxalate and phosphate stones are denser, and struvite is associated with urease-producing infection. Gout supports uric acid composition.
  16. The correct answer is B (Low-dose non-contrast CT of the abdomen and pelvis): Non-contrast low-dose CT has sensitivity and specificity above 95 percent for ureteric stones and shows size, location and alternative diagnoses. Contrast obscures calcification within the collecting system. IV urography is largely replaced, and MRI poorly depicts small stones.
  17. The correct answer is D (Urgent decompression with a ureteric stent or percutaneous nephrostomy): An obstructing stone with infection and falling blood pressure needs urgent decompression by stent or nephrostomy, with definitive stone treatment after the sepsis settles. Early lithotripsy of either kind risks bacteraemia in an infected system. Antibiotics alone are insufficient because pus under pressure will not respond without drainage.
  18. The correct answer is A (Physiological dilatation from uterine compression of the right ureter at the pelvic brim): Right-sided mild hydronephrosis is common from mid-pregnancy because the gravid uterus compresses the ureter as it crosses the pelvic brim and the dextrorotated uterus favours the right. Dilatation that tapers at the brim, with no stone or infection, is physiological and managed expectantly.
  19. The correct answer is C (Raised resistance in the dilated kidney, supporting acute obstruction): A resistive index above about 0.70, or an interrenal difference above about 0.06 to 0.10, supports acute obstruction. Renal artery stenosis produces a prolonged systolic acceleration time and a damped, low-resistance waveform downstream. Chronic atrophy would show a thin cortex rather than acute dilatation.
  20. The correct answer is A (Emphysematous pyelonephritis, with urgent drainage or nephrectomy and broad-spectrum antibiotics): Gas dissecting through the parenchyma in a diabetic indicates emphysematous pyelonephritis, a necrotising infection with high mortality. Extensive disease without fluid collections is usually treated with nephrectomy or drainage plus intravenous antibiotics. Emphysematous pyelitis, with gas limited to the collecting system, has a better prognosis.
  21. The correct answer is D (Xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis, chronic obstructive infection with lipid-laden macrophages): Xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis typically shows the bear paw sign of dilated calyces, a staghorn calculus and a non-functioning enlarged kidney. It occurs in chronic infection with obstruction, usually in middle-aged women. Nephrectomy is usually required.
  22. The correct answer is B (No initial imaging, with ultrasound reserved for lack of improvement within 72 hours): Acute uncomplicated pyelonephritis is a clinical diagnosis, and imaging is reserved for sepsis, suspected obstruction, diabetes, immunosuppression, prior stones, or lack of response by 48 to 72 hours. Ultrasound is a first-line choice when imaging is required, with CT for complicated cases.
  23. The correct answer is D (Renal infarction, with the cortical rim sign from preserved capsular collateral supply): Embolic arterial occlusion causes a sharply demarcated wedge of non-enhancing parenchyma. A thin enhancing subcapsular rim occurs because capsular arteries preserve cortex. Atrial fibrillation provides the embolic source.
  24. The correct answer is C (Transplant renal artery stenosis, causing focal high velocity and damped downstream waveforms): Anastomotic velocities above about 2.5 m/s with downstream tardus parvus waveforms and a low resistive index indicate arterial stenosis. Renal vein thrombosis causes reversed diastolic flow with a high resistive index. Acute tubular necrosis and rejection are nonspecific and typically show raised resistive indices.
  25. The correct answer is B (Renal vein thrombosis, an early surgical emergency threatening graft survival): Reversed end-diastolic flow in the allograft with absent venous flow is characteristic of renal vein thrombosis, typically presenting in the first week. Urgent surgical review is needed. Rejection raises the resistive index but does not abolish venous flow.
  26. The correct answer is C (Lymphocele, which appears weeks after surgery and can compress the ureter): Lymphoceles arise from disrupted lymphatics four to eight weeks after transplantation and are typically septated and simple on ultrasound. They may cause hydronephrosis by compressing the ureter. Urinomas appear earlier and haematomas are echogenic initially.
  27. The correct answer is A (Urothelial carcinoma, with an enhancing soft tissue filling defect and obstruction): An enhancing soft tissue filling defect of 35 HU is tumour until proven otherwise. Calculi are denser and do not enhance, and clot does not enhance. Fibroepithelial polyps are smooth and usually found in younger patients.
  28. The correct answer is D (VI-RADS 5, indicating muscle invasion with spread into perivesical fat): VI-RADS 5 denotes invasive tumour extending beyond the bladder wall into extravesical fat, while VI-RADS 4 is invasion of the muscularis propria alone. VI-RADS 1 and 2 predict muscle-invasive disease is unlikely, and 3 is equivocal. Disrupted T2 and DWI findings together give the highest scores.
  29. The correct answer is D (Urachal adenocarcinoma arising from the remnant, outside the urothelial lining): A mass at the dome extending toward the umbilicus with calcification and mucinous features indicates urachal adenocarcinoma. The normal overlying urothelium suggests an extramucosal origin. It is managed with partial cystectomy including the urachal remnant.
  30. The correct answer is C (No further imaging, as 10 HU or less confirms a lipid-rich adenoma): A homogeneous adrenal nodule of 10 HU or less on unenhanced CT is a lipid-rich adenoma and needs no further imaging. Washout or chemical shift imaging is needed only for nodules above 10 HU. Biochemical screening is considered according to clinical context.
  31. The correct answer is D (Absolute washout of about 73 percent, favouring a lipid-poor adenoma): Absolute washout is (95 minus 46) divided by (95 minus 28), about 73 percent, and above the 60 percent threshold. Relative washout is (95 minus 46) divided by 95, about 52 percent, above 40 percent. Both indicate adenoma.
  32. The correct answer is D (Lipid-poor adenoma, with intracellular lipid causing signal loss on opposed-phase images): Loss of signal on opposed-phase imaging reflects intracytoplasmic lipid and is typical of adenoma, including lesions of 10 to 30 HU. Metastases and pheochromocytomas lack intracellular fat. Carcinomas are heterogeneous and usually larger.
  33. The correct answer is D (Raised plasma metanephrines in the setting of a T2 bright, avidly enhancing adrenal mass): Paroxysmal headache, sweating and palpitations with a T2 bright, avidly enhancing adrenal mass are confirmed by raised plasma metanephrines. Aldosterone excess and cortisol excess are tempting because adrenal tumours cause hypertension in both, but neither produces paroxysmal catecholamine symptoms or this imaging pattern.
  34. The correct answer is B (Myelolipoma, a benign tumour of fat and marrow elements, not requiring treatment): Macroscopic fat in an adrenal mass indicates myelolipoma. It is benign and observed unless it bleeds or becomes symptomatically large. Carcinoma and metastasis rarely contain fat.
  35. The correct answer is B (Adrenocortical carcinoma, a large heterogeneous tumour that may invade the IVC): Adrenocortical carcinoma is typically over 4 to 6 cm, heterogeneous with necrosis and calcification, and may invade the IVC. Functioning tumours can cause Cushing syndrome or virilisation. Adenomas are small and homogeneous.
  36. The correct answer is B (FDG PET-CT or image-guided biopsy, after excluding pheochromocytoma biochemically): Failure to meet washout criteria, with no lipid on MRI in a patient with cancer, makes metastasis likely. Pheochromocytoma must be excluded before biopsy, and PET-CT or biopsy then confirms the nature. Short-interval characterisation is better than long delay.
  37. The correct answer is C (Idiopathic retroperitoneal fibrosis, a periaortic process often linked to IgG4-related disease): Retroperitoneal fibrosis forms a plaque from the renal hila to the iliac vessels, encasing the aorta and drawing the ureters medially. Lymphoma and nodal metastases typically displace the aorta and ureters outward. The aorta is not dilated, in contrast to inflammatory aneurysm.
  38. The correct answer is C (Retroperitoneal lymphoma, which commonly elevates and surrounds the aorta without constricting it): Anterior displacement of the aorta from the spine and lateral ureteric deviation are features of lymphadenopathy, such as lymphoma, rather than fibrosis. Fibrosis moulds around the vessels and pulls the ureters medially. Haematoma is hyperdense and acute.
  39. The correct answer is B (PI-RADS 4, as DWI is the dominant sequence in the peripheral zone): In the peripheral zone DWI determines the score, and a lesion under 1.5 cm that is markedly restricted gets 4. DCE is only used to upgrade a DWI score of 3 to 4 when positive and does not downgrade. A score of 5 requires 1.5 cm or more, or definite extraprostatic extension.
  40. The correct answer is C (PI-RADS 4, since a positive DCE upgrades a peripheral zone DWI score of 3): A DWI score of 3 in the peripheral zone becomes PI-RADS 4 if DCE is positive. DCE has this limited role only in the peripheral zone. A score of 5 needs a DWI score of 5.
  41. The correct answer is B (PI-RADS 4, as a T2 score of 3 is upgraded by a DWI score of 5): In the transition zone T2 is the dominant sequence, but a T2 score of 3 is upgraded to 4 when the DWI score is 5. Hence the final PI-RADS is 4. In the peripheral zone DWI dominates instead.
  42. The correct answer is A (Stage T3a, with extraprostatic extension but no seminal vesicle invasion): Broad capsular contact of more than 1.5 cm, capsular bulge and neurovascular bundle asymmetry are features of extraprostatic extension, T3a. Seminal vesicle invasion would be T3b, and T4 requires fixation or invasion of adjacent structures such as rectum or bladder neck.
  43. The correct answer is D (Seminoma, a germ cell tumour that is classically homogeneous and hypoechoic): A solid, hypoechoic, vascular intratesticular mass in a young man is a germ cell tumour until proven otherwise, and a homogeneous appearance with normal AFP favours seminoma. Lymphoma affects older men. Epidermoid cysts are avascular.
  44. The correct answer is C (Epidermoid cyst, a benign lesion that may be managed with testis-sparing surgery): The onion-ring pattern of layered keratin, with an avascular lesion and normal markers, is characteristic of an epidermoid cyst. Testis-sparing enucleation is possible. Germ cell tumours are vascular and often heterogeneous.
  45. The correct answer is A (Immediate surgical exploration, without waiting for further imaging or tests): Absent flow with a twisted cord is torsion, and salvage falls rapidly after six hours, so immediate exploration is required. Manual detorsion may be attempted in some units but is never an end point and needs surgery. Antibiotics suit epididymo-orchitis, which shows increased flow, and scintigraphy only delays operation.
  46. The correct answer is A (Acute epididymo-orchitis, with increased vascularity and reactive wall thickening): Infection produces an enlarged hyperaemic epididymis, often with a reactive hydrocele and skin thickening. Torsion produces reduced or absent flow, though early or partial torsion can be subtle. A hernia would contain bowel.
  47. The correct answer is B (Regressed germ cell tumour in the testis with a retroperitoneal nodal metastasis): A small shadowing scar in a young man with retroperitoneal nodal disease suggests a burned-out primary germ cell tumour. The metastasis can persist after the testicular tumour regresses. The nodal pattern, near the renal veins on the right, matches testicular drainage.
  48. The correct answer is A (Tumour thrombus from renal cell carcinoma, enhancing and expanding the vessel): Enhancement and expansion of the vein indicate tumour thrombus extending from renal cell carcinoma. Bland thrombus is the usual cause of an IVC filling defect, but it does not enhance or expand the vein. Mixing artefact resolves on delayed phases, and primary leiomyosarcoma is rare.
  49. The correct answer is D (Surveillance until the largest solid lesion reaches 3 cm, then nephron-sparing intervention): In VHL, renal tumours are surveilled until the largest reaches about 3 cm, a threshold at which metastatic risk rises, and then treated with nephron-sparing surgery or ablation to preserve renal function. Radical nephrectomy is tempting for a malignant-looking lesion but sacrifices renal function in a patient who will develop many more tumours, biopsy of every lesion adds bleeding risk without changing management, and chest radiography does not assess the kidneys.
  50. The correct answer is C (Intraperitoneal bladder rupture, which usually needs operative repair): Contrast in the peritoneal cavity outlining bowel indicates intraperitoneal rupture, from dome tear in a distended bladder, and it requires operative repair. Extraperitoneal ruptures show flame-shaped perivesical extravasation and are usually catheter managed.
  51. The correct answer is D (Retrograde urethrography, before any attempt at urinary catheterisation): Blood at the meatus after pelvic trauma suggests a urethral injury, and catheter insertion can convert a partial tear to a complete one. Retrograde urethrography identifies the site and extent.
  52. The correct answer is C (Renal lymphoma, with homogeneous hypoenhancing masses and a preserved contour): Lymphoma deposits are homogeneous and poorly enhancing, often multiple and bilateral, and the kidney keeps its shape. RCC enhances avidly and invades veins. Angiomyolipoma has fat.
  53. The correct answer is D (Small avulsed bone fragment in the gap between the first cuneiform and second metatarsal base): A fleck between the medial cuneiform and second metatarsal base represents avulsion of the Lisfranc ligament and is specific for tarsometatarsal instability. A navicular avulsion or a cuboid compression fracture occur with similar mechanisms but do not involve the tarsometatarsal joint. A dorsal talar avulsion indicates a Chopart injury.
  54. The correct answer is B (Type B, a fracture at the level of the syndesmosis that may be stable): Weber B fractures begin at the level of the tibial plafond or syndesmosis, which matches the described fibular fracture. Weber A lies below the syndesmosis, usually transverse, while Weber C lies above it with syndesmotic injury. Normal medial and tibiofibular clear spaces favour a stable pattern. The Weber scheme has no type D, and a plafond fracture is a separate pilon injury.
  55. The correct answer is D (Garden III, complete fracture with partial displacement and rotated head trabeculae): Garden III is a complete fracture with partial displacement, producing misalignment of the femoral head and acetabular trabecular lines. Stage I is incomplete or impacted and stage II is complete but undisplaced. Stage IV shows full displacement, so the head sits freely and trabeculae return to a normal alignment in the acetabulum. Recognising the trabecular pattern is the key discriminator between III and IV.
  56. The correct answer is A (Type III, a fracture through the physis exiting through the epiphysis into the joint): A fracture that passes through the physis then turns through the epiphysis to the joint surface is Salter-Harris III, and the absent metaphyseal fragment excludes types II and IV. Type II is the commonest and carries a metaphyseal fragment. Type IV involves the metaphysis as well. Type V is a crush injury and is usually recognised only retrospectively because the radiograph appears normal at presentation.
  57. The correct answer is B (Early MRI of the wrist, which shows marrow oedema or a fracture line): MRI is highly sensitive and specific for occult scaphoid fracture and can be done within days, allowing an early decision about immobilisation and return to sport. Repeat radiographs at ten to fourteen days are less sensitive and delay care. Bone scintigraphy is unreliable in the first forty eight to seventy two hours. Ultrasound effusion is not specific enough to confirm a fracture.
  58. The correct answer is B (Scapholunate ligament disruption with rotary subluxation of the scaphoid): A scapholunate interval greater than 3 mm with the cortical ring sign of the flexed scaphoid indicates scapholunate ligament rupture and rotary subluxation. Lunotriquetral injury widens the ulnar interval and produces a different instability pattern. A scaphoid waist fracture would show a lucent line. Kienbock disease is a lunate problem and leaves the scapholunate gap normal.
  59. The correct answer is B (Perilunate dislocation, with the carpus displaced dorsally around a normally positioned lunate): In perilunate dislocation the lunate stays in the radial fossa while the capitate and remainder of the carpus dislocate, usually dorsally. In lunate dislocation the capitate stays aligned with the radius and the lunate is displaced volarly, producing a spilled teacup sign. Midcarpal instability and DISI are chronic functional patterns and do not give this acute lateral appearance.
  60. The correct answer is C (Giant cell tumour of bone in a skeletally mature adult): Giant cell tumour typically occurs in skeletally mature adults, is eccentric and lytic, abuts the subchondral surface and lacks matrix or a sclerotic margin. Chondroblastoma occurs before physeal closure and often contains calcification. Osteosarcoma produces osteoid matrix and aggressive periosteal reaction. A non-ossifying fibroma occurs in children and is metaphyseal with a sclerotic border.
  61. The correct answer is B (Osteoid osteoma with a small nidus and reactive sclerosis): A lucent nidus smaller than 1.5 cm with variable central mineralisation and reactive cortical thickening, plus night pain relieved by NSAIDs, is classic for osteoid osteoma. Brodie abscess lacks a calcified nidus relieved by NSAIDs. A stress fracture shows a linear lucent line rather than a nidus. Osteoblastoma is histologically similar but exceeds 1.5 cm and often involves the spine.
  62. The correct answer is A (Conventional osteosarcoma, a matrix-producing primary bone malignancy of adolescence): Conventional osteosarcoma is the commonest primary bone malignancy of adolescence and produces fluffy, cloud-like osteoid in a metaphyseal location, usually around the knee. Ewing sarcoma lacks neoplastic bone matrix and favours diaphyses. Chondrosarcoma shows rings and arcs, and is rare in adolescents. An aneurysmal bone cyst does not produce matrix.
  63. The correct answer is C (Ewing sarcoma, a small round blue cell tumour): A diaphyseal permeative lesion with onion skin periosteal reaction and a disproportionate soft tissue mass in a child is typical of Ewing sarcoma, and fever may accompany it. Osteomyelitis is the main mimic but usually shows a smaller soft tissue component. Langerhans cell histiocytosis is the other mimic but is usually well-defined. Osteosarcoma is metaphyseal and produces matrix.
  64. The correct answer is D (Endosteal scalloping involving more than two thirds of the cortical thickness): Deep endosteal scalloping beyond two thirds of the cortex, along with pain, cortical destruction and soft tissue extension, favours chondrosarcoma. Ring and arc matrix and lobulated high T2 signal occur in both entities. Proximal long bone sites are common for both conditions, so location alone does not discriminate. Pain at the lesion site in the absence of a fracture is another useful clinical clue.
  65. The correct answer is D (No further imaging or intervention, with reassurance as it is a benign developmental lesion): An eccentric metaphyseal lesion with a scalloped sclerotic margin in a child is a non-ossifying fibroma, which regresses without treatment. Biopsy and MRI are tempting but unnecessary when the radiograph is classic. CT is used to search for a nidus in osteoid osteoma, which does not apply here.
  66. The correct answer is C (Unicameral bone cyst with a fallen fragment sign): A central metaphyseal lucent lesion in a child with a pathological fracture is typical of a simple bone cyst, and the fallen fragment sign (a cortical fragment moving to the dependent portion) is virtually pathognomonic. Aneurysmal bone cysts are eccentric with fluid levels. Fibrous dysplasia has matrix. Eosinophilic granuloma may contain a button sequestrum but this sits centrally and does not fall.
  67. The correct answer is C (Aneurysmal bone cyst with blood-filled cavities and a thin periosteal shell): An expansile, eccentric multiloculated lesion with a thin rim and fluid-fluid levels in an adolescent is characteristic of an aneurysmal bone cyst. Fluid levels in the majority of the lesion argue against giant cell tumour, which is predominantly solid, though one can be secondary. Osteochondroma is identified by medullary and cortical continuity with the parent bone. A cortical defect is small and sclerotic.
  68. The correct answer is D (Chondroblastoma, a chondroid epiphyseal tumour of skeletally immature patients): Epiphyseal lucent lesions with internal chondroid calcification in adolescents are chondroblastomas. The characteristic low T2 signal reflects chondroid matrix, and extensive perilesional oedema is typical and can mislead toward infection. Giant cell tumour appears in skeletally mature patients. Clear cell chondrosarcoma is rare and occurs in adults. A Brodie abscess lacks internal calcification and would show a penumbra sign.
  69. The correct answer is B (Confluent low T1 marrow signal with high STIR signal and a rim-enhancing subperiosteal collection): Confluent T1 hypointense marrow replacement, STIR hyperintensity and a rim-enhancing subperiosteal collection indicate acute osteomyelitis with an abscess needing drainage. Preserved fatty marrow on T1 suggests oedema rather than infection. Isolated physeal signal is not diagnostic. A lamellated reaction with a solid mass suggests Ewing sarcoma, the key mimic in this age group.
  70. The correct answer is C (The penumbra sign, indicating a subacute intraosseous abscess): The penumbra sign on T1 shows a rim of granulation tissue of intermediate-to-high signal lining an abscess cavity, seen in Brodie abscess and subacute osteomyelitis. The double line sign of avascular necrosis is seen on T2 images. The fallen fragment sign applies to simple bone cysts. The ghost sign is a radiographic phenomenon in osteomyelitis and is not a T1 marrow pattern.
  71. The correct answer is C (Replacement of normal fatty marrow signal on T1 adjacent to the ulcer): Osteomyelitis is contiguous with an ulcer or sinus tract, usually at the forefoot, and replaces the fatty marrow, giving low T1 signal. Charcot arthropathy classically involves the midfoot joints with subluxation, fragmentation and marrow change on both sides of the joint that preserves fat on T1. Debris, effusion and cysts around the tarsometatarsal joints suggest neuropathic change.
  72. The correct answer is D (Nutritional rickets caused by vitamin D deficiency with impaired mineralisation): Physeal widening with cupping and fraying in a vitamin D deficient infant is rickets. Scurvy shows a sclerotic metaphyseal line and a ring epiphysis sign. Hypophosphatasia may mimic rickets but is rare and includes low alkaline phosphatase. Metaphyseal chondrodysplasia mimics rickets radiographically, yet the vitamin D history and bowing in a breastfed infant favour nutritional rickets.
  73. The correct answer is D (Brown tumour, an osteoclastic lytic lesion of advanced hyperparathyroidism): Subperiosteal resorption in the phalanges establishes hyperparathyroidism, and a lytic expansile pelvic lesion in that setting is a brown tumour. Giant cell tumour, aneurysmal bone cyst and renal metastasis all look similar on a radiograph, which makes them tempting. The metabolic context points to a brown tumour.
  74. The correct answer is C (Rugger jersey spine, from secondary hyperparathyroidism with osteosclerosis): Rugger jersey spine, with endplate sclerosis and central lucency, is a manifestation of renal osteodystrophy with secondary hyperparathyroidism. Bamboo spine reflects fused syndesmophytes. Paget disease produces a picture frame vertebra with thick borders. Osteopetrosis gives a similar sandwich appearance, but occurs in a young patient with diffuse sclerosis rather than a dialysis patient.
  75. The correct answer is A (Osteoporosis, because the lowest site T-score is minus 2.5 or below): The diagnosis rests on the lowest T-score among the lumbar spine, total hip and femoral neck, and a value of minus 2.5 or below defines osteoporosis in a postmenopausal woman. The hip result alone would suggest osteopenia, which tempts readers who use one site. Spine readings can be falsely raised by degenerative change, but none is described here, so the low spine value stands. The scan cannot be called normal.
  76. The correct answer is B (Report the Z-score, since a T-score diagnosis of osteoporosis does not apply to premenopausal women): In premenopausal women, men under 50 and children, the Z-score is reported, with a value of minus 2.0 or lower indicating density below the expected range for age. WHO T-score categories apply to postmenopausal women and men over 50. Steroid exposure raises fracture risk but does not by itself define osteoporosis. Ongoing steroid therapy warrants earlier, not later, reassessment.
  77. The correct answer is C (Superscan from diffuse osteoblastic metastases of prostate carcinoma): Intense symmetrical axial uptake with faint renal and soft tissue activity is a superscan, and a raised PSA in an older man makes prostate bone metastases the best explanation. Metabolic bone disease can produce a similar scan, but nothing in the history suggests it. Myeloma more often gives reduced or absent uptake. Paget disease is typically patchy and expansile rather than diffusely symmetrical.
  78. The correct answer is D (Paget disease of bone, producing a picture frame vertebra): Vertebral enlargement with thick cortical borders and coarse trabeculae is the picture frame appearance of Paget disease, supported by isolated high alkaline phosphatase. Metastatic and lymphomatous ivory vertebrae maintain normal size. Osteopetrosis shows sandwich endplates in a young patient and diffuse sclerosis. Enlargement of the bone is the key discriminating feature.
  79. The correct answer is A (Secondary osteosarcoma, requiring MRI and image-guided biopsy before planning treatment): New pain and cortical destruction with a soft tissue mass in long-standing Paget disease suggests sarcomatous transformation, most often osteosarcoma, so MRI and biopsy are needed. An insufficiency fracture would not create a soft tissue mass. The osteolytic phase of Paget does not breach the cortex. Metastasis is possible but needs tissue diagnosis, and a bone scan alone cannot provide it.
  80. The correct answer is A (Rheumatoid arthritis, an erosive inflammatory polyarthropathy of synovial joints): Symmetrical marginal erosions with periarticular osteopenia and uniform joint narrowing at the MCP and PIP joints, sparing the DIP joints, are classic for rheumatoid arthritis. Psoriatic arthritis favours DIP joints and produces new bone. Osteoarthritis shows osteophytes and sclerosis rather than osteopenia. Gout preserves joint space and bone density until late disease.
  81. The correct answer is B (Psoriatic arthritis, a seronegative spondyloarthropathy affecting distal joints): Distal erosions with adjacent bone proliferation, pencil-in-cup deformity, preserved bone density and periostitis characterise psoriatic arthritis, especially with nail changes. Rheumatoid disease causes periarticular osteopenia without new bone. Erosive osteoarthritis shows gull-wing deformity but not fluffy periostitis. Reactive arthritis mainly affects the feet and sacroiliac joints rather than the hand.
  82. The correct answer is C (Chronic tophaceous gout from long-standing urate crystal deposition): Juxta-articular punched-out erosions with overhanging edges, preserved joint space and normal mineralisation indicate chronic tophaceous gout. Septic arthritis causes early joint space loss and osteopenia. Rheumatoid arthritis produces marginal erosions with osteopenia. Pseudogout is identified by chondrocalcinosis and degenerative changes in typical sites, not overhanging-edge erosions.
  83. The correct answer is B (Calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate deposition disease with articular chondrocalcinosis): Chondrocalcinosis with scapholunate advanced collapse and isolated patellofemoral disease is the typical distribution of CPPD arthropathy. Haemochromatosis can mimic it but affects the second and third metacarpophalangeal joints with hook osteophytes. Primary osteoarthritis favours the medial compartment and the first carpometacarpal joint. Rheumatoid arthritis produces marginal erosions.
  84. The correct answer is C (Erosive osteoarthritis, an inflammatory subset of interphalangeal joint degeneration): Central erosions at the interphalangeal joints with osteophytes produce the gull-wing deformity of erosive osteoarthritis, and the MCP joints and wrist are spared with normal density. Rheumatoid arthritis erodes the margins and causes osteopenia. Psoriatic arthritis shows periostitis. Haemochromatosis involves the metacarpal heads.
  85. The correct answer is B (Osteonecrosis, likely steroid related, with reactive interface around infarcted bone): A serpiginous low T1 margin with a double line sign on T2 is highly specific for avascular necrosis, here likely steroid related. Transient osteoporosis causes diffuse marrow oedema without a defined margin. A subchondral insufficiency fracture is a single line. Septic arthritis would demonstrate effusion and synovitis rather than a geographic femoral head lesion.
  86. The correct answer is B (A crescent sign from subchondral fracture, indicating an established avascular phase): In Perthes disease the subchondral lucent line is the crescent sign, a subchondral fracture that marks the avascular necrotic segment and helps assess extent. SCFE occurs in older adolescents and affects the physis. Harris lines are transverse metaphyseal lines. Subchondral cysts are a feature of osteoarthritis in adults, not a six-year-old with a small dense epiphysis.
  87. The correct answer is D (Klein line, indicating slipped capital femoral epiphysis in teenagers): Klein line drawn along the superior femoral neck should intersect the lateral epiphysis; failure to do so indicates slipped capital femoral epiphysis, which presents in overweight adolescents with referred knee pain. Shenton, Hilgenreiner and Perkins lines assess dysplasia and displacement in infants. Perthes affects younger children and is not assessed with these lines.
  88. The correct answer is D (Osteopetrosis, a sclerosing bone dysplasia from defective osteoclast resorption): Diffuse sclerosis, endplate banding, bone within bone and Erlenmeyer flask deformity with fractures and marrow failure indicate osteopetrosis. Pyknodysostosis also has sclerosis and fractures but includes acro-osteolysis and a distinctive facial skeleton. Hypervitaminosis D gives metaphyseal bands. Fluorosis occurs in adults with prolonged exposure.
  89. The correct answer is A (Fibrous dysplasia, a developmental lesion with fibro-osseous replacement of medullary bone): A young patient with an expansile, well-defined proximal femoral lesion, a thick sclerotic rind and varus deformity fits fibrous dysplasia, the shepherd's crook pattern. Paget disease is unusual at this age and gives coarse trabeculae with cortical thickening. A non-ossifying fibroma is eccentric and cortically based. Low-grade osteosarcoma can look expansile but lacks the homogeneous hazy matrix and rind.
  90. The correct answer is A (Catheter angiography with embolisation of the bleeding arterial branch): A contrast blush from an internal iliac branch in a patient who remains haemodynamically unwell after a binder indicates active arterial bleeding that angiographic embolisation can treat directly. Preperitoneal packing is a reasonable option where angiography is unavailable but does not target the vessel. External fixation stabilises the ring but does not stop arterial bleeding. Laparotomy risks losing tamponade of the retroperitoneal haematoma.
  91. The correct answer is C (MRI of the hip, with T1 and STIR sequences): MRI is the most sensitive and specific test for occult hip fracture and can be done promptly, showing a T1 low signal line with marrow oedema. Repeat radiographs delay care. Bone scans are often negative in the first days after injury in older patients. An effusion on ultrasound does not prove a fracture. CT is an alternative where MRI is unavailable.
  92. The correct answer is D (Maisonneuve injury, with radiographs of the full length of the fibula): Medial clear space widening with tibiofibular widening and tenderness near the knee indicates a Maisonneuve injury, in which an external rotation force travels up the interosseous membrane to a proximal fibular fracture. Radiographs of the full fibula confirm it. A syndesmotic sprain alone would not explain the knee tenderness. A Weber C fracture shows a fibular fracture at the ankle, and an isolated deltoid rupture does not explain the tibiofibular widening.
  93. The correct answer is D (Galeazzi fracture dislocation, involving the distal radioulnar joint): A radial shaft fracture with distal radioulnar joint disruption is a Galeazzi injury. Monteggia is the reverse pattern, an ulnar shaft fracture with radial head dislocation. Essex-Lopresti has a radial head fracture, interosseous membrane tear and distal radioulnar joint injury. A Colles fracture is metaphyseal and does not typically show shaft involvement.
  94. The correct answer is B (Posterior glenohumeral dislocation with a lightbulb appearance of the humeral head): Seizures and electric shocks cause posterior dislocation, with an arm fixed in internal rotation and a lightbulb humeral head because of fixed rotation. Loss of normal overlap is another clue. Anterior dislocation is more common but typically leaves the arm externally rotated. Acromioclavicular injury does not fix rotation. A reverse Hill-Sachs lesion accompanies posterior dislocations, and an axillary or Y view confirms the diagnosis.
  95. The correct answer is A (Myeloma lesions often show little tracer uptake, so scintigraphy underestimates disease extent): Multiple lytic lesions with anaemia and relatively little uptake on bone scintigraphy are classic for myeloma, where osteoblastic activity is low and the survey is the better test. Renal metastases can be photopenic, but myeloma is more likely with this combination. Brown tumours would normally take up tracer avidly. Assuming scintigraphy is more sensitive would wrongly dismiss the survey findings.
  96. The correct answer is D (Talocalcaneal coalition, best characterised with CT of the hindfoot): The C-sign and talar beak with a rigid, painful flatfoot and peroneal spasm are typical of talocalcaneal coalition, and CT defines the bony, cartilaginous or fibrous bridge. Calcaneonavicular coalition produces the anteater sign on lateral views and is best seen on oblique radiographs. An accessory navicular is a medial midfoot finding. Pes cavus is the opposite foot shape.
  97. The correct answer is A (Osteomalacia with Looser zones from malabsorption of vitamin D): Symmetrical thin lucent bands perpendicular to the cortex at the medial femoral necks and pubic rami are Looser zones, and with a malabsorption state, proximal weakness and high alkaline phosphatase they indicate osteomalacia. Hyperparathyroidism produces subperiosteal resorption rather than Looser zones. Renal osteodystrophy has no renal disease here. Insufficiency fractures of osteoporosis are sclerotic lines rather than symmetrical perpendicular bands.
  98. The correct answer is D (Medullary bone infarct from marrow ischaemia, likely steroid related): A serpiginous calcified border, a fat-containing centre and a double line on T2 in a steroid-treated patient indicate a medullary bone infarct. Enchondroma has lobulated T2 hyperintense cartilage without a fatty core. Chondrosarcoma would show destruction or soft tissue extension. Fibrous dysplasia has ground glass density and a rind. The fat-preserving centre is the discriminating clue.
  99. The correct answer is D (Haemochromatosis arthropathy from iron deposition in the synovium and cartilage): Hook-like osteophytes on the radial side of the second and third metacarpal heads with subchondral cysts and wrist chondrocalcinosis suggest haemochromatosis arthropathy, particularly with diabetes and abnormal liver function tests. Calcium pyrophosphate disease also causes chondrocalcinosis but usually favours the radiocarpal joint and spares these metacarpal heads. Common osteoarthritis spares the second and third metacarpophalangeal joints. Rheumatoid arthritis shows marginal erosions and osteopenia rather than hook osteophytes.
  100. The correct answer is C (Unstable lesion, because high T2 signal fluid undermines the fragment): A high T2 signal rim or cyst-like fluid beneath an osteochondral fragment indicates instability, as synovial fluid tracks around it, even with intact overlying cartilage. Such lesions often need surgery. Fragment position in the crater alone does not prove stability. Marrow oedema is common but does not account for a separate fragment with underlying fluid.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Sacral insufficiency fracture, with an H-shaped uptake pattern called the Honda sign): Increased uptake in both sacral alae joined by a horizontal band across S2 and S3 gives the H-shaped Honda sign of sacral insufficiency fracture, which is common in older women on long-term corticosteroids. Metastasis can mimic it but the pattern is usually asymmetric or focal. Sacroiliitis localises to the joint lines. Paget disease enlarges the bone with diffuse uptake.
  2. The correct answer is C (Displaced bucket-handle tear of the medial meniscus with a double PCL sign): A low-signal curved fragment in the notch lying parallel and anterior to the posterior cruciate ligament, with a truncated meniscal body, is the double PCL sign of a displaced bucket-handle tear. The truncated medial meniscus places it medially. A flipped ACL stump lies in the anterior notch with absent ligament fibres. A flap tear gives a fragment in a recess without the long central body loss.
  3. The correct answer is C (Small cortical avulsion fracture from the anterolateral margin of the lateral tibial plateau): Kissing contusions at the lateral femoral condyle and posterolateral tibial plateau with a torn ACL indicate a pivot-shift injury, and a Segond fracture is the associated small avulsion from the anterolateral lateral tibial plateau. A fibular head avulsion suggests a posterolateral corner injury. A posterior rim avulsion belongs with PCL injury. An MCL tear can coexist but is not the characteristic bony association.
  4. The correct answer is B (Posterior root tear of the medial meniscus with extrusion and subchondral stress response): Loss of the root attachment produces a ghost sign, extrusion and secondary subchondral stress in the loaded compartment, and it often follows low-energy squatting. Spontaneous osteonecrosis is a related condition but the meniscus would not be extruded with an absent root. The lateral radial tear is in the wrong compartment. A horizontal body tear does not typically extrude the whole meniscus.
  5. The correct answer is C (Grade 2 partial tear with preserved continuity of the superficial fibres): A grade 2 injury shows partial fibre disruption with signal change and thickening while the superficial fibres remain continuous. A complete rupture would show discontinuity and often retraction. Grade 1 shows periligamentous oedema without fibre disruption. Pellegrini-Stieda calcification is a chronic finding rather than a grade.
  6. The correct answer is C (Marrow oedema in the inferomedial patella and anterolateral femoral condyle with a torn medial patellofemoral ligament): As the patella dislocates laterally and relocates, the inferomedial patella strikes the anterolateral femoral condyle and the medial patellofemoral ligament tears. The mid lateral femoral condyle and posterolateral tibial plateau pattern belongs to ACL rupture. The anterior tibia and posterior femoral pattern suggests a posterior force injury, and the medial compartment pattern suggests a degenerative root problem.
  7. The correct answer is B (Fluid-intensity signal interposed between the fragment and the subchondral bone of the condyle): Fluid-intensity signal behind the fragment means synovial fluid has gained access, which signals loosening and instability. A sclerotic low T1 margin suggests chronic healing or a stable lesion. Intact articular cartilage supports stability. The typical location is common to stable and unstable lesions and does not discriminate between them.
  8. The correct answer is A (Advanced fatty infiltration with atrophy of the supraspinatus muscle belly): Advanced fatty infiltration and atrophy of the rotator cuff muscle belly predict a poor repair and a high re-tear rate, because the muscle cannot regain function even when the tendon is reattached. A hooked acromion and enthesophyte relate to impingement. Biceps subluxation signals a subscapularis problem. Bursal fluid is a common nonspecific finding.
  9. The correct answer is A (Irregular cleft extending laterally into the labrum, posterior to the biceps anchor): A recess is smooth, medially directed, and lies anterior to the biceps anchor, whereas a SLAP tear is irregular, laterally directed and extends posteriorly past the anchor. The Buford complex is a normal variant with absent anterosuperior labrum. Anteroinferior stripping describes a Bankart type lesion and does not involve the superior labrum.
  10. The correct answer is C (Anterior labroligamentous periosteal sleeve avulsion with medial displacement of the labrum): In a periosteal sleeve avulsion the labrum, with its intact periosteum, slides medially and heals in a malpositioned rolled-up state. A Bankart lesion has a torn periosteum. A Perthes lesion keeps the labrum near its normal site because the periosteum is intact but not displaced. A glenolabral articular disruption involves a cartilage flap rather than medial labral rolling.
  11. The correct answer is C (Anisotropy artefact in a normal tendon, requiring no further investigation for this finding): Tendons look hypoechoic when the beam is not perpendicular to the fibres, and the appearance normalises when the probe is angled correctly. A true tear stays hypoechoic regardless of angle. Resorptive calcification would persist and may show a shadow or posterior brightness. A full-thickness tear would show a persistent gap.
  12. The correct answer is D (Mid-portion Achilles tendinopathy with intact tendon fibres and no tear): A fusiform thickened hypoechoic tendon with neovascularity at 2 to 6 cm above the insertion is typical of mid-portion tendinopathy. A partial tear would show a focal defect or discontinuity. Haglund syndrome affects the insertion and bursa. Paratenonitis shows peritendinous fluid but the tendon itself keeps normal calibre.
  13. The correct answer is B (Aspherical femoral head-neck junction causing shear on the anterosuperior acetabular rim): A raised alpha angle with lost offset defines cam morphology, which shears the anterosuperior labrum and cartilage during flexion. Pincer impingement arises from rim overcoverage and shows a normal alpha angle. Dysplasia shows undercoverage. Ischiofemoral impingement causes posterior hip pain with quadratus femoris oedema.
  14. The correct answer is A (Crossover sign with a prominent ischial spine and posterior wall sign): Acetabular retroversion shows the crossover sign, a prominent ischial spine and a posterior wall sign. An alpha angle above 55 degrees and a pistol-grip deformity describe cam morphology on the femoral side. A raised Tonnis angle with a low centre-edge angle indicates dysplasia and undercoverage rather than overcoverage.
  15. The correct answer is A (Double line sign on T2 weighted images at the margin of necrotic bone): The double line sign, with an outer low and inner high signal line around the necrotic zone, is highly specific for osteonecrosis. Diffuse marrow oedema without a band suggests transient oedema of the hip. A subchondral line parallel to the surface suggests insufficiency fracture. An effusion with synovitis is nonspecific.
  16. The correct answer is D (Longitudinal split tear of peroneus brevis in the retromalleolar groove): A boomerang or chevron shaped brevis wrapped around the longus in the groove is characteristic of a longitudinal split tear. A longus rupture would leave a tendon gap near the cuboid. Tenosynovitis lacks intrasubstance tearing. Subluxation is shown by displacement lateral to the fibula and a retinaculum injury, not a split tendon.
  17. The correct answer is B (Syndesmotic sprain involving the anterior inferior tibiofibular ligament): Pain above the ankle after an external rotation mechanism, with oedema of the ligament crossing the anterior distal tibiofibular joint and a widened interval, indicates a syndesmotic sprain. An inversion injury would disrupt the lateral ligaments instead. A deltoid tear can coexist but does not explain the tibiofibular widening. A Maisonneuve injury requires a proximal fibular fracture, which is not described.
  18. The correct answer is C (Posterior tibial tendon dysfunction with acquired flatfoot deformity): A split, enlarged tendon behind the medial malleolus with surrounding fluid and a thinned spring ligament in a middle-aged woman with a progressive flat arch fits posterior tibial tendon dysfunction. Flexor digitorum longus disease is rare and does not flatten the arch. Tarsal tunnel syndrome gives neuropathic symptoms. Deltoid insufficiency causes valgus tilt but not a tendon split.
  19. The correct answer is A (Class 1B traumatic avulsion at the ulnar attachment of the complex): Ulnar-sided peripheral detachment at the fovea, often with a styloid fracture, is Palmer 1B. A 1A tear is a central disc perforation. A 1D injury detaches the radial attachment. Class 2C is a degenerative perforation seen with ulnar impaction and lunate change.
  20. The correct answer is D (Unenhanced MRI of the wrist to assess for marrow oedema or fracture line): MRI has high sensitivity and specificity for occult scaphoid fracture and also identifies other causes of wrist pain. Further radiographs delay diagnosis. Planar bone scintigraphy has lower specificity and needs delay for uptake. Ultrasound is operator dependent and not reliable for scaphoid waist fractures.
  21. The correct answer is C (Avascular necrosis of the proximal scaphoid fragment with nonunion): A nonunited proximal pole fragment that is sclerotic, low on T1 signal and non-enhancing after gadolinium has lost its blood supply, indicating avascular necrosis. Nonunion with preserved vascularity would enhance. Kienbock disease affects the lunate. Scapholunate injury widens the interval without necrosis of the scaphoid pole.
  22. The correct answer is A (Grade 2 strain with a partial tear at the myotendinous junction): Partial fibre disruption of less than complete cross-section at the myotendinous junction defines grade 2. Grade 1 has oedema without disruption. Grade 3 is complete tear with retraction. An ischial avulsion would show detachment from the tuberosity and retraction, which is not present.
  23. The correct answer is B (Mature peripheral rim ossification with a less mineralised central zone): Myositis ossificans matures from the outside inward, so the periphery is densely ossified and the centre is softer, called the zoning phenomenon. Osteosarcoma shows the reverse, with dense central mineralisation. Cortical destruction and rapid growth point towards malignancy.
  24. The correct answer is A (Atypical lipomatous tumour, confirmed by MDM2 amplification on biopsy tissue): A large deep fatty thigh mass with septa over 2 mm and a nodular enhancing component is an atypical lipomatous tumour until proved otherwise, and MDM2 amplification confirms it. A simple lipoma follows fat on all sequences with fine septa. Myxoid liposarcoma is predominantly non-fatty with high T2 signal. Haemangioma shows serpentine vessels rather than a nodule.
  25. The correct answer is A (Benign peripheral nerve sheath tumour such as a schwannoma): A fusiform, well-defined mass along the sciatic nerve with fat at each pole (split fat sign) and a target pattern of T2 signal is a benign nerve sheath tumour such as a schwannoma. A malignant tumour would be larger, infiltrative and heterogeneous. A myxoma can have a fat rim but lacks nerve continuity. Synovial sarcoma is heterogeneous rather than fusiform with a target pattern.
  26. The correct answer is B (Pigmented villonodular synovitis, the diffuse form of tenosynovial giant cell tumour): Nodular synovium with haemosiderin blooming on gradient echo images and recurrent haemarthrosis is classic for pigmented villonodular synovitis. Haemophilia can look similar, but coagulation is normal here. Synovial chondromatosis shows calcified bodies. Rheumatoid pannus is not typically haemosiderin laden.
  27. The correct answer is C (Popliteal cyst communicating with the knee joint through the posterior capsule): The classic neck between the medial gastrocnemius head and semimembranosus identifies a Baker cyst. An aneurysm would show arterial continuity and Doppler flow. A parameniscal cyst arises at the joint line. A sarcoma would be solid with vascularity.
  28. The correct answer is A (Type 2, representing fatty replacement of the endplate marrow): Type 2 changes follow fat signal, bright on T1 and suppressed with fat saturation. Type 1 is low on T1 and high on T2. Type 3 is low on both sequences from sclerosis. Infection shows disc hyperintensity, erosion and enhancement.
  29. The correct answer is D (Sequestration with cranial migration of a free fragment): A fragment 12 mm above the disc space with no tissue connecting it to the parent disc is a sequestered free fragment that has migrated cranially. A migrated extrusion would remain continuous with the disc through a neck. A subligamentous extrusion is contained deep to the posterior longitudinal ligament. A protrusion has a base wider than the displaced material, so it fits only a minor herniation.
  30. The correct answer is A (Right L5 traversing root within the lateral recess): A paracentral or posterolateral herniation at L4/5 compresses the traversing L5 root, which explains the foot drop and dorsal foot sensory loss. The L4 exiting root is affected by foraminal herniation. The S1 root is affected at L5/S1. Bilateral cauda equina compression would produce symmetrical findings.
  31. The correct answer is B (Urgent surgical referral for same-day decompression of the cauda equina): Bilateral leg weakness, saddle numbness and acute urinary retention with a large central disc extrusion indicate cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency needing same-day decompression. Further imaging adds delay because MRI has already shown the cause. Steroids do not relieve mechanical compression. Whole spine MRI is unnecessary before decompression of a clear cause.
  32. The correct answer is B (Thin-section CT of the L5 pars interarticularis with sagittal reformats): Thin-section CT displays pars cortical continuity and stages the lesion as stress reaction, incomplete or complete defect, which guides rest or bracing versus surgery. Flexion and extension films test instability, not early pars injury. Gadolinium does not resolve cortical detail. Myelography images roots rather than bone.
  33. The correct answer is A (Isthmic spondylolisthesis with 25 to 50 percent slippage, Meyerding grade II): Slippage between 25 and 50 percent is grade II, and bilateral pars defects in an adolescent indicate isthmic type. Grade I is up to 25 percent and grade III is 50 to 75 percent. Degenerative slips occur in older adults with intact pars.
  34. The correct answer is B (Convex posterior cortex bulge with pedicle signal change and an epidural soft tissue component): Posterior convex bulging, pedicle involvement and an epidural soft tissue mass indicate tumour infiltration. A band-like pattern with preserved marrow, a fluid cleft and a retropulsed fragment with multiple levels favour benign osteoporotic collapse.
  35. The correct answer is C (Grade 2, moderate fracture with 25 to 40 percent height loss): Thirty percent height loss lies in the 25 to 40 percent band for a moderate grade 2 deformity. Grade 1 is 20 to 25 percent, and grade 3 is above 40 percent. Grade 0 describes a normal vertebra.
  36. The correct answer is A (Fracture through the posterior vertebral wall with retropulsion into the spinal canal): Burst fractures involve the posterior wall of the body, often with retropulsed fragments narrowing the canal. Wedge fractures keep the posterior wall intact. A horizontal split through the pedicles describes a Chance injury. An isolated spinous fracture spares the body.
  37. The correct answer is D (Hollow viscus or mesenteric injury from flexion over the lap belt): A horizontal fracture through the vertebral body, pedicles and spinous process after a lap-belt-only restraint is a flexion-distraction (Chance) injury, and the belt compresses the abdomen over the fulcrum. Hollow viscus and mesenteric injuries are the classic associations. Renal artery injury is less typical. Cord injury is often absent in this injury, and pancreatic transection is not the most common association.
  38. The correct answer is A (Tuberculous spondylitis with paraspinal abscess and relative disc sparing): Relative disc preservation, large calcified paraspinal collections and multilevel subligamentous spread are typical of tuberculosis. Pyogenic infection destroys the disc early. Brucellosis tends to be lumbar with small collections. Metastasis would show a solid mass rather than an abscess.
  39. The correct answer is B (T2 hyperintense disc without an intranuclear cleft and an enhancing paravertebral collection): Infected discs are bright on T2, lose the intranuclear cleft, and are accompanied by endplate erosion and soft tissue collections. Vacuum phenomenon and desiccation point to degeneration. Modic 1 changes keep a dark disc. A Schmorl node is a benign herniation.
  40. The correct answer is A (Periarticular marrow oedema on STIR seen on at least two slices): Active inflammation appears as bone marrow oedema on STIR or fat-saturated T2 near the joint on multiple slices. Fatty metaplasia and sclerosis represent chronic structural damage. Joint space widening and erosions show structural change rather than activity.
  41. The correct answer is B (Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis (DISH) affecting the thoracic spine): Flowing ossification across four or more levels with preserved discs and normal sacroiliac joints defines DISH, usually right-sided in the thoracic spine. Ankylosing spondylitis produces thin syndesmophytes with sacroiliitis. Psoriatic disease shows non-marginal syndesmophytes. Spondylosis deformans narrows the disc.
  42. The correct answer is A (Asymmetric unilateral sacroiliitis with bulky non-marginal syndesmophytes in the lumbar spine): Psoriatic and reactive disease tend to be asymmetric with bulky non-marginal syndesmophytes. Symmetric sacroiliitis with thin marginal syndesmophytes, shiny corners, squaring and bamboo spine belong to classic ankylosing spondylitis.
  43. The correct answer is D (Plantar fasciitis with thickening above the 4 mm threshold): A fascia measuring about 6 mm at the calcaneal insertion with hypoechoic change and a small spur is the typical appearance of plantar fasciitis, and the morning first-step pain fits. Plantar fibromatosis produces a discrete nodule, usually in the mid-arch rather than at the insertion. A stress fracture needs marrow oedema or a cortical abnormality on cross-sectional imaging. A partial tear follows a sudden event and shows perifascial fluid with interruption of fibres.
  44. The correct answer is A (Osteoporosis diagnosed clinically from a low-trauma vertebral fracture despite the T-score): A low-trauma vertebral fracture establishes osteoporosis clinically whatever the T-score, so he is in the highest risk group. Classifying him as osteopenic by T-score alone would delay treatment. A second fracture is not required for the diagnosis. Waiting for a repeat T-score below minus 2.5 applies the densitometric criterion that a fragility fracture replaces.
  45. The correct answer is B (Partial gluteus medius tear within greater trochanteric pain syndrome): Partial tearing of gluteus medius at its trochanteric attachment with peritrochanteric fluid explains lateral hip pain on lying. The iliotibial band syndrome occurs at the knee. A stress fracture would show marrow oedema in the neck. Ischiofemoral impingement causes posterior pain.
  46. The correct answer is B (Complete discoid lateral meniscus as a congenital variant): A lateral meniscus with continuous body tissue on three or more consecutive sagittal slices defines a discoid meniscus, a congenital variant prone to tearing. A bucket-handle tear would show a notch fragment. A meniscal ossicle is small and posterior. A meniscal cyst is a fluid collection.
  47. The correct answer is C (Adhesive capsulitis with capsular thickening and reduced joint volume): Axillary pouch thickening, rotator interval fat obliteration and a small joint volume in a diabetic patient with stiffness indicate adhesive capsulitis. A full cuff tear would show contrast leaking. Calcific tendinitis shows a deposit. Osteoarthritis shows cartilage loss and osteophytes.
  48. The correct answer is B (Posteromedial talar dome, the most common site of such lesions): Inversion in plantar flexion with external rotation impacts the posteromedial talar dome, the more frequent site. Anterolateral lesions arise from inversion in dorsiflexion. A tibial plafond lesion is rarer. The posterior process is the site of os trigonum syndrome.
  49. The correct answer is B (Kienbock disease, or avascular necrosis of the lunate): Diffuse low T1 lunate signal with negative ulnar variance fits Kienbock disease. Ulnolunate impaction occurs with positive variance and involves the ulnar proximal lunate. Preiser disease affects the scaphoid. Scaphoid nonunion advanced collapse requires a scaphoid abnormality.
  50. The correct answer is C (Facet joint synovial cyst associated with degenerative spondylolisthesis): A cystic lesion contiguous with a degenerate facet and associated with a slip is a synovial cyst. A nerve sheath tumour would be solid and enhancing. A disc fragment would show disc signal and continuity with the disc space. An abscess would have clinical features of infection and discitis.
  51. The correct answer is A (Large salvageable penumbra around a small core, favouring urgent endovascular clot retrieval): A Tmax abnormality of 120 mL against a core of 15 mL shows a large volume of tissue at risk, and the dense M1 segment is a target for retrieval at three hours. Intravenous thrombolysis alone is less likely to recanalise a proximal M1 occlusion than retrieval. A core of 15 mL is well below the size at which reperfusion stops helping. Waiting for repeat imaging would let the penumbra infarct.
  52. The correct answer is C (Infarct is probably within a few hours of onset, so thrombolysis may still be considered): Diffusion restriction appears within minutes, while FLAIR signal rises later, usually after roughly four and a half hours, so the mismatch suggests an early lesion. Subacute infarcts usually show FLAIR change as well. Old gliosis does not restrict diffusion. A FLAIR negative lesion says nothing against salvageable tissue and actually argues for early presentation.
  53. The correct answer is C (A single dominant thalamoperforator trunk arising from one posterior cerebral artery): Bilateral paramedian thalamic and rostral midbrain infarction arises from a shared trunk supplying both sides, the artery of Percheron. Basilar tip occlusion would also involve occipital and cerebellar territories. Deep venous thrombosis gives thalamic oedema but typically less restricted diffusion and abnormal venous signal. Choroidal infarcts affect the internal capsule posterior limbs, not the medial thalami.
  54. The correct answer is C (Distal basilar artery at the bifurcation into the posterior cerebral arteries): Infarcts of the rostral brainstem, thalami, superior cerebellum and occipital lobes follow the territory fed by the basilar apex and its branches. Vertebral origin disease usually gives lower brainstem and cerebellar infarcts. Posterior inferior cerebellar occlusion spares the thalami and occipital lobes. A carotid terminus occlusion would involve the anterior circulation.
  55. The correct answer is D (Middle meningeal artery lacerated by the underlying bony injury): A lentiform collection bounded by sutures after a temporal fracture and a lucid interval points to arterial bleeding from the middle meningeal artery. Bridging vein rupture produces a crescentic subdural collection that crosses sutures. A sagittal sinus injury would lie at the vertex. A lenticulostriate bleed is intraparenchymal and deep.
  56. The correct answer is C (Chronic hypertensive degeneration of small deep perforating arteries): Putaminal haematoma in a hypertensive patient with a normal angiogram reflects lipohyalinosis of lenticulostriate arteries. Amyloid angiopathy favours lobar bleeds in older patients. A ruptured aneurysm would show subarachnoid or sylvian blood and an aneurysm on angiography.
