Haemorrhagic conversion occurs within a cortical or large territory infarct, not a deep isolated clot.
  57. The correct answer is A (Beta amyloid deposition within the walls of small superficial leptomeningeal arteries): Strictly lobar microbleeds with superficial siderosis in an older patient is the typical imaging picture of amyloid angiopathy. Hypertensive disease places microbleeds in basal ganglia, thalami and pons. Shear injury microbleeds sit at the grey white junction and corpus callosum after trauma. Familial cavernomas show larger lesions with a hemosiderin rim and mixed core.
  58. The correct answer is D (Lumbar puncture with spectrophotometry for bilirubin in cerebrospinal fluid): CT sensitivity for subarachnoid blood falls over days as blood clears, so a normal scan three days on does not exclude it. Xanthochromia from bilirubin develops after about twelve hours and persists, making puncture appropriate. Repeating CT adds little. Enhanced MRI does not detect acute bleeding reliably. Doppler assesses vasospasm after a confirmed bleed.
  59. The correct answer is B (Carotid origin of the posterior communicating artery on that side): A pupil involving third nerve palsy results from compression of the nerve as it passes beside the posterior communicating origin. Anterior communicating aneurysms usually present with frontal or hypothalamic effects. Basilar trunk aneurysms are rare and do not tend to produce an isolated oculomotor palsy. Distal anterior cerebral aneurysms do not neighbour the third nerve.
  60. The correct answer is C (CT angiography with perfusion to show arterial narrowing and hypoperfused territory): Neurological decline in the second week after subarachnoid haemorrhage with clear CT suggests vasospasm related ischaemia. Angiography with perfusion shows narrowed vessels and the hypoperfused territory, guiding treatment. Coil compaction causes rebleeding, which CT would show. Venous thrombosis and ventriculitis are less likely without supporting features.
  61. The correct answer is B (Venous drainage into the deep system through the internal cerebral vein): The score is built from nidus size, eloquence of adjacent brain and pattern of venous drainage, with deep drainage scoring one point. Associated aneurysms, number of feeders and haemorrhage history influence risk assessment but are not part of the score. The size of 2.5 cm scores one point and the visual cortex counts as eloquent.
  62. The correct answer is A (Developmental venous anomaly, a benign variant that needs no intervention): Radially arranged medullary veins converging on a single collector in normal parenchyma are a developmental venous anomaly, which drains normal brain and is left alone. Resecting the collector risks venous infarction, so that management is wrong even with the right diagnosis. A cavernous malformation has a haemosiderin rim on susceptibility imaging. A dural fistula is centred on a sinus wall with arterialised flow.
  63. The correct answer is D (Venography by CT or MR showing a filling defect in the sinus): A hyperdense sinus with bilateral parasagittal haemorrhage in a woman on hormones points to cerebral venous thrombosis, which is confirmed by a filling defect on CT or MR venography. Diffusion imaging would show infarction in an arterial pattern, which these lesions do not follow. CT angiography of the arteries cannot show sinus occlusion. Contrast MRI may show some change but does not confirm thrombus.
  64. The correct answer is D (Marked restricted diffusion with low ADC values throughout the central cavity): Pus within an abscess cavity is viscous and restricts diffusion, whereas necrotic tumour fluid generally has facilitated diffusion. Elevated rim perfusion and spectroscopic choline favour glioblastoma. Callosal crossing is a tumour behaviour. The smooth thin wall, thinner medially, is already stated but is less decisive than central restriction.
  65. The correct answer is B (Primary B cell lymphoma confined to the central nervous system): Dense cellular tumours are hyperattenuating on CT, restrict diffusion and enhance uniformly, typical for lymphoma in the periventricular white matter. Glioblastoma usually necrotic with raised perfusion. A metastasis is normally spherical with greater oedema. Tumefactive demyelination produces an open ring and less restriction.
  66. The correct answer is B (Vestibular schwannoma arising from the eighth cranial nerve complex): Acoustic canal extension with canal widening and an acute interface with the bone is characteristic of schwannoma, and cystic change is common. Meningioma has an obtuse dural interface with a tail and rarely expands the canal. Epidermoid cysts do not enhance. A metastasis would be unusual with this history and morphology.
  67. The correct answer is C (Contrast MRI of the spine to look for cerebrospinal fluid seeding): A dense, diffusion restricting vermian mass in a child is typically medulloblastoma, which seeds the spine through cerebrospinal fluid. Spinal contrast MRI should precede surgery because post-operative blood and inflammation mimic drop metastases. Lumbar puncture is unsafe with obstructive hydrocephalus. Spectroscopy may suggest the tumour type but does not stage the disease.
  68. The correct answer is C (Pilocytic astrocytoma, a low grade tumour with a good surgical prognosis): A cerebellar cyst with an enhancing mural nodule and no restriction is the classic pattern of pilocytic astrocytoma in childhood. Medulloblastoma is solid, midline and dense with restricted diffusion. Ependymoma squeezes through foramina and calcifies. Rhabdoid tumours occur in infants and appear aggressive with haemorrhage and restriction.
  69. The correct answer is C (Von Hippel Lindau disease causing a cerebellar haemangioblastoma): An enhancing nodule with vascular flow voids in a cerebellar cyst is typical of haemangioblastoma, and retinal angiomas complete the picture of Von Hippel Lindau. Neurofibromatosis type 2 gives bilateral vestibular schwannomas and meningiomas. Tuberous sclerosis tumours arise near the foramen of Monro. Sturge Weber has leptomeningeal angiomas.
  70. The correct answer is A (Tumefactive demyelination, usually in the setting of multiple sclerosis): An open ring that opens toward the cortex, with low perfusion and modest mass effect, strongly suggests active demyelination. A high grade glioma would show elevated blood volume. An abscess forms a complete ring thicker on the cortical side. An infarct would follow a vascular territory and not present subacutely in this way.
  71. The correct answer is A (Ovoid lesions perpendicular to the ventricle with callosal involvement): Lesions that radiate along medullary veins perpendicular to the ventricle and involve the corpus callosum are characteristic of demyelination. Small vessel disease tends to sit as confluent caps and bands around the ventricles and spares the callosum. Lacunes in deep nuclei favour ischaemia. Symmetric posterior change suggests a metabolic or toxic process.
  72. The correct answer is D (Herpes simplex encephalitis, treated with empirical intravenous aciclovir at once): Fever, seizure and limbic plus insular involvement with basal ganglia sparing is classic for herpes encephalitis, where treatment is started without waiting for confirmation. An MCA infarct would involve the putamen and follow a territory. Paraneoplastic limbic disease is subacute and usually bilateral. Prion disease has a slower course and cortical ribboning.
  73. The correct answer is D (Thiamine deficiency, treated with intravenous thiamine ahead of glucose): Symmetric lesions of the medial thalami, periaqueductal region and mammillary bodies in this clinical triad indicate thiamine deficiency. Thiamine should precede glucose to avoid precipitating worsening. Pontine myelinolysis involves the basis pontis after rapid sodium shifts. Hepatic encephalopathy shows globus pallidus T1 hyperintensity.
  74. The correct answer is C (No tracer uptake in the lesions on thallium-201 single photon emission imaging): Toxoplasma abscesses are not metabolically active and show no thallium uptake, while lymphoma is avid. Multiple basal ganglia lesions with an eccentric target sign also favour infection. A solitary callosal mass, intense glucose uptake and Epstein Barr virus DNA in cerebrospinal fluid each point toward lymphoma.
  75. The correct answer is A (Cortical ribbon restriction together with caudate and putamen signal change): Sporadic prion disease produces cortical gyral restriction on diffusion plus basal ganglia involvement, making diffusion the most sensitive imaging sequence. A limbic restricted pattern suggests autoimmune or herpes encephalitis. Watershed lesions arise from hypotension. White matter predominant restriction suggests a leukoencephalopathy.
  76. The correct answer is A (Hummingbird sign of midbrain atrophy, indicating progressive supranuclear palsy): Midbrain atrophy with relative pontine preservation gives the hummingbird profile on sagittal images, fitting vertical gaze palsy and early falls. The hot cross bun sign reflects pontine fibre loss in multiple system atrophy. Loss of nigrosome signal belongs to Parkinson disease. Knife edge atrophy marks frontotemporal dementia.
  77. The correct answer is B (Behavioural variant frontotemporal dementia with asymmetric lobar degeneration): Early personality change with frontal and anterior temporal atrophy and relative sparing of the medial temporal and parietal regions points to a frontotemporal disorder. Alzheimer disease shows early hippocampal and posterior parietal loss. Lewy body dementia has relative preservation of medial temporal structures but a different clinical picture. No infarcts are described.
  78. The correct answer is A (Acute callosal angle with tight high convexity sulci and wide sylvian fissures): Disproportionately enlarged subarachnoid spaces with tight sulci at the vertex and an acute callosal angle make up the typical pattern of normal pressure hydrocephalus. Global sulcal widening indicates atrophy. Frontal caps are common in ageing and not discriminating. A posterior fossa abnormality describes Chiari malformation.
  79. The correct answer is B (Cerebral aqueduct between the third and fourth ventricles): A normal fourth ventricle with dilated upstream ventricles locates the block at the aqueduct. Outlet obstruction would enlarge the fourth ventricle too. Blockage of both foramina of Monro would spare the third ventricle. Arachnoid granulation failure gives communicating hydrocephalus with every ventricle enlarged.
  80. The correct answer is B (Susceptibility weighted imaging showing multiple punctate foci of signal loss): Diffuse axonal injury produces small haemorrhages that are best shown as punctate signal loss on susceptibility weighted imaging. Diffusion imaging detects non-haemorrhagic shear lesions but does not show blood products. FLAIR shows the oedema of these lesions but misses micro-haemorrhage. T1 spin echo needs subacute methaemoglobin and is insensitive to tiny foci.
  81. The correct answer is B (Posterior cerebral artery infarction at the tentorial notch margin): Downward and medial uncal displacement squeezes the posterior cerebral artery against the free edge of the tentorium, causing occipital infarction. Anterior cerebral compression is the complication of subfalcine herniation. The anterior inferior cerebellar artery and middle cerebral artery are not compressed at the incisura in this way.
  82. The correct answer is A (Encasement of over two thirds of the intracavernous carotid circumference): Encasement of more than two thirds of the intracavernous carotid circumference is a highly specific sign of cavernous sinus invasion, corresponding to higher Knosp grades. Extension that stays medial to the carotid does not indicate invasion. Stalk deviation and sellar remodelling occur with expansile tumours without invasion.
  83. The correct answer is D (Haemorrhagic infarction of a pituitary adenoma with acute mass effect): A sudden headache with visual loss and a sellar lesion containing blood products is pituitary apoplexy, usually in a pre-existing adenoma. A Rathke cleft cyst may contain proteinaceous fluid but does not present acutely. Cavernous thrombosis centres on the sinus. Craniopharyngioma is suprasellar and calcified.
  84. The correct answer is B (Adamantinomatous craniopharyngioma arising from Rathke pouch epithelial remnants): A calcified, cystic and solid suprasellar mass in a child, with oily cyst fluid causing T1 hyperintensity, is typical of the adamantinomatous type. Adenomas are rare in children and seldom calcify. Optic pathway glioma expands the nerve and chiasm. Germinoma usually thickens the stalk and causes diabetes insipidus.
  85. The correct answer is A (Subependymal giant cell astrocytoma, a candidate for mTOR inhibitor therapy): Growth, avid enhancement and location at the foramen of Monro in tuberous sclerosis define a subependymal giant cell astrocytoma, which can obstruct cerebrospinal fluid flow. Stable nodules do not grow or enhance avidly. Ependymoma and central neurocytoma are less likely in this setting and do not account for the syndrome.
  86. The correct answer is B (Leptomeningeal enhancement with an enlarged ipsilateral choroid plexus): Gyriform cortical calcification with hemiatrophy in a child with a facial vascular birthmark and seizures defines Sturge Weber syndrome, and leptomeningeal enhancement with an enlarged ipsilateral choroid plexus is the expected MRI finding. Dyke Davidoff Masson syndrome shows calvarial thickening but no leptomeningeal angioma. Focal cortical dysplasia gives a transmantle sign without calcification. Subependymal nodules point to tuberous sclerosis.
  87. The correct answer is C (Melanoma, whose melanin and blood products shorten T1 relaxation): Intrinsically bright T1 lesions that bloom on susceptibility imaging are explained by melanin and blood products, a hallmark of melanoma metastases. Renal, thyroid and choriocarcinoma deposits also bleed but melanoma is the textbook answer. Small cell lung and oesophageal deposits generally lack intrinsic T1 brightness. Prostate disease tends to involve dura and bone.
  88. The correct answer is A (The posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome with vasogenic oedema): Hypertension or eclampsia with posterior vasogenic oedema that is bright on FLAIR and not restricted on diffusion defines this syndrome and is usually reversible. Arterial infarcts restrict diffusion. Venous thrombosis would show sinus abnormality and often haemorrhage. Herpes encephalitis favours the temporal lobes and presents with fever.
  89. The correct answer is A (Profound acute hypoxic ischaemic injury of the metabolically active deep nuclei): A sudden profound insult at term injures the most metabolically active regions: the ventrolateral thalami, posterior putamina, perirolandic cortex and brainstem. Prolonged partial hypoxia instead involves the watershed cortex and white matter. Hypoglycaemia mainly injures the occipital and parietal cortex. Kernicterus also gives T1 hyperintensity but centres on the globus pallidus and subthalamic nuclei.
  90. The correct answer is D (IDH mutant oligodendroglioma with 1p/19q co-deletion in a young adult): A frontal cortical and subcortical tumour with coarse calcification in an adult presenting with seizure is the typical oligodendroglioma picture, with little oedema and slow growth. A meningioma is extra-axial. Pleomorphic xanthoastrocytoma has a cyst and nodule near the surface in younger people. Dysembryoplastic tumours occur in children with intractable epilepsy.
  91. The correct answer is A (Colloid cyst intermittently obstructing flow through the foramina of Monro): A smooth hyperdense round lesion at the anterior third ventricle roof with positional headache is typical for a colloid cyst, which acts as a ball valve at the foramina of Monro. Central neurocytoma is a solid lobulated mass on the septum. Giant cell astrocytoma needs the syndrome. A papilloma sits in the atrium or fourth ventricle.
  92. The correct answer is C (Cine MRI of cerebrospinal fluid flow across the craniocervical junction): Tonsillar descent with a syrinx indicates Chiari I, and cine phase contrast MRI assesses cerebrospinal fluid flow across the foramen magnum to support decompression. Whole spine contrast MRI is reasonable only if the syrinx looks atypical. Flexion extension films assess instability, which is not the main planning question. Venography addresses a secondary cause that is not suggested here.
  93. The correct answer is D (Pars flaccida cholesteatoma with scutum erosion and ossicular destruction): Soft tissue in Prussak space with scutal blunting and ossicular erosion is the classic pars flaccida cholesteatoma pattern. Chronic otitis media with granulation can fill the middle ear but usually leaves the scutum sharp. A glomus tympanicum lies on the promontory and enhances avidly. Congenital cholesteatoma presents behind an intact drum in children.
  94. The correct answer is B (Non-echo-planar diffusion-weighted imaging, showing restricted diffusion in cholesteatoma): Keratin debris restricts diffusion, so non-echo-planar DWI shows high signal and avoids the susceptibility artefact of echo-planar sequences at the skull base. Granulation tissue enhances strongly, while cholesteatoma shows at most peripheral enhancement. T2 and CT cannot reliably separate the soft tissue types.
  95. The correct answer is C (Fenestral otosclerosis centred on the fissula ante fenestram): A lucent focus anterior to the oval window at the fissula ante fenestram, with a normal drum and conductive loss, is fenestral otosclerosis. The cochlear form shows a halo around the cochlea with sensorineural loss. Osteogenesis imperfecta and Paget disease are more diffuse and occur in different settings.
  96. The correct answer is C (Necrotising otitis externa with early skull base osteomyelitis): A diabetic or immunosuppressed older patient with severe pain, canal floor erosion and loss of the fat planes at the skull base has necrotising otitis externa. Carcinoma can look similar and may need biopsy, but the clinical picture favours infection. Keratosis obturans and mastoiditis do not produce this skull base pattern.
  97. The correct answer is A (Allergic fungal rhinosinusitis with dense inspissated allergic mucin): Expansile opacification of several sinuses with central hyperattenuating mucin and very low T2 signal in an asthmatic with polyps is allergic fungal rhinosinusitis. Invasive disease occurs in an immunocompromised host and destroys bone. A fungal ball is usually confined to one sinus, often the maxillary. Uncomplicated polyposis lacks central hyperattenuation and the markedly low T2 signal.
  98. The correct answer is B (Acute invasive mucormycosis with early perisinus fat involvement): Loss of turbinate enhancement with early retro-antral fat infiltration in a poorly controlled diabetic indicates angioinvasive mucormycosis. Invasive aspergillosis can look similar but occurs mainly in neutropenic or transplant patients. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis causes septal destruction over a longer course. Bacterial sinusitis enhances normally and does not produce a non-enhancing turbinate.
  99. The correct answer is C (Sinonasal inverted papilloma, with the hyperostosis marking the site of origin): A unilateral lateral nasal wall mass extending into the maxillary sinus with avid enhancement and focal hyperostosis at its attachment is inverted papilloma, the hyperostosis marking the origin to be resected. Squamous carcinoma destroys rather than thickens bone. An antrochoanal polyp is T2 bright and has a stalk through the ostium. Melanoma is typically T1 hyperintense and has no focal hyperostosis.
  100. The correct answer is B (Nerve enhancement with swelling, plus brain MRI for demyelination): Painful subacute monocular visual loss with pain on movement and a relative afferent pupillary defect is typical of optic neuritis, which shows nerve enhancement and swelling on fat-suppressed post-contrast imaging. Because it is commonly the first presentation of multiple sclerosis, brain MRI is added. Sheath meningioma and glioma show enlargement or a tram-track pattern, and myositis enhances the muscle and tendon without an afferent pupillary defect.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Subperiosteal abscess needing urgent surgical review for drainage): A lentiform rim-enhancing collection along the medial wall displacing the medial rectus after ethmoid sinusitis is a subperiosteal abscess, which usually needs surgical drainage in a child with proptosis. Orbital cellulitis without a collection is treated medically. Preseptal cellulitis lies anterior to the septum and spares movement. Cavernous sinus thrombosis shows a filling defect and bilateral signs rather than a medial collection.
  2. The correct answer is B (Thyroid-associated orbitopathy sparing the tendons of the enlarged muscles): Bilateral enlargement of the inferior and medial rectus bellies with tendon sparing in a patient with Graves disease is thyroid orbitopathy. Idiopathic myositis involves the tendon and is painful. IgG4-related disease enlarges the lacrimal gland and infraorbital nerve and does not follow the thyroid history. A carotid-cavernous fistula shows a dilated superior ophthalmic vein, not enlarged muscle bellies.
  3. The correct answer is C (Optic nerve sheath meningioma with calcification along the perioptic sheath): Tram-track enhancement around a normal central nerve, with calcification, is typical of optic nerve sheath meningioma in a middle-aged woman. Glioma enlarges the nerve itself in children, neuritis is painful with intrinsic enhancement, and idiopathic intracranial hypertension distends the sheath without enhancement.
  4. The correct answer is A (Retinoblastoma of the left globe with intraocular calcification): A posterior intraocular mass with calcification in a toddler with leukocoria is retinoblastoma until proved otherwise. Coats disease lacks calcification, persistent hyperplastic primary vitreous has a small globe with a retrolental stalk, and retinopathy of prematurity is linked to prematurity.
  5. The correct answer is D (A thick enhancing rim around the low-density collection): Rim enhancement around a low-density collection indicates a drainable abscess, whereas reactive oedema has no enhancing wall. Low attenuation and airway narrowing occur with both oedema and abscess, so neither discriminates. Reactive nodes with preserved hila accompany both conditions. A thick enhancing wall is the feature that separates the two.
  6. The correct answer is D (Ludwig angina, with the airway as the most urgent concern): Bilateral submandibular and sublingual infection of odontogenic origin with gas and airway narrowing is Ludwig angina, and securing the airway comes first. Parapharyngeal abscess, peritonsillar abscess and sialadenitis involve different spaces and are not bilateral floor of mouth disease.
  7. The correct answer is C (A deep parotid lobe tumour displacing parapharyngeal fat medially): Medial displacement of parapharyngeal fat with continuity to the deep parotid lobe indicates a parotid origin. Carotid space masses displace the space anteriorly but arise posterior to the styloid. Masticator masses displace fat posteriorly, and minor gland tumours are mucosal.
  8. The correct answer is A (Pleomorphic adenoma with a thin low signal capsule): A well-defined lobulated parotid mass with very high T2 signal and a thin capsule is typical of pleomorphic adenoma, the commonest salivary tumour. Warthin tumour favours older male smokers and may be bilateral. Carcinomas are infiltrative and lymphoma arises in glands with chronic autoimmune disease.
  9. The correct answer is A (Warthin tumour of both parotid tails, with oncocytic tracer retention): Warthin tumour accumulates pertechnetate through its oncocytic epithelium and retains it after stimulation, and it is often bilateral at the parotid tail in older male smokers. Pleomorphic adenomas are generally cold, and oncocytoma and metastases do not fit the bilateral retention pattern.
  10. The correct answer is B (Punctate sialectasis with a snowstorm or cherry blossom appearance): Diffuse bilateral parotid enlargement with scattered small hypoechoic foci in a sicca patient is Sjogren syndrome, which gives punctate sialectasis on sialography. A sausage-link main duct reflects chronic sialodochitis. Stretched sparse ducts occur with sialosis. A single stricture produces segmental dilatation of one gland rather than bilateral punctate change.
  11. The correct answer is A (Non-contrast CT of the floor of mouth, since most stones are radiopaque): About eighty percent of submandibular stones are radiopaque, so non-contrast CT or ultrasound is a good first test. MR sialography shows stones as signal voids and is mainly used for duct anatomy. Scintigraphy tests function, and conventional sialography is avoided during acute infection.
  12. The correct answer is C (Fine needle aspiration, since the nodule is TR5 and exceeds 10 mm): Solid composition, marked hypoechogenicity, a taller-than-wide shape and punctate echogenic foci place the nodule in TR5 on ACR TI-RADS, where biopsy is recommended at 10 mm or more. One year follow-up applies to lower categories, and radioiodine scanning is for hyperthyroidism.
  13. The correct answer is D (TR1 benign, since a spongiform appearance is scored as benign): An almost completely spongiform nodule is scored zero points in composition and falls in TR1, benign, so no biopsy is recommended regardless of size. The other categories would apply to nodules with solid components, hypoechogenicity or suspicious foci.
  14. The correct answer is B (Four-dimensional CT with early arterial enhancement and rapid washout): Four-dimensional CT shows a single adenoma by early arterial enhancement and rapid washout and gives the surgeon anatomical detail. Venous sampling lateralises but gives no anatomy and is invasive. Repeating sestamibi imaging adds little after a non-diagnostic study. Dynamic MRI has a lower detection rate for small adenomas.
  15. The correct answer is B (Metastatic papillary thyroid carcinoma with cystic and calcified change): Cystic change, punctate microcalcification and peripheral vascularity in a node with a thyroid nodule are typical of papillary thyroid cancer metastasis. Reactive nodes keep a hilum, tuberculous nodes lack punctate calcification, and lymphoma is solid and homogeneous.
  16. The correct answer is D (T3, because of cord fixation and paraglottic space invasion): Vocal cord fixation or paraglottic space invasion without cartilage penetration is T3 in a glottic cancer. T2 requires extension to supraglottis or subglottis, or impaired mobility. T4a needs invasion through the outer cortex of thyroid cartilage or into extralaryngeal tissue.
  17. The correct answer is B (T4a, moderately advanced local disease with extralaryngeal extension): Invasion through the thyroid cartilage outer cortex or into strap muscles is T4a. T3 disease stays in the larynx. T4b requires prevertebral space, carotid encasement or mediastinal involvement, and T2 disease lacks cartilage erosion.
  18. The correct answer is A (Nasopharyngeal carcinoma with parapharyngeal and skull base invasion): A unilateral enhancing nasopharyngeal mass with serous otitis media, parapharyngeal extension and clival marrow involvement in a man from an endemic region is nasopharyngeal carcinoma. Lymphoma is bulkier with homogeneous signal and less invasion. A chordoma arises in the clivus itself with very bright T2 signal. Adenoid cystic carcinoma tracks along nerves and is rare in this site.
  19. The correct answer is B (HPV-related oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma with cystic node): A cystic level II node in an adult, together with a small tonsillar primary, strongly suggests HPV-related oropharyngeal cancer with cystic nodal metastasis. A second branchial cleft cyst can look similar, but the tonsillar lesion makes it unlikely. Tuberculous nodes and thyroglossal cysts do not match.
  20. The correct answer is B (Level IIB, a node that can be spared in selected neck dissections): Level II nodes lie from the skull base to the hyoid; IIB lies posterior to the internal jugular vein with a fat plane. This sublevel may be spared in selected dissections where it is not at risk. Level IIA is anterior or in contact with the vein, and the others are in different regions.
  21. The correct answer is B (Foramen rotundum, carrying the maxillary division of the trigeminal nerve): The maxillary division passes through foramen rotundum, so perineural spread along it enlarges that foramen on CT. Foramen ovale carries the mandibular nerve. The vidian canal can be involved by spread from the pterygopalatine fossa but is not the main passage to the middle fossa. The inferior orbital fissure carries a branch of the maxillary nerve, not the trunk entering the cranium.
  22. The correct answer is D (Foramen ovale, via the auriculotemporal and mandibular nerves): Parotid tumours can follow the auriculotemporal nerve to the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve and then to foramen ovale, causing chin numbness and weakness of mastication. The other foramina carry nerves that do not match these deficits.
  23. The correct answer is C (Glomus jugulare paraganglioma with a salt-and-pepper flow void pattern): A vascular jugular foramen mass with permeative erosion of the jugular spine and salt-and-pepper flow voids is glomus jugulare paraganglioma. Schwannomas remodel smoothly, meningiomas cause hyperostosis, and metastases lack the flow-void pattern.
  24. The correct answer is D (Glossopharyngeal, vagus and accessory nerves pass through; the hypoglossal nerve uses its own canal): The glossopharyngeal, vagus and accessory nerves cross the jugular foramen, explaining hoarseness, dysphagia and weak shoulder elevation, while the hypoglossal nerve leaves through its own hypoglossal canal. Moving the hypoglossal nerve into the foramen leaves the accessory nerve elsewhere, which is wrong. The accessory nerve does pass through the foramen. The hypoglossal nerve does not exit via the foramen magnum.
  25. The correct answer is B (A zygomaticomaxillary complex fracture with three separation sites): Separation at the zygomaticofrontal suture, infraorbital rim and zygomatic arch defines the zygomaticomaxillary complex, or tripod, fracture. Le Fort patterns involve the pterygoid plates, and a pure blowout fracture spares the inferior orbital rim.
  26. The correct answer is D (Le Fort I, a horizontal fracture through the maxilla above the teeth): A horizontal fracture across the maxilla above the alveolar ridge with pterygoid plate fractures is a Le Fort I. Le Fort II is pyramidal, involving the nasal root and orbit, and Le Fort III separates the facial skeleton from the skull base at the zygomaticofrontal suture and arch.
  27. The correct answer is B (Entrapment of the inferior rectus muscle with restricted upgaze): Herniation of the inferior rectus through an orbital floor fracture can trap the muscle and restrict upgaze, causing diplopia. Optic nerve avulsion, cavernous sinus thrombosis and retrobulbar haemorrhage have different clinical findings.
  28. The correct answer is B (CT of the facial bones with multiplanar reformats): Multiplanar CT shows displacement, condylar involvement and associated facial fractures across the whole mandible in one acquisition. Orthopantomogram with a posteroanterior view is a reasonable screen in some centres but misses subtle condylar and ramus fractures. Cone beam CT of the joints omits the body, and MRI is less accurate for cortical fracture lines.
  29. The correct answer is B (Odontogenic keratocyst growing along the long axis of the jaw): A lucent angle and ramus lesion with scalloped corticated margins that grows along the long axis with little expansion is typical of odontogenic keratocyst. Dentigerous cysts surround an unerupted crown, ameloblastoma expands bone, and radicular cysts are at apices of non-vital teeth.
  30. The correct answer is D (Ameloblastoma of the posterior mandible with enhancing solid nodules): A multilocular soap-bubble posterior mandibular lesion with expansion, root resorption and enhancing solid nodules is ameloblastoma. Odontogenic myxoma, simple bone cyst and central giant cell granuloma differ in internal architecture and site.
  31. The correct answer is A (Dentigerous cyst enveloping the crown of the unerupted third molar): A unilocular lucency attached at the cementoenamel junction and enveloping an unerupted crown is a dentigerous cyst. Keratocysts, ameloblastoma and periapical abscess differ in loculation and attachment.
  32. The correct answer is D (Cholesterol granuloma of the petrous apex with blood products): High signal on both T1 and T2 without enhancement or restricted diffusion, in an expansile petrous apex lesion, indicates cholesterol granuloma from blood products. Cholesteatoma is dark on T1 and restricts diffusion, apicitis enhances, and chordoma enhances.
  33. The correct answer is D (Perineural spread along the maxillary division of the trigeminal nerve): Enlargement and enhancement of the infraorbital nerve with foraminal widening and fat loss in the pterygopalatine fossa shows perineural tumour spread. Fibrosis, sinusitis and pseudotumour do not follow a nerve or widen a foramen.
  34. The correct answer is A (Tongue deviates toward the lesion, because the canal carries the hypoglossal nerve): The anterior condylar canal is the hypoglossal canal and transmits the twelfth cranial nerve. A lower motor neuron lesion weakens the ipsilateral genioglossus, so the protruded tongue deviates toward the lesion with wasting on that side. The glossopharyngeal and vagus nerves leave through the jugular foramen, and the vagus affects the palate and uvula rather than the tongue.
  35. The correct answer is C (Spinal ependymoma, in which the cap sign reflects chronic haemosiderin deposition from repeated bleeding): Central, well-circumscribed, uniformly enhancing expansion with a haemosiderin cap and rostral syrinx is the classic adult cervical ependymoma. Astrocytoma is eccentric and infiltrative with ill-defined enhancement. Haemangioblastoma has an enhancing nodule with prominent cyst and serpentine flow voids. The myxopapillary variant is centred on the conus and filum, not the cervical cord.
  36. The correct answer is B (MRI of brain and whole spine, plus retinal, renal and adrenal imaging for further lesions): A dorsal enhancing nodule with a large cyst and flow voids in a VHL patient is a haemangioblastoma, and VHL is a multisystem disease. Staging needs the brain and whole spine for further haemangioblastomas plus retinal, renal and adrenal assessment. Neuraxis imaging alone, angiography for planning, or biochemical screening alone with deferred imaging each cover only part of the required work-up.
  37. The correct answer is B (A broad-based dural attachment with a tapering dural tail and punctate calcification on CT): Meningiomas in the thoracic canal of middle-aged women usually have a broad dural base, a dural tail and occasionally calcification, and they seldom exit the foramen. Schwannomas arise from nerve roots, so dumbbell extension, cystic change and multiplicity point towards a nerve sheath origin.
  38. The correct answer is D (Urgent surgical decompression and drainage, with blood cultures and empirical intravenous antibiotics): Discitis with an enhancing epidural collection showing restricted diffusion is a spinal epidural abscess, and new leg weakness makes it a neurosurgical emergency. Cultures and intravenous antibiotics start at once together with decompression and drainage. Conservative antibiotic treatment is reserved for patients without deficit, and extra imaging or biopsy only delays decompression.
  39. The correct answer is C (Several short lesions, each under two vertebral segments, lying peripherally in the dorsolateral cord): MS cord plaques are typically short, under two vertebral segments, eccentric and dorsolateral, occupying less than half the cross-section. NMOSD produces longitudinally extensive, central lesions, often with an axial bright spot and with area postrema or dorsal medullary involvement.
  40. The correct answer is D (MOG antibody associated disease, which commonly involves the conus and shows an H-sign): Conus involvement and an H-shaped grey matter pattern on axial T2 are recognised features of MOG antibody disease, which can mimic AQP4 disease. MS plaques are short and peripheral. Sarcoid needs subpial or central canal enhancement. Idiopathic transverse myelitis can be longitudinally extensive.
  41. The correct answer is D (Catheter spinal angiography to find the fistula level and plan embolisation or surgery): Cord oedema with dorsal perimedullary flow voids in a middle-aged man indicates a spinal dural arteriovenous fistula causing venous congestion. Spinal digital subtraction angiography identifies the feeding radicular artery and the level for treatment. Steroids can worsen the condition by increasing venous hypertension.
  42. The correct answer is B (Anterior spinal artery territory infarction, producing the owl's eyes appearance of the anterior horns): Sudden bilateral weakness with loss of pain and temperature but preserved vibration after aortic surgery points to the anterior spinal artery territory. Paired ventral grey matter hyperintensities with diffusion restriction give the owl's eyes appearance. Dural fistula and demyelinating or inflammatory myelopathies evolve over days to weeks and do not restrict diffusion in this way.
  43. The correct answer is C (Thin-section multidetector CT of the cervical spine with sagittal and coronal reformats): High-energy mechanism and midline tenderness place him in a high-risk group. CT is far more sensitive than radiographs for fracture and is the first test. MRI is added for neurological deficit or suspected ligamentous or disc injury. Flexion-extension views are unreliable and unsafe in the acute setting.
  44. The correct answer is C (MRI of the cervical spine to show cord contusion, oedema and ligament disruption): Hyperextension on a stenotic spondylotic canal produces central cord syndrome, and a CT without fracture does not exclude cord injury. MRI shows contusion, oedema, haemorrhage and disc or ligament disruption. CT angiography answers a vascular question, myelography shows only compression, and dynamic radiographs are hazardous with canal stenosis.
  45. The correct answer is D (Widening of the occipital condyle to C1 joint beyond 2 mm on coronal reformats): Atlanto-occipital dissociation is a craniocervical ligamentous injury, shown by a condyle to C1 joint space above about 2 mm, a widened basion-dens interval, and prevertebral swelling. Atlantodental widening, lateral mass overhang and C2-C3 translation each indicate injuries at lower levels.
  46. The correct answer is B (Probable transverse atlantal ligament rupture, making this a mechanically unstable injury): A two-part burst of the atlas ring is a Jefferson fracture. Combined lateral mass overhang above about 7 mm on the open-mouth view suggests transverse ligament rupture, so the injury is unstable and needs MRI and halo or surgical fixation. Collar treatment suits the stable pattern, while craniocervical dissociation and odontoid fractures have different imaging findings.
  47. The correct answer is D (Bilateral interfacetal dislocation, an unstable flexion distraction injury with high risk of cord damage): Anterolisthesis of about 50 per cent with bilateral perched or locked facets indicates bilateral facet dislocation, a flexion distraction injury with posterior ligament failure and frequent complete cord injury. Unilateral dislocation shows about 25 per cent translation. Teardrop fractures show a corner fragment, and clay-shoveller injuries are stable.
  48. The correct answer is A (Lipomyelomeningocele, a closed dysraphism with a skin-covered mass and tethered cord): The skin-covered fatty mass, posterior defect and lipoma stalk joining the cord make this a lipomyelomeningocele, a closed dysraphism that tethers the cord. Myelomeningocele is open. A dermal sinus is a tract. Caudal regression shows sacral agenesis with a blunted conus.
  49. The correct answer is D (Ultrasound of the lumbosacral spine to show conus level and cord pulsation): In infants under about three months the posterior elements are incompletely ossified, so ultrasound shows the conus level, filum and cord pulsation without radiation or sedation. MRI follows if ultrasound is abnormal or the stigmata are high risk. Waiting until six months loses the acoustic window, and radiographs do not display the cord.
  50. The correct answer is D (A bony or cartilaginous spur with a separate dural sac for each hemicord): Type I split cord malformation has an osseous or cartilaginous spur splitting the canal and two dural tubes, each with a hemicord. Type II has a single dural sac and a fibrous septum. Low conus, thick filum and lipomas can accompany either or arise separately.
  51. The correct answer is A (Beaked tectum with a small posterior fossa and downward herniation of the vermis and medulla): Chiari II is nearly always associated with myelomeningocele and shows a small posterior fossa, tectal beaking, vermian and medullary herniation, and often hydrocephalus. Chiari I has tonsillar descent only. Dandy-Walker shows an enlarged posterior fossa and absent vermis.
  52. The correct answer is B (Cine phase-contrast MRI of CSF flow at the foramen magnum, with whole-spine MRI): Tonsillar descent beyond 5 mm with Valsalva-provoked headache and a syrinx suggests symptomatic Chiari I. Phase-contrast cine MRI shows obstructed CSF flow at the foramen magnum, and whole-spine MRI defines the syrinx and associated scoliosis or tethering. Venography and angiography answer different questions.
  53. The correct answer is A (Whole-spine and craniocervical MRI to look for a syrinx or Chiari malformation): Left thoracic curve, pain, male sex, early onset and abnormal neurological signs are red flags for underlying neural axis pathology such as syringomyelia, Chiari malformation or tethered cord. MRI of the whole spine and craniocervical junction is indicated. Osteoid osteoma is possible but less likely to explain abnormal reflexes.
  54. The correct answer is B (Smooth enhancement of the labyrinthine segment, with no mass along the facial nerve): Bell palsy typically shows smooth, mild enhancement of the intratemporal facial nerve, mainly labyrinthine and geniculate segments. Nodular enhancement, foraminal enlargement, fat replacement at the stylomastoid foramen and a parotid mass indicate neoplastic perineural spread.
  55. The correct answer is B (Thin-section heavily T2-weighted 3D sequence with MR angiography through the pons): Classic trigeminal neuralgia is usually due to vascular loop compression, most often by the superior cerebellar artery, at the root entry zone. Heavily T2-weighted 3D sequences such as CISS or FIESTA show the nerve and adjacent vessel, and MR angiography confirms the artery. Contrast imaging screens for secondary causes, but routine sequences and tractography do not show the conflict.
  56. The correct answer is A (CT angiography to look for a posterior communicating artery aneurysm): A pupil-involving third nerve palsy with acute headache is an aneurysm until proven otherwise, classically the posterior communicating artery at its junction with the internal carotid. CT or catheter angiography is urgent because rupture risk is high. The alternatives do not address the compressive vascular cause.
  57. The correct answer is C (Centred on the internal auditory canal with canal widening and an acute angle to petrous bone): A vestibular schwannoma arises from the nerve within the internal auditory canal, widens the canal and meets the petrous bone at an acute angle, giving an ice cream cone shape. Meningioma sits on a broad dural base with an obtuse angle, hyperostosis and calcification. Avid enhancement is common to both and does not separate them.
  58. The correct answer is A (A discrete enhancing mass infiltrating surrounding fat, with nodular T2 hyperintense thickening): Radiation plexopathy gives diffuse, relatively uniform thickening, mild enhancement and fibrosis within the radiation field. Recurrent tumour forms a focal enhancing mass with infiltrative margins and nodular T2 bright thickening of the nerve trunks. The other findings overlap with benign post-treatment change, so a discrete mass is the strongest discriminator.
  59. The correct answer is B (Loss of the target sign with ill-defined margins, heterogeneous enhancement and surrounding oedema): In NF1, rapid growth with rest pain suggests malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour. Loss of the target sign, ill-defined margins, heterogeneous enhancement with necrosis and perilesional oedema favour malignant change. Large deep lesions are at higher risk, but size alone is less specific, and a target sign, smooth margins and split-fat sign favour a benign neurofibroma.
  60. The correct answer is C (Proceed directly to thrombectomy, adding intravenous thrombolysis if there is no contraindication): A disabling stroke with a small established core and a proximal large vessel occlusion within six hours is a clear indication for thrombectomy. Perfusion imaging is not required in the early window and MRI delays treatment. Thrombolysis should proceed if eligible, but it should not replace or delay thrombectomy.
  61. The correct answer is D (Trials show thrombectomy improves functional outcome in selected large core strokes, with more haemorrhage): Several randomised trials of large core stroke, including patients with ASPECTS 3 to 5, showed better functional outcomes with thrombectomy than medical care, with more symptomatic haemorrhage but no excess mortality. The older ASPECTS threshold of 6 no longer excludes treatment, and perfusion volume thresholds belong to late window protocols.
  62. The correct answer is A (Likely symptom onset within about 4.5 hours, supporting eligibility for intravenous thrombolysis): Diffusion restriction appears within minutes, while FLAIR hyperintensity takes several hours to develop. A DWI lesion without FLAIR change implies onset within roughly 4.5 hours and is used to select unknown-onset patients for thrombolysis. It does not show old infarction, penumbra alone, or haemorrhage.
  63. The correct answer is D (Endovascular thrombectomy, since trials show benefit in basilar occlusion at this time point): Recent randomised trials of severe basilar artery occlusion showed better functional outcomes with thrombectomy, extending beyond the early window. Without extensive brainstem infarction, mechanical clot retrieval is appropriate. Observation or lysis alone risks death or locked-in syndrome.
  64. The correct answer is A (A crescentic T1 hyperintense mural haematoma surrounding an eccentric, narrowed flow void): Methaemoglobin in a subacute intramural haematoma appears bright on fat-suppressed T1 as a crescent around a small residual lumen, usually starting a few centimetres above the bulb. Atherosclerosis causes calcified concentric thickening, and aneurysmal dilation is a different lesion.
  65. The correct answer is A (The V3 segment between the C2 transverse foramen and the foramen magnum): The V3 segment is mobile as it loops around the atlas and passes through the dura, so it is vulnerable to rotation and stretch injury. V1 is usually the site of atheroma. V2 is protected within the transverse foramina. V4 dissections are less common but carry a subarachnoid haemorrhage risk.
  66. The correct answer is C (Sixty per cent stenosis, calculated against the normal distal internal carotid diameter): The NASCET method measures 1 minus the narrowest lumen divided by the distal normal internal carotid diameter, so 1 minus 2 over 5 gives 60 per cent. The residual lumen ratio of 40 per cent is not the stenosis. ECST uses the estimated bulb diameter, which gives a higher figure.
  67. The correct answer is C (Carotid web, a fibromuscular dysplasia variant that promotes thrombus through flow stagnation): A thin shelf-like projection from the posterior wall of the carotid bulb in a young patient with cryptogenic stroke is a carotid web, a focal variant of fibromuscular dysplasia. Stagnant flow in the pocket beyond it promotes thrombus. Classic multifocal dysplasia is beaded, free thrombus floats in the lumen, and a dissection flap has a false lumen.
  68. The correct answer is A (Retrograde flow in the left vertebral artery, increasing with arm exercise): Proximal subclavian stenosis lowers distal pressure, so blood is drawn retrogradely down the ipsilateral vertebral artery to supply the arm. Reversed vertebral flow that increases with arm exercise defines subclavian steal. A dampened distal waveform, a high stenotic velocity and a mid-systolic notch indicate stenosis or an early pre-steal stage but not established steal.
  69. The correct answer is A (From the aortic arch to the vertex in a single arterial phase): Stroke CT angiography should run from the aortic arch to the vertex in one arterial phase so that tandem lesions, arch branch origins, cervical dissection and intracranial occlusion are all assessed. Starting at the bifurcation misses proximal disease. A venous phase adds dose without answering the arterial question, and multiphase intracranial protocols omit the neck.
  70. The correct answer is C (Bovine arch, with the left common carotid sharing the brachiocephalic trunk origin): When the left common carotid shares an origin with the brachiocephalic trunk, the pattern is termed a bovine arch and is found in roughly a fifth of people. A bicarotid trunk leaves the right subclavian separate, an aberrant right subclavian is a fourth branch passing behind the oesophagus, and a direct left vertebral origin is a different variant.
  71. The correct answer is D (Spinal cord sarcoidosis, where dorsal subpial and central canal enhancement forms the trident sign): Dorsal subpial enhancement with central canal extension, called the trident sign on axial images, with mediastinal nodes strongly suggests neurosarcoidosis. A dural fistula shows serpentine flow voids, MS shows short peripheral lesions, and B12 myelopathy is non-enhancing and symmetrical.
  72. The correct answer is D (Grade 2, cord compression with CSF still visible around the cord): The Bilsky scale grades epidural spinal cord compression: 1a to 1c are epidural impingement without cord compression, grade 2 is cord compression with CSF visible, and grade 3 is cord compression with no visible CSF. Grades 2 and 3 are high grade and affect decisions about surgery or radiotherapy.
  73. The correct answer is C (Cavernous malformation, with a popcorn core and haemosiderin rim from old bleeding): A reticulated popcorn core with a complete haemosiderin rim and marked blooming on gradient echo is typical of a cavernous malformation. Ependymoma can bleed and form a haemosiderin cap, but it enhances avidly and expands the cord. Metastasis and haemangioblastoma also enhance, and the latter shows flow voids and a cyst.
  74. The correct answer is D (Enhancement of over half the optic nerve length, extending into the chiasm): Optic neuritis in NMOSD is typically longitudinally extensive, more than half the nerve length, and often involves the posterior nerve and chiasm. MS usually causes short-segment enhancement. Perineural sheath enhancement is characteristic of MOG antibody disease.
  75. The correct answer is C (Dual-energy CT iodine maps, or repeat CT at 24 hours, since staining clears): Iodinated contrast can leak across a damaged blood-brain barrier and mimic haemorrhage. Dual-energy CT iodine maps or virtual non-contrast images separate iodine from blood, and staining resolves by about 24 hours. Short interval repeat CT and diffusion MRI are less discriminating.
  76. The correct answer is A (Intraplaque haemorrhage, seen as high signal on T1-weighted imaging): Intraplaque haemorrhage, bright on T1, together with a thin or ruptured fibrous cap and a large lipid core, marks a vulnerable plaque with a higher stroke risk independent of stenosis degree. Calcification and a thick fibrous cap are stable features.
  77. The correct answer is B (Category 5, highly suggestive of malignancy, so image-guided tissue sampling then surgical consultation): An irregular spiculated mass with an angular, non-parallel, shadowing sonographic correlate meets the threshold for a probability of malignancy above 95 percent, which defines category 5. Category 4C is tempting because it also prompts biopsy, but it is reserved for lesions short of classic features. Category 0 is wrong because targeted ultrasound has already completed the work-up. Category 4A with cytology under-calls the lesion and uses an inadequate sampling method.
  78. The correct answer is C (High-grade ductal carcinoma in situ with comedo-type necrosis within involved ducts): Fine linear branching calcifications in a segmental distribution represent a cast of a duct filled with necrotic debris, the classic appearance of high-grade DCIS with comedo necrosis. Sclerosing adenosis and columnar cell change give rounder, amorphous or punctate forms in lobular distribution. Secretory calcifications of duct ectasia are thick, smooth, rod-shaped and radiate toward the nipple, so they look quite different.
  79. The correct answer is C (Involuting fibroadenoma with coarse calcification, a benign finding allowing return to routine screening): Coarse popcorn-type calcification inside a long-stable circumscribed oval mass is the typical appearance of an involuting fibroadenoma, a benign category 2 finding. Papilloma and mucinous carcinoma would not be expected to remain unchanged for years with this calcification pattern. Secretory calcifications of duct ectasia are linear rods radiating toward the nipple and are not contained within a mass.
  80. The correct answer is A (Report as benign fat necrosis, assign category 2, and continue routine surveillance imaging): A fat-density oil cyst with thin eggshell calcification at the surgical site is pathognomonic of fat necrosis and needs no biopsy. Short interval follow-up is reserved for probably benign lesions, not for lesions with a diagnostic benign appearance. MRI adds nothing here, since lipid content on mammography already establishes the diagnosis.
  81. The correct answer is A (Amorphous calcifications, a suspicious morphology that warrants tissue sampling in a new cluster): Amorphous calcifications are small and hazy, so indistinct that no specific shape can be assigned, and they fall in the suspicious morphology group in BI-RADS 5th edition. Punctate calcifications are round and sharply defined. Dystrophic calcifications are coarse and irregular and follow trauma or treatment. Fine pleomorphic forms show distinct varying shapes, and even then biopsy precedes excision.
  82. The correct answer is A (Milk of calcium within microcysts, a benign finding requiring no further assessment): Calcific sediment layering within microcysts changes shape with the beam direction: round and indistinct on the craniocaudal view and a crescent or teacup on the true lateral view. This is diagnostic of milk of calcium. DCIS calcifications do not change shape between projections. Vascular and dermal calcifications have their own characteristic morphologies that are not crescentic.
  83. The correct answer is B (Thin reconstructed slices separate overlapping fibroglandular tissue and remove a summation artefact): Tomosynthesis acquires projections over an arc and reconstructs thin slices, so overlapping tissue no longer superimposes and apparent masses caused by summation disappear. The technique does not use contrast agents, and dose per view is not the mechanism. Compression is similar to conventional mammography and does not explain the resolution.
  84. The correct answer is D (Tomosynthesis-guided vacuum-assisted biopsy of the distortion, with marker clip placement): Unexplained architectural distortion without a history of trauma or surgery is suspicious, and when it is seen only on tomosynthesis, biopsy must use tomosynthesis guidance. MRI could be added but is not the required next step and may delay diagnosis. Follow-up and discharge are inappropriate for a finding that carries a meaningful risk of malignancy or radial scar.
  85. The correct answer is C (Non-parallel orientation, with the long axis perpendicular to the skin surface): Non-parallel orientation, a taller-than-wide shape, is among the most predictive sonographic signs of malignancy, because invasive tumours grow across tissue planes. Colour Doppler vascularity and elastography are adjuncts that do not alter the BI-RADS category reliably. Posterior enhancement favours a benign mass such as a fibroadenoma or cyst.
  86. The correct answer is A (Ultrasound-guided vacuum-assisted core biopsy of the nodule, with surgical referral planned): A vascular solid intraductal nodule in a woman with bloody discharge is an intraductal papilloma or papillary carcinoma until proven otherwise, and ultrasound has already shown the target for core biopsy. Cytology has poor sensitivity and cannot assess architecture. Galactography would show a filling defect but would not provide tissue.
  87. The correct answer is D (Rim enhancement, with a thick bright peripheral ring and a less enhancing centre): Rim enhancement is a suspicious internal pattern, reflecting a peripherally active tumour with a necrotic or fibrotic centre. Homogeneous enhancement is more typical of benign masses, non-enhancing dark internal septations are a classic sign of fibroadenoma, and a persistent kinetic curve is the pattern most associated with benign tissue.
  88. The correct answer is D (Segmental distribution with clustered ring or clumped internal enhancement): Segmental or linear non-mass enhancement follows a duct system, with a clumped or clustered ring internal pattern being the most specific for DCIS. Diffuse, symmetric homogeneous enhancement indicates hormonal or physiological change. Regional heterogeneous and stippled patterns are common in benign proliferative change and background parenchymal enhancement.
  89. The correct answer is C (In the second week of the cycle, between days 7 and 14): Background parenchymal enhancement is lowest in the second week of the cycle, giving the best specificity, so scanning on days 7 to 14 reduces false positives. The late luteal phase is when enhancement is highest, which is why her previous study was limited. Scanning at ovulation or during menses does not offer the same consistent suppression.
  90. The correct answer is B (Annual mammography with tomosynthesis plus annual contrast-enhanced breast MRI): A lifetime risk of 20 percent or more, as with this woman, warrants supplemental annual MRI alongside annual mammography. Average-risk biennial screening is inadequate. Ultrasound alone is less sensitive than MRI and cannot detect calcifications reliably. MRI alone misses some DCIS presenting as calcifications, which mammography detects.
  91. The correct answer is A (Discordant result, so repeat sampling or surgical excision is required): Fibrocystic change does not explain an irregular spiculated mass, so the result is discordant even though the needle appeared to pass through the lesion. The mass remains BI-RADS 5 and needs repeat sampling or excision, because the core may have missed the tumour. Short-interval follow-up or MRI first would delay diagnosis, and a concordant benign reading is incorrect.
  92. The correct answer is B (Contrast-enhanced breast MRI to assess disease extent and the contralateral breast): Invasive lobular carcinoma infiltrates in single files without forming a mass, so it is often underestimated on mammography and ultrasound. MRI most accurately shows extent, multifocality and contralateral disease. FDG PET-CT is relatively insensitive for lobular tumours, and repeating mammography or ultrasound adds little.
  93. The correct answer is B (Pure mucinous carcinoma, with tumour cells suspended in extracellular mucin pools): Mucin accounts for the marked T2 hyperintensity, and the circumscribed margin with posterior enhancement can mimic a benign lesion. Centripetal progressive enhancement and absence of septations point toward mucinous carcinoma in an older woman. Fibroadenoma is the closest mimic but typically shows dark septations, while phyllodes tumour and inclusion cyst have different demographics and architecture.
  94. The correct answer is C (Surgical excision, because of the risk of upgrade to carcinoma at final pathology): Papillary lesions containing atypia have a real risk of harbouring DCIS or invasive carcinoma that was not sampled, so surgical excision is recommended. Concordance with the imaging does not remove the upgrade risk. Repeat biopsy and imaging surveillance leave the unsampled portion of the lesion unassessed.
  95. The correct answer is B (Surgical excision, because the pleomorphic type carries a risk of upgrade and unsampled invasion): Classic LCIS is usually managed with surveillance, but the pleomorphic variant, especially with necrosis and calcification, behaves more like high-grade DCIS and carries an upgrade risk, so excision is recommended. Chemoprevention reduces risk but does not address possible unsampled invasive disease. Repeat biopsy does not resolve it.
  96. The correct answer is D (Stereotactic vacuum-assisted biopsy, with specimen radiography confirming calcifications were retrieved): Calcifications without a sonographic correlate are sampled using stereotactic or tomosynthesis guidance with a vacuum-assisted device, and specimen radiography confirms that calcifications were obtained. Ultrasound cannot reliably target such lesions, cytology gives inadequate architectural information, and MRI guidance is reserved for MRI-only findings.
  97. The correct answer is D (Place a marker clip in the tumour and in the biopsied node): If the tumour responds completely, nothing may be visible at surgery, so a marker must be placed in the tumour before treatment and ideally in the biopsied node for targeted axillary surgery. Wires cannot remain in place for months, and later marker placement is unreliable once the tumour has shrunk or disappeared.
  98. The correct answer is A (Focal eccentric cortical lobulation with local effacement of the fatty hilum): Focal eccentric cortical expansion with indentation of the fatty hilum suggests a tumour deposit within the cortex. Overall length, mild Doppler vascularity and a thin cortex over most of the node are normal or reactive findings. Only the focal asymmetric thickening is specific for metastasis.
  99. The correct answer is B (Intracapsular implant rupture, with the collapsed shell floating within the silicone gel): Multiple low-signal curvilinear lines inside the silicone that do not reach the capsule, the linguine sign, indicate a collapsed shell and therefore intracapsular rupture. Radial folds are infoldings that connect to the periphery. Free silicone outside the capsule would indicate extracapsular rupture, and capsular contracture does not produce floating shell lines.
  100. The correct answer is A (Silicone lymphadenopathy from extracapsular rupture, producing the snowstorm sign): Free silicone from extracapsular rupture produces a very echogenic area with dirty posterior shadowing, the snowstorm sign, and migrates to axillary nodes. Calcified metastatic or granulomatous nodes can shadow but lack the diffuse echogenic pattern and implant history. Intracapsular rupture stays within the capsule and does not reach the axilla.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Ultrasound-guided aspiration of the fluid, with cytology and CD30 immunohistochemistry): A late peri-implant seroma more than a year after surgery in a textured implant requires aspiration for cytology and CD30 testing to exclude breast implant-associated anaplastic large cell lymphoma. Antibiotic treatment is inappropriate without signs of infection, and MRI or mammography may follow but must not delay fluid analysis.
  2. The correct answer is B (Invasive ductal carcinoma of the male breast, the usual histological type): An eccentric, spiculated, irregular mass with nipple retraction is typical of male breast carcinoma, most often invasive ductal. Gynaecomastia is retroareolar, usually symmetric or central and merges with fat. Pseudogynaecomastia and lipoma show fat without a solid irregular mass.
  3. The correct answer is C (Gynaecomastia, which needs no biopsy and is managed by removing the cause): Bilateral symmetric tender retroareolar glandular tissue in a man on anabolic steroids is typical of gynaecomastia, with a fan or flame-shaped density merging into fat. No biopsy is needed when the pattern is classic. Carcinoma is unilateral and eccentric. Pseudogynaecomastia contains no glandular density at all.
  4. The correct answer is C (The new irregular area containing fine pleomorphic calcifications at the lumpectomy site): New suspicious calcifications or an enlarging irregular mass at the scar after three years are features favouring local recurrence. The oil cyst with rim calcification is a classic benign fat necrosis appearance, mild skin thickening is an expected radiotherapy change, and a stable scar is unchanged postoperative architecture.
  5. The correct answer is A (Intramammary lymph node with a fatty hilum, benign finding, BI-RADS category 2): A small, oval, circumscribed mass with a fatty hilum notch in the upper outer quadrant near the axillary tail is a typical intramammary lymph node and is BI-RADS 2. Fibroadenoma lacks the notch, an inclusion cyst lies in the skin and is shown by tangential views, and papillary carcinoma would not look like a typical node.
  6. The correct answer is B (Hamartoma, a benign lesion composed of mixed fat and glandular tissue): The 'breast within a breast' appearance, with a capsule enclosing fat and fibroglandular tissue, is characteristic of a hamartoma. A lipoma is purely fatty, an oil cyst is lucent with rim calcification, and phyllodes tumour is a solid mass showing cleft-like spaces and rapid growth.
  7. The correct answer is A (Surgical excision with wide margins, given suspected phyllodes tumour): Rapid growth, large size, cleft-like spaces and a cellular fibroepithelial core result raise concern for a phyllodes tumour, which cannot be reliably separated from fibroadenoma on core biopsy. Complete surgical excision with wide margins is needed. Surveillance and limited vacuum excision risk incomplete removal and local recurrence.
  8. The correct answer is D (Ultrasound-guided core biopsy of abnormal tissue with skin punch biopsy): Rapid onset of erythema with diffuse skin and trabecular thickening that fails to improve with antibiotics should prompt biopsy for inflammatory breast carcinoma. Skin punch biopsy can show dermal lymphatic invasion. Antibiotics, drainage of a nonexistent abscess or imaging without tissue only delay diagnosis.
  9. The correct answer is A (Category C, heterogeneously dense, where small masses may be hidden): Category C describes breasts in which dense tissue may obscure small masses, though areas of fat remain visible. Category B has only scattered fibroglandular areas, category D is so dense that sensitivity is lowered even more, and category A is mostly fat.
  10. The correct answer is A (Two-view mammography every two years through the national programme): BreastScreen Australia invites women aged 50 to 74 for two-view mammography every two years. Annual supplemental ultrasound or MRI is not part of average-risk population screening, and a three-year interval is longer than the programme standard.
  11. The correct answer is A (Assign category 3 with unilateral follow-up mammography at six months): A new cluster of punctate calcifications without other findings is probably benign, with a malignancy risk of 2 percent or less, so short interval follow-up is recommended. Category 2 skips the surveillance a new cluster deserves, while categories 4A and 4B over-call round punctate forms and would lead to unnecessary biopsy.
  12. The correct answer is B (Developing asymmetry, which is suspicious and warrants tissue sampling): A focal asymmetry that is new, larger or denser than on prior studies is a developing asymmetry and carries a higher risk of malignancy than a stable focal asymmetry. Because it persists on tomosynthesis, summation is excluded, and a global asymmetry involves a large volume of the breast instead.
  13. The correct answer is D (Radial scar, but sampling is required because carcinoma can coexist within the lesion): A radiolucent centre, long thin spicules and variable appearance between views favour radial scar over carcinoma, which usually has a central dense mass. Radial scars may coexist with DCIS or invasive cancer, so tissue sampling remains necessary. A surgical scar needs a relevant history.
  14. The correct answer is D (Punch biopsy of the nipple skin, plus sampling of the calcifications): Unilateral nipple eczema refractory to steroids with suspicious retroareolar calcifications suggests Paget disease of the nipple with underlying DCIS or invasive cancer. Skin biopsy confirms the diagnosis and calcification sampling assesses the underlying disease. Empirical treatment, delayed imaging and cytology alone are inadequate.
  15. The correct answer is A (Contrast-enhanced breast MRI to look for an occult primary tumour): Axillary adenocarcinoma of breast origin with negative mammography and ultrasound should be evaluated with breast MRI, which finds an occult primary in a majority of cases and guides targeted treatment. PET-CT is less sensitive for small breast cancers, CT is insensitive, and delay is inappropriate.
  16. The correct answer is A (MRI-guided biopsy of the additional mass before the surgical plan is finalised): An additional suspicious MRI lesion that could change surgery, such as converting breast conservation to mastectomy, needs histological confirmation. When second-look imaging finds no correlate, MRI-guided biopsy is the way to sample it. Ignoring, delaying or using PET-CT does not resolve it.
  17. The correct answer is B (Contrast-enhanced breast MRI, which finds the most mammographically occult cancers): In women with extremely dense breasts, supplemental MRI detects the highest number of additional cancers and reduces interval cancers, at the cost of more recalls than mammography alone. Ultrasound adds fewer cancers with more false positives, tomosynthesis helps but does not remove masking, and repeating mammography does not add sensitivity.
  18. The correct answer is C (Galactocele, a milk-retention cyst containing a layering fat component): A painless circumscribed cystic lesion in a lactating woman with a fat-fluid level on a horizontal beam view is a galactocele. An abscess is tender with a thick wall and oedema, a simple cyst is anechoic without fat, and a lipoma is solid fat without a fluid level.
  19. The correct answer is A (Repeat biopsy or surgical localisation excision, because the calcifications were not sampled): Fine pleomorphic calcifications are suspicious, and a result that does not contain the calcifications is a sampling failure, not benign concordance. The lesion remains unsampled and needs repeat biopsy or excision. Follow-up or MRI would leave a potentially malignant lesion undiagnosed.
  20. The correct answer is D (Contrast-enhanced mammography using dual-energy imaging after intravenous iodinated contrast): Contrast-enhanced mammography shows neovascularity using iodinated contrast and dual-energy subtraction, giving sensitivity close to MRI, and suits women who cannot undergo MRI. Ultrasound alone and mammography alone are less sensitive, while sedation does not solve the pacemaker problem.
  21. The correct answer is A (Complicated cyst, because uniform low-level echoes are present without any solid component): A simple cyst must be anechoic. Uniform low-level echoes with enhancement, a thin wall and no solid component or flow define a complicated cyst. Complex cystic and solid masses contain a mural nodule, thick septation or thick wall, and clustered microcysts are multiple tiny cysts with thin septa.
  22. The correct answer is A (Expected radiotherapy changes that usually improve over time, with routine surveillance continuing): Skin thickening, coarsened trabeculae and mildly increased density are expected after whole-breast radiotherapy and usually settle over months to years. Early recurrence would show a new mass, pleomorphic calcification or progressive change, so short-interval follow-up is not needed. Mastitis would be painful with an acute course, and fat necrosis is a focal scar-related lesion rather than a diffuse change.
  23. The correct answer is A (Haematogenous metastases from melanoma, which often appear as multiple circumscribed round masses): Metastases to the breast are typically round, circumscribed, dense masses that are often multiple and lack calcification or spiculation, and a known extramammary primary supports this. Primary carcinoma typically spiculates, intramammary nodes are small with a fatty hilum, and new fibroadenomas are unusual at this age.
  24. The correct answer is D (Targeted ultrasound, then ultrasound-guided core biopsy and shielded mammography if malignant): Ultrasound is the first-line test for a palpable lump in pregnancy because it uses no radiation, and it directs core biopsy if suspicious. Mammography with abdominal shielding can follow if cancer is confirmed. Gadolinium is avoided in pregnancy and PET-CT exposes the fetus to radiation.
  25. The correct answer is A (Secretory calcifications of duct ectasia, benign, BI-RADS category 2): Smooth, thick, rod-like calcifications that radiate toward the nipple are secretory calcifications of duct ectasia and are benign. Fine linear branching forms are thin, irregular and discontinuous, vascular calcification runs parallel tracks along vessels, and coarse heterogeneous calcifications are irregular and not rod-like.
  26. The correct answer is A (Tubal ectopic pregnancy presenting as a tubal ring adjacent to the left ovary): An extra-ovarian adnexal ring that slides independently of the ovary, with an empty uterus and a raised hCG, is a tubal ectopic until proven otherwise. A corpus luteum is tempting because it also has a vascular rim, but it moves with the ovary. Complete miscarriage would not leave a rising hCG with free fluid. Hydrosalpinx has a thin wall and incomplete folds rather than a thick echogenic ring.
  27. The correct answer is D (Pregnancy of unknown location, with repeat hCG at 48 hours and clinical review): When no intrauterine or extra-uterine pregnancy is seen in a stable woman with a positive test, the correct label is pregnancy of unknown location. The hCG trend over 48 hours separates a growing intrauterine pregnancy, a failing pregnancy and an ectopic. Surgery without a visualised ectopic is premature, and a complete miscarriage requires documented hCG decline or a prior intrauterine sac.
  28. The correct answer is C (Diagnostic of a failed pregnancy because the embryo exceeds 7 mm without a heartbeat): A crown-rump length of 7 mm or more without cardiac activity is a diagnostic criterion for failed pregnancy in current consensus guidance. Cardiac activity is normally visible well before the embryo reaches that size. A repeat scan is reserved for findings that are only suspicious, and hCG trends are not needed to confirm an ultrasound-diagnosed failure.
  29. The correct answer is C (Anembryonic pregnancy, diagnosed because the sac exceeds 25 mm without an embryo): A mean sac diameter of 25 mm or more with no embryo is diagnostic of an anembryonic pregnancy, so waiting for a repeat scan is unnecessary. A pseudosac is small, central and lacks a true thickened decidual rim. Incomplete miscarriage shows irregular endometrial tissue rather than an intact round sac, and a repeat scan in a week applies only to sacs below the diagnostic threshold.
  30. The correct answer is B (Eleven days after the first scan, when the sac with a yolk sac is unchanged): Consensus criteria give 14 days for a sac without a yolk sac and 11 days for a sac with a yolk sac when no embryo with a heartbeat develops. The 14 day interval is tempting but belongs to the first situation. Seven days or a 48 hour hCG check is not diagnostic and could cause a wrongly terminated viable pregnancy.
  31. The correct answer is B (Redate using the crown-rump length, since the discrepancy exceeds seven days at this gestation): Crown-rump length is the most accurate dating parameter between 9 weeks and 13 weeks 6 days, and a difference of more than seven days from uncertain menstrual dates justifies redating. A wider threshold applies only later in pregnancy, where head circumference is used and the allowed difference rises to 10 days from 16 weeks and 14 days from 22 weeks. Repeating the measurement adds nothing because crown-rump length is already reliable at this size.
  32. The correct answer is D (Raised nuchal translucency, warranting counselling and offer of diagnostic or cell-free DNA testing): A nuchal translucency of 3.5 mm or more is raised and carries increased risk of aneuploidy, structural anomaly and genetic syndromes. The technique described is correct, so repeating the scan is unnecessary. Structural imaging is needed but does not replace genetic counselling and the offer of diagnostic or cell-free DNA testing. Combined risk calculation helps screening but a markedly raised measurement already justifies offering testing.
  33. The correct answer is A (Chromosomal abnormality such as monosomy X, with a high chance of hydrops and fetal loss): A large septated nuchal cystic hygroma with hydrops in the first trimester is strongly associated with aneuploidy, especially Turner syndrome, and carries a poor prognosis. Neural tube defects present with intracranial signs, and diaphragmatic hernia is shown by bowel or stomach in the chest. Isolated lymphatic malformation is a diagnosis of exclusion after karyotyping.
  34. The correct answer is C (Severe early-onset growth restriction with advanced compromise, needing urgent senior review of delivery timing): Reversed end-diastolic flow in the umbilical artery, brain-sparing and an absent ductus venosus a-wave indicate severe placental insufficiency with advanced compromise. Surveillance alone suits earlier Doppler stages. Late-onset restriction occurs after 32 weeks with normal umbilical flow. A chromosomal cause may be considered, but delivery timing cannot wait for karyotyping.
  35. The correct answer is C (Twin-twin transfusion syndrome, Quintero stage I with a stuck donor twin): Oligohydramnios with a maximum vertical pocket under 2 cm in one sac and polyhydramnios over 8 cm in the other, in a monochorionic pair, defines twin-twin transfusion syndrome. Stage I is present because the donor bladder is still visible and Doppler is normal. Selective growth restriction has no fluid discordance of this degree, and anaemia polycythaemia is defined by Doppler velocities.
  36.
