The correct answer is B (Dichorionic diamniotic, supported by the lambda sign at the membrane insertion): A triangular wedge of chorionic tissue at the membrane base is the lambda or twin-peak sign and indicates dichorionic diamniotic twins, even when the two placentas appear fused as one mass. The T sign has a thin membrane meeting the placenta at a right angle and indicates monochorionic twins. Chorionicity is reliably assigned at 11 to 14 weeks and should not be left until later.
  37. The correct answer is A (Umbilical cord entanglement causing sudden intrauterine death of one or both fetuses): Monoamniotic twins share one sac, and the freely moving cords entangle, which is the dominant cause of unexpected fetal death. The sacs are not separate, so fluid discordance does not define the problem. Conjoined twins are fused, which is excluded by two separate bodies. Cord entanglement is recognised by intermingled cords and two different heart rates on Doppler.
  38. The correct answer is A (Transvaginal ultrasound with an emptied bladder to measure the distance to the internal os): When the placental edge is not seen clearly, transvaginal ultrasound with an empty bladder is safe, accurate and defines the distance to the internal os. A fuller bladder obscures the lower segment and can mimic a low placenta. A 32 week scan is the usual follow-up only after the edge has been defined. MRI is reserved for suspected invasion, not for routine localisation.
  39. The correct answer is B (Interruption of the bladder wall with bridging vessels crossing the uterine serosa and bladder): Lacunae, loss of the clear zone and thinning of the myometrium already suggest accreta spectrum disease. Interruption of the bladder wall with bridging vessels crossing the serosa and bladder indicates invasion beyond the myometrium, or percreta, with high haemorrhage risk. Lacunae, subplacental hypervascularity and placental bulge all occur in accreta without demonstrating extension beyond the uterus.
  40. The correct answer is D (Dark intraplacental T2 bands, heterogeneous placental signal and focal uterine bulging): Dark intraplacental bands on T2, heterogeneous signal, uterine bulging and tenting of the bladder are the main MRI features of accreta spectrum. A homogeneous placenta with an intact myometrium is normal. Retroplacental high T1 signal suggests haemorrhage, and fibroids can mimic a mass but are distinguished by a low T2 capsule and no placental interdigitation.
  41. The correct answer is A (Fetal exsanguination at membrane rupture, so planned caesarean delivery before labour): Unprotected fetal vessels crossing the internal os are torn when membranes rupture, which can cause rapid fetal exsanguination. Planned caesarean birth before labour, usually at 34 to 36 weeks, is the standard plan. Abruption is a maternal bleed from placental separation, and cord prolapse is a mechanical event. Fetomaternal haemorrhage would be slow and surveillable, unlike this acute risk.
  42. The correct answer is B (Gastroschisis, with a low chance of chromosomal abnormality but a risk of bowel atresia): Free-floating bowel to the right of a normal cord insertion, without a covering membrane, defines gastroschisis. It carries a low risk of aneuploidy but risks of bowel atresia and growth restriction. Omphalocele has a covering membrane, may contain liver and has a higher association with aneuploidy and cardiac anomalies.
  43. The correct answer is D (Liver herniated into the chest together with a low observed to expected lung-to-head ratio): Intrathoracic liver together with a low observed to expected lung-to-head ratio indicates severe pulmonary hypoplasia and poor survival, and it guides consideration of fetal endoscopic tracheal occlusion. Stomach herniation with a good ratio is a weaker predictor. Polyhydramnios is common but does not measure lung volume, and bowel-only herniation is less severe.
  44. The correct answer is D (Fetal brain MRI at about 28 to 32 weeks for associated cortical or callosal anomalies): Mild to moderate ventriculomegaly (10 to 15 mm) needs MRI to detect associated findings such as callosal agenesis, polymicrogyria or haemorrhage, because these change prognosis. Fetal CT is avoided because of radiation. Middle cerebral artery Doppler assesses anaemia and does not explain ventricular enlargement.
  45. The correct answer is B (It uses no ionising radiation, and gadolinium is avoided in standard protocols): Fetal MRI uses magnetic fields and radiofrequency pulses, not ionising radiation, and gadolinium crosses the placenta so it is not used in standard fetal protocols. Both 1.5 and 3 tesla scanners are acceptable. Fast single shot sequences allow imaging from the second trimester without sedation.
  46. The correct answer is B (Open spina bifida with Chiari II malformation features): A scalloped frontal bone (lemon sign), curved cerebellar hemispheres (banana sign) and an obliterated cisterna magna form the cranial signature of open spinal dysraphism with Chiari II malformation. Dandy-Walker has an enlarged posterior fossa. Aqueductal stenosis has a normal posterior fossa, and rhombencephalosynapsis fuses the hemispheres without a small head and obliterated cisterna.
  47. The correct answer is D (Dandy-Walker malformation, distinguished by vermian hypoplasia and rotation with a high torcular): Dandy-Walker malformation combines a hypoplastic, rotated vermis, a cystic dilatation of the fourth ventricle and an elevated torcular. Mega cisterna magna has a normal vermis and fourth ventricle. Arachnoid cysts compress without communicating, and Blake pouch cysts have a normal vermis but display hydrocephalus and a typical tentorial position.
  48. The correct answer is C (Septate uterus, which can be treated by hysteroscopic septum resection): A septate uterus has a normal or minimally indented outer fundus, with a septum dividing the cavity, and responds to hysteroscopic resection. A bicornuate uterus has a deep fundal cleft, usually over 1 cm, and muscle in the divider. Didelphys has two cervices, and a unicornuate uterus has a single curved cavity.
  49. The correct answer is A (Uterus didelphys with obstructed hemivagina and ipsilateral renal agenesis): Two widely separated horns, each with its own endometrium and cervix, define uterus didelphys. A vaginal septum with obstruction of one side and ipsilateral renal agenesis completes the OHVIRA association. Mullerian agenesis lacks two cervices, a septate uterus has a single outer contour, and a rudimentary horn does not have its own cervix.
  50. The correct answer is D (Renal anomalies, most often unilateral renal agenesis, with vertebral anomalies): Mayer-Rokitansky-Kuster-Hauser syndrome has normal ovaries and secondary sexual characteristics with an absent uterus and upper vagina. Renal anomalies, most often unilateral agenesis, and vertebral anomalies are the common associations. Turner features go with streak gonads, retained Y material needs a 46,XY karyotype, and polycystic ovarian morphology is not part of this syndrome.
  51. The correct answer is A (Schedule soon after menstruation ends, once pregnancy and active pelvic infection are excluded): Hysterosalpingography is performed after menstruation ends and before ovulation, so pregnancy is excluded, the endometrium is thin and menstrual debris does not mimic polyps. Active pelvic infection and pregnancy are contraindications. Scheduling near ovulation risks irradiating an early conception, and menstruation produces confusing filling defects. A negative test and antibiotics do not replace correct timing.
  52. The correct answer is B (Right hydrosalpinx caused by distal tubal occlusion at the ampulla): A tube dilated distally with pooling of contrast and no peritoneal spill is a hydrosalpinx from distal occlusion, which reduces the success of in vitro fertilisation and may need salpingectomy. Spasm affects the cornu and resolves. Isthmic nodosa shows small diverticula, and sterilisation clips cause a sharp proximal cut-off.
  53. The correct answer is C (Red (carneous) degeneration of a fibroid, typical during pregnancy): Haemorrhagic infarction produces a T1 hyperintense rim from methaemoglobin with non-enhancing tissue, typical of red degeneration in pregnancy and sudden pain. Cystic degeneration is bright on T2 without T1 signal. Sarcoma is rare and has irregular enhancing solid tissue with restricted diffusion, while adenomyoma has ill-defined margins.
  54. The correct answer is B (Subendometrial linear striations with myometrial cysts and no distinct rounded margin): Subendometrial echogenic striations, myometrial cysts and ill-defined borders favour adenomyosis. Globular enlargement and heterogeneous echotexture occur in both adenomyosis and fibroids, so they do not discriminate. Rim shadowing from discrete nodules and circumferential vascularity around a mass are fibroid features.
  55. The correct answer is A (Junctional zone thickness greater than 12 mm on T2-weighted images): A junctional zone thickness greater than 12 mm on T2-weighted MRI is highly specific for adenomyosis, especially with bright foci. Values of 8 to 11 mm are indeterminate and 8 mm or less is normal. Endometrial thickness changes with the cycle, and uterine volume is nonspecific because fibroids also enlarge the uterus.
  56. The correct answer is B (Endometrioma, a benign lesion consistent with O-RADS US category 2): A unilocular ovarian cyst with homogeneous ground-glass content and no vascular solid component is characteristic of an endometrioma. O-RADS US places a classic endometrioma under 10 cm in category 2, almost certainly benign. Teratomas show echogenic, shadowing material, and simple cysts are anechoic.
  57. The correct answer is C (Endometrioma, whose persistence and T2 shading favour it over a haemorrhagic functional cyst): T1 high signal persisting after fat suppression indicates blood products, and T2 shading reflects iron-rich concentrated blood, both typical of endometrioma. A haemorrhagic functional cyst should shrink within weeks. Teratoma fat signal drops on fat suppression, and mucinous tumours are multilocular with variable locules.
  58. The correct answer is A (Absent sliding sign with a nodule extending into the muscularis propria of the rectal wall): An absent sliding sign shows pouch of Douglas obliteration, and a nodule invading the muscularis propria of the rectum predicts bowel involvement that may need shaving, disc excision or resection. Obliteration with a serosal nodule suggests adhesions but not wall invasion. Kissing ovaries imply adhesions, and a retrocervical nodule with preserved sliding is less extensive disease.
  59. The correct answer is C (Reassurance with advice to re-present if bleeding recurs, as cancer risk is low): An endometrial thickness of 4 mm or less in a woman with a single episode of postmenopausal bleeding carries a very low risk of cancer, and reassurance with advice to re-present if bleeding recurs is accepted practice. Hysteroscopy, biopsy or sonohysterography is indicated for thicker or irregular endometrium or persistent bleeding. Repeat scanning in six weeks adds nothing.
  60. The correct answer is B (Endometrial polyp, indicated by a single feeding vessel on Doppler): A focal echogenic lesion with a single feeding vessel, particularly on early follicular phase imaging, is typical for an endometrial polyp. Fibroids show circumferential flow and are of low echogenicity, and hyperplasia is diffuse. Retained products are expected only after pregnancy or termination and show disordered flow.
  61. The correct answer is C (Mature cystic teratoma, typical dermoid under 10 cm, O-RADS US category 2): A hyperechoic mural nodule with shadowing, fine echogenic lines and fat or calcification on CT is a mature teratoma. A typical dermoid under 10 cm is O-RADS US 2, and category 3 applies at 10 cm or more. Immature teratomas have a large solid component, and a haemorrhagic cyst has no fat on CT.
  62. The correct answer is D (No follow-up needed, as this is a classic haemorrhagic cyst under 5 cm): A classic haemorrhagic cyst has a lacy network, a retracting clot with concave margins and no flow. O-RADS US places it in category 2, and guidance suggests that no follow-up is needed for premenopausal women when under 5 cm. Short interval follow-up applies to larger lesions. Surgery and oncology referral are not justified.
  63. The correct answer is B (O-RADS US 3, as a simple cyst of 10 cm or more, with follow-up): A simple unilocular cyst under 10 cm is O-RADS US 2, but one of 10 cm or more moves to category 3, a low risk lesion. Management is follow-up or MRI characterisation and consideration of the clinical context. Categories 4 and 5 need solid components or ascites.
  64. The correct answer is B (O-RADS US 5, with oncology referral and CT staging of the chest, abdomen and pelvis): Four or more papillary projections, ascites and peritoneal nodularity place this lesion in O-RADS US 5, with a high likelihood of malignancy. The plan is gynaecological oncology referral and CT staging. Category 4 would suit MRI problem solving. Cystectomy by a non-oncologist risks spillage, and percutaneous biopsy risks tumour seeding.
  65. The correct answer is B (Score 2, almost certainly benign, typical of fibrothecoma): Solid tissue that is dark on T2 and dark on high b-value diffusion imaging, as in fibrothecoma, is O-RADS MRI score 2. Scores 3 to 5 depend on solid tissue enhancement relative to the myometrium and curve type. The mild enhancement below the myometrium supports a benign tissue.
  66. The correct answer is A (Torsion remains likely, since preserved Doppler flow does not exclude it): A large ovary with peripheral follicles, stromal oedema and free fluid in an adolescent with sudden pain is torsion until proven otherwise. The ovary has a dual blood supply, so Doppler flow is often preserved in early or partial torsion, and this does not exclude it. A ruptured cyst would not produce uniform stromal oedema with peripheral follicles, and an abscess needs fever and a complex adnexal mass. Surgical review should not wait on flow.
  67. The correct answer is B (Meets the criterion of 20 or more follicles per ovary or volume of 10 mL): International PCOS guidance accepts polycystic ovary morphology as at least 20 follicles per ovary (2 to 9 mm) using a high frequency probe, or an ovarian volume of 10 mL or more. It is used for diagnosis in adults, with the other features of hyperandrogenism and ovulatory dysfunction.
  68. The correct answer is C (Stage IB2, as the intact stromal ring excludes parametrial invasion): An intact low-signal stromal ring on T2 imaging has a high negative predictive value for parametrial invasion. Under FIGO 2018, a tumour of 2 cm to under 4 cm confined to the cervix is IB2, and 4 cm or more is IB3. The 3.5 cm size therefore gives IB2, and the stage IB3 option is tempting only if the size threshold is misremembered.
  69. The correct answer is C (Stage IIA2, as the upper vagina is involved without parametrial spread): Tumour reaching the upper two-thirds of the vagina without parametrial invasion is stage IIA, and a size over 4 cm makes it IIA2. Lower third involvement is IIIA. Stage IVA needs bladder or rectal mucosal invasion, and IB describes disease limited to the cervix.
  70. The correct answer is D (Invasion of less than half of the myometrium, consistent with stage IA): Tumour that breaches the subendometrial enhancement line but leaves a thicker preserved outer myometrial rim invades less than half of the wall, which is stage IA. Stage IB needs invasion of half or more, which the preserved outer rim argues against. Dynamic contrast imaging is the best sequence to judge depth. The cervix and adnexa are normal, so stage II and stage IIIA are not supported.
  71. The correct answer is D (Stage II, due to cervical stromal invasion by the endometrial tumour): Intermediate signal replacing the low-signal cervical stromal ring means tumour has invaded the cervical stroma, which is stage II. Cavity distension does not prove deep myometrial invasion, and stage I applies only when the cervix is not invaded. A thin intact outer stromal rim argues against parametrial extension. The serosa is intact, the bladder wall is normal and no nodes are enlarged.
  72. The correct answer is A (Complete hydatidiform mole with bilateral theca lutein cysts): A grape-like mass without a fetus, markedly raised hCG, hyperemesis and bilateral theca lutein cysts is a complete mole. A partial mole usually has a fetus or fetal parts with a thickened cystic placenta and triploidy. Hydropic miscarriages seldom reach such hCG levels.
  73. The correct answer is D (Gestational trophoblastic neoplasia with myometrial invasion and lung metastases, needing chemotherapy): A plateauing or rising hCG after molar evacuation, an invasive hypervascular myometrial mass and lung metastases meet the criteria for gestational trophoblastic neoplasia. Management is chemotherapy, with FIGO risk scoring. Further curettage and embolisation would not treat metastatic disease and may cause haemorrhage.
  74. The correct answer is D (Caesarean scar pregnancy, a form of ectopic pregnancy with a risk of rupture): A sac implanted within the anterior lower segment scar with a thin myometrium toward the bladder and high-velocity flow is a caesarean scar pregnancy. A cervical ectopic has a sac below the internal os and an expanded cervix. Inevitable miscarriage would show a descending sac within the canal and no vascular implantation.
  75. The correct answer is A (Sac fixed in place with peritrophoblastic flow, and a negative sliding sign): A cervical ectopic is implanted in the canal wall, so the sac stays fixed under probe pressure and shows peritrophoblastic flow. A miscarriage in progress slides or deforms with pressure, lacks surrounding vascularity and often shows an open os with debris. A low intrauterine sac that moves with a Valsalva manoeuvre is also a miscarriage sign, not an ectopic one.
  76. The correct answer is C (Interstitial ectopic pregnancy with imminent rupture and haemoperitoneum): An eccentric sac in the uterine cornu with a thin surrounding myometrium of under 5 mm and an interstitial line is an interstitial ectopic, which can rupture with catastrophic bleeding. Angular pregnancies sit within a normal myometrial mantle. A rudimentary or bicornuate horn has a visible cavity.
  77. The correct answer is D (Heterotopic pregnancy, with a viable intrauterine pregnancy and a coexisting tubal ectopic): Assisted conception raises the chance of coexisting intrauterine and ectopic pregnancy, so a separate extra-ovarian ring with free fluid after a live intrauterine pregnancy is a tubal ectopic until proven otherwise. A corpus luteum lies within the ovary and has a vascular rim. Ovarian hyperstimulation enlarges both ovaries, and hydrosalpinx is tubular with incomplete folds. Prompt treatment protects the intrauterine pregnancy.
  78. The correct answer is D (Tubo-ovarian abscess arising from pelvic inflammatory disease in this woman): A thick-walled complex adnexal mass with a dilated tube showing incomplete septa, debris and echogenic fat in a febrile woman with discharge points to tubo-ovarian abscess. Cystadenocarcinoma has papillary tissue without systemic infection signs. Ectopic pregnancy is excluded without positive hCG.
  79. The correct answer is C (Offer cervical cerclage, as length is under 25 mm before 24 weeks): A cervical length under 25 mm before 24 weeks with funnelling and a previous spontaneous preterm birth supports cerclage. Surveillance alone waits for further shortening, and steroids are reserved for threatened delivery nearer viability and are not given to an asymptomatic woman at 22 weeks. Amniocentesis is considered with a dilated cervix or signs of infection, which she does not have.
  80. The correct answer is A (Retained products of conception with a vascular trophoblastic remnant): An echogenic endometrial focus with internal flow and a feeding vessel after pregnancy indicates retained products. A clot lacks vascularity and a polyp is unlikely so soon after a procedure. An arteriovenous malformation is a myometrial vascular tangle, not an intracavitary mass.
  81. The correct answer is B (It does not exclude abruption, as ultrasound has low sensitivity for placental abruption): Abruption is a clinical diagnosis, and ultrasound detects only a minority of cases because fresh blood can be isoechoic with the placenta. A normal scan therefore does not exclude it and must not delay delivery when the fetal heart rate is abnormal and the uterus is rigid. Vasa praevia usually causes painless bleeding, and rupture would be suspected from a scar and other findings.
  82. The correct answer is D (Surfactant deficiency causing diffuse alveolar collapse in a premature lung): Low volumes, a uniform ground-glass granularity and air bronchograms in a preterm infant without steroid cover indicate hyaline membrane disease. Transient tachypnoea gives normal or increased volumes with perihilar streaking and settles quickly. Meconium aspiration produces coarse asymmetric change with hyperinflation in a term or post-term baby. Group B streptococcal pneumonia can look identical, but the gestation, absent maternal sepsis features and low volumes favour surfactant deficiency.
  83. The correct answer is A (Delayed clearance of fetal lung fluid, expected to resolve within two days): Elective caesarean delivery skips the labour-driven absorption of lung fluid, giving streaky perihilar opacities, fissural fluid and well-expanded lungs that clear within 24 to 72 hours. Surfactant deficiency would show low volumes and air bronchograms in a preterm baby. Lobar overinflation is unilateral and progressive. Pneumonia would show consolidation and a clinically unwell infant.
  84. The correct answer is A (Air leak from air trapping behind partially obstructed small airways): Meconium causes ball-valve obstruction of small airways, so positive pressure ventilation readily produces pneumothorax, seen as an abrupt hypertransradiant hemithorax. Pulmonary hypertension and pulmonary haemorrhage can cause sudden desaturation but do not produce a lucent hemithorax. Tube migration causes collapse of the opposite lung rather than a lucent one.
  85. The correct answer is C (Chronic lung disease of prematurity with fibrosis and emphysematous change): Oxygen dependence at 36 weeks with a coarse reticular and bubbly pattern is the established form of bronchopulmonary dysplasia. Interstitial emphysema occurs in the first days and shows linear radiating lucencies. Surfactant deficiency presents in the first hours with low volumes. Aspiration produces dependent focal consolidation rather than diffuse coarse change.
  86. The correct answer is B (Nasogastric tube tip projecting above the diaphragm within the left chest): A nasogastric tube tip lying in the left chest proves that the stomach has herniated through the diaphragm, which separates hernia from a congenital airway malformation. Mediastinal shift and contralateral hypoplasia occur with both lesions. Thin-walled cysts of varying size in one lung point to an airway malformation instead.
  87. The correct answer is B (Complete duodenal obstruction from atresia, associated with trisomy 21): Two bubbles with a gasless distal bowel in an infant with trisomy 21 and polyhydramnios indicate duodenal atresia. Volvulus is a close mimic but usually shows some distal gas and a different clinical course. Jejunal atresia gives a triple bubble pattern. Meconium ileus shows a bubbly mass-like pattern with microcolon.
  88. The correct answer is C (Immediate surgical referral because ischaemic bowel makes the situation time critical): A reversed vessel relationship with a whirlpool pattern in an infant with bilious vomiting and signs of ischaemia is midgut volvulus, and bowel can infarct within hours. Further contrast imaging, CT or observation only delays a Ladd procedure. Contrast study is the usual test in a stable baby, but this infant needs theatre without delay.
  89. The correct answer is D (Free intraperitoneal gas indicating perforation of a necrotic bowel wall): Pneumoperitoneum, best seen on a cross-table or decubitus view, is the clearest indication for laparotomy or drain placement. Portal venous gas, a fixed loop and widespread pneumatosis all signal severe disease and may prompt closer review, but they are relative rather than definitive triggers.
  90. The correct answer is A (Muscle thickness of 3 mm or more with an elongated canal): Ultrasound criteria for hypertrophic pyloric stenosis are muscle thickness of 3 mm or more and a canal length over about 15 mm, with failure of the channel to open. Values near 1.5 mm are normal. Antral thickening alone does not make the diagnosis, and small diameters with a short canal fall below the thresholds.
  91. The correct answer is D (Free peritoneal gas with signs of peritonitis suggesting perforation of bowel): Perforation or peritonitis is a contraindication because enema pressure risks further contamination, and the child needs surgery. Longer symptoms, a possible lead point and small bowel dilatation lower the chance of success or raise risk, but a careful attempt after resuscitation is still reasonable.
  92. The correct answer is A (A pathological lead point such as lymphoma or a Meckel diverticulum): Intussusception beyond infancy and outside the ileocolic region more often has a pathological lead point, particularly lymphoma, polyp, duplication cyst or Meckel diverticulum. Lymphoid hyperplasia and idiopathic telescoping explain most cases under two years. Enlarged nodes alone rarely cause a true intussusception.
  93. The correct answer is D (Rectosigmoid index below one with a transition zone in the distal colon): In Hirschsprung disease the aganglionic segment is narrow, so the rectum is not wider than the sigmoid, giving an inverted rectosigmoid ratio with a cone-shaped transition and delayed evacuation. Microcolon and ileal pellets indicate meconium ileus. A meconium plug is a transient functional problem, and dilated ileum with a normal colon suggests a proximal obstruction.
  94. The correct answer is C (Cystic fibrosis, with genetic testing for the chloride channel gene): Thick inspissated meconium filling the ileum with an unused microcolon is the hallmark of meconium ileus, which is the earliest presentation of cystic fibrosis in most cases. Hypothyroidism and Hirschsprung disease can delay stool but do not produce a microcolon. Trisomy 21 relates to duodenal atresia and Hirschsprung disease, not this pattern.
  95. The correct answer is C (Persistent lucency and volume of the dependent lung without the expected deflation): A normal dependent lung loses volume and becomes denser when lying on its side. If it stays lucent and expanded, the bronchus is partly obstructed by a ball-valve foreign body. Complete obstruction would produce collapse instead. Bilateral peribronchial thickening suggests viral airway disease.
  96. The correct answer is D (Treat as pneumonia with clinical review and follow-up radiograph if symptoms persist): A smooth spherical consolidation with fever and a raised white count in a young child is classically round pneumonia, because immature collateral air pathways limit spread. Treatment is antibiotics with clinical review and a follow-up film if symptoms persist. Urgent CT, bronchoscopy or biopsy is unnecessary without features of tumour or inhalation.
  97. The correct answer is A (Swollen epiglottis and aryepiglottic folds with a distended hypopharynx): Swelling of the epiglottis and aryepiglottic folds with a distended hypopharynx on the lateral view identifies supraglottic inflammation. Subglottic narrowing is the steeple sign of croup. An irregular, membranous tracheal wall suggests bacterial tracheitis, and prevertebral thickening with gas suggests retropharyngeal abscess.
  98. The correct answer is B (Grade III, with mild to moderate dilatation of the collecting system): Grade III reflux fills the ureter and pelvis with mild to moderate dilatation and minimal blunting of fornices while the ureter stays straight. Grade II lacks dilatation. Grade IV adds moderate dilatation with tortuosity and loss of sharp fornix angles but preserved papillary impressions. Grade V shows gross dilatation and loss of papillae.
  99. The correct answer is A (Split function below 40 percent with prolonged drainage despite furosemide): Reduced differential function on the affected side with prolonged tracer retention after diuretic indicates significant obstruction at the pelviureteric junction and supports pyeloplasty, particularly if function falls on follow-up. Prompt washout and equal split function indicate a non-obstructed system suitable for observation. Stasis at the lower ureter points to a megaureter.
  100. The correct answer is A (Dilated elongated posterior urethra with abrupt calibre change and thickened bladder neck): Posterior urethral valves show a dilated, elongated posterior urethra above an abrupt narrowing, with a thickened bladder neck and trabeculated bladder. A wide posterior urethra without an obstructing lesion suggests prune belly syndrome. Anterior valves dilate the bulbar urethra, and reflux with a normal urethra does not confirm outlet obstruction.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Stretched renal parenchyma wrapping the mass, with displaced rather than encased vessels): Wilms tumour arises within the kidney, stretching normal parenchyma around it as a claw sign and displacing rather than encasing vessels. Necrosis and haemorrhage occur in both tumours. Stippled calcification, vascular encasement, raised catecholamine metabolites and MIBG uptake all favour neuroblastoma.
  2. The correct answer is D (Neoadjuvant chemotherapy followed by nephron-sparing surgery after response assessment): For bilateral Wilms tumour the aim is cure with preserved renal reserve, so chemotherapy is given first to shrink the lesions and surgery is nephron sparing. Bilateral radical nephrectomy would leave the child anephric. Initial radiotherapy is not standard and risks long-term renal damage. A unilateral nephrectomy ignores the contralateral tumour.
  3. The correct answer is A (MIBG scintigraphy, taken up by catecholamine-producing neural crest tumour cells): Neuroblastoma arises from sympathetic neural crest cells and concentrates metaiodobenzylguanidine, so MIBG maps soft tissue, marrow and skeletal disease with high specificity. A bone scan misses marrow and soft tissue deposits. Octreotide imaging is a second-line option, and gallium has no specific role.
  4. The correct answer is D (Cross-table lateral projection with the hip left in neutral rotation): A cross-table lateral shows posterior displacement without moving the limb, which matters because an unstable slip may displace further with abduction. The frog-leg view carries that risk in an acute slip. Judet views assess the acetabulum, and ultrasound shows effusion but not slip angle.
  5. The correct answer is C (Lateral pillar height loss greater than half of the original height): The Herring lateral pillar classification predicts outcome: collapse of more than half of the lateral pillar height (group C) has the worst prognosis. A crescent sign marks the subchondral fracture in early disease. Preserved pillar height (group A) and younger age at onset carry better outcomes.
  6. The correct answer is D (Graf type D, a decentring hip requiring early treatment): An alpha angle of 43 to 49 degrees places the hip in the dysplastic range, and a beta angle above 77 degrees with early displacement identifies type D, a decentring hip. Type IIc has the same alpha range but a beta angle below 77 degrees. Type IIa is a physiological variant at a younger age. Type III has a dislocated head with alpha below 43 degrees.
  7. The correct answer is B (At about six weeks of age, when physiological laxity has resolved): Early scans overcall dysplasia because maternal hormones loosen the ligaments, so screening for a breech girl with a normal examination is best at about six weeks. By about four months the femoral head begins to ossify and ultrasound becomes less reliable. Scanning at birth or two weeks risks false positives.
  8. The correct answer is B (Widened, irregular physis with fraying and cupping of the distal femoral metaphysis): Failed mineralisation at the growth plate gives a widened physis with a frayed, cupped metaphysis, most obvious at the knees and wrists. A dense provisional zone with a ringed epiphysis suggests scurvy. Corner fractures suggest abuse, and lucent metaphyseal bands suggest leukaemia or systemic illness.
  9. The correct answer is D (MRI with fat-suppressed sequences and intravenous gadolinium contrast): Radiographs lag the disease by 7 to 14 days, so a normal film does not exclude infection. MRI detects marrow oedema and subperiosteal or soft tissue abscess early and guides drainage. CT is poorer for marrow and uses radiation. Ultrasound can show subperiosteal collections but is inadequate alone.
  10. The correct answer is C (Posteromedial rib fractures near the costovertebral junctions in an infant): Posterior rib fractures near the costovertebral junctions arise from forceful anteroposterior chest squeezing and are highly specific for abuse in infants. A femoral fracture in a non-mobile infant and a complex skull fracture raise concern but have more accidental explanations, and clavicle fractures are common birth or fall injuries.
  11. The correct answer is C (Repeat limited survey at 11 to 14 days to demonstrate healing fractures): Healing callus makes subtle fractures visible after about two weeks, so a limited repeat survey at 11 to 14 days improves detection. At 48 hours callus has not formed, and by eight weeks many injuries have remodelled. Bone scintigraphy is not a substitute for radiographic follow-up.
  12. The correct answer is B (Interhemispheric subdural haemorrhage, diffuse hypoxic-ischaemic change and retinal haemorrhages): Interhemispheric subdural blood, diffuse hypoxic-ischaemic injury and retinal haemorrhage together are the characteristic combination of abusive head trauma in an infant. Bilateral collections without retinal haemorrhage fit benign enlargement or chronic effusions. A fracture with a thin subdural fits a short fall, and diffuse oedema alone is non-specific.
  13. The correct answer is A (Restricted diffusion in the basal ganglia, thalami and perirolandic cortex): Acute profound insults injure the metabolically active deep grey nuclei, the ventrolateral thalami and the perirolandic cortex. Prolonged partial hypoperfusion instead damages the watershed zones. Germinal matrix haemorrhage and cystic leukomalacia are patterns of prematurity.
  14. The correct answer is D (Grade III, intraventricular haemorrhage with ventricular dilatation present): Blood within a distended ventricle without parenchymal involvement is grade III on the Papile scheme. Grade I is limited to the germinal matrix. Grade II has intraventricular blood without enlargement. Grade IV requires echogenic haemorrhagic infarction in the adjacent white matter.
  15. The correct answer is B (Small posterior fossa with tonsillar and vermian herniation through the foramen magnum): Open myelomeningocele leaks cerebrospinal fluid in utero, leaving a small posterior fossa with caudal herniation of the tonsils and vermis through the foramen magnum, which is Chiari II. Chiari I has isolated tonsillar descent without vermian herniation. Dandy-Walker has an enlarged fossa, and the molar tooth sign marks Joubert syndrome.
  16. The correct answer is D (Conus ending at the L4 level with a thickened filum terminale): After infancy the conus normally ends at about L1 to L2, and a filum thicker than about 2 mm is abnormal. A low conus with a fatty or thickened filum fixes the cord and explains the neurological deterioration. The other findings do not demonstrate tethering.
  17. The correct answer is D (Ultrasound of the lumbosacral spine assessing conus position and cord motion): In infants younger than about three months the posterior elements are unossified, so ultrasound shows the conus level, filum and cord pulsation without radiation or sedation. MRI is reserved for abnormal ultrasound or older infants. Radiographs and CT do not show the neural structures adequately.
  18. The correct answer is C (Homogeneously hyperdense solid mass with marked restricted diffusion and little calcification): Medulloblastoma is densely cellular, so it is hyperdense on CT with strong diffusion restriction. Ependymoma often calcifies and squeezes out through the foramina. A cystic lesion with a mural nodule suggests pilocytic astrocytoma, and a bulky pons enveloping the basilar artery suggests a diffuse pontine glioma.
  19. The correct answer is D (Diffuse midline glioma of the pons, diagnosable from typical MRI features): Rapid cranial neuropathies with a diffusely infiltrative, non-enhancing expanded pons are characteristic of diffuse midline glioma. Pilocytic tumours are focal, cystic and enhance. Medulloblastoma arises in the fourth ventricle roof. Acute disseminated encephalomyelitis gives multifocal lesions rather than a single expanded pons.
  20. The correct answer is D (CT of the orbits, owing to raised risk of radiation-induced second cancers): Children with germline RB1 mutations are highly susceptible to radiation-induced second malignancies, so CT of the orbits is avoided where MRI can answer the question. Anaesthetic and sedation risks are real but are accepted because MRI gives radiation-free staging of the optic nerve and brain. Gadolinium is used when needed for nerve invasion and trilateral disease, so it does not justify skipping MRI.
  21. The correct answer is D (Greater radiosensitivity of developing tissues and longer life expectancy for malignancy to arise): Rapidly dividing tissues are more radiosensitive and children have decades in which a radiation-induced cancer can manifest, so risk per unit dose is greater. Paediatric protocols use lower exposure, and scatter or apron efficiency are not the basis of the higher lifetime risk.
  22. The correct answer is C (Lower tube current and kilovoltage scaled to patient size, with a single phase): Dose should reflect size and clinical question: reduced tube current and often lower kilovoltage, with a single contrast phase unless more are essential. Retaining adult parameters or adding unenhanced and multiphase acquisitions increases dose without improving most diagnoses in children.
  23. The correct answer is D (Not routinely recommended, as shields may obscure anatomy and add little benefit): Modern guidance holds that gonad shields placed in the field can hide pathology and trigger repeat exposures, and internal scatter limits their protective effect while gonad dose from modern equipment is low. Sex-based or age-based shielding policies belong to older practice. Poor collimation is corrected by technique rather than by a shield, so routine shielding is no longer advised.
  24. The correct answer is A (Type IV, crossing metaphysis, physis and epiphysis with joint involvement): A fracture line involving the metaphysis, physis and epiphysis is type IV. Type III stays within epiphysis and physis, so it fails on the metaphyseal fragment, and type II spares the epiphysis, so it fails on the joint surface involvement. Type V is a crush injury with no visible fracture line. Type IV needs anatomical reduction because of the growth arrest and joint risk.
  25. The correct answer is A (Elevated posterior fat pad visible on the lateral projection): Normally the posterior fat pad is hidden in the olecranon fossa. Haemarthrosis lifts it into view, which implies an intra-articular fracture even when no break can be seen. The alignment lines described and a small anterior pad are normal.
  26. The correct answer is C (Removable splint for about three weeks without routine repeat radiographs): Torus fractures are stable impaction injuries that heal quickly. A removable splint with analgesia and an early return to activity is sufficient and avoids repeat imaging. Reduction, long plaster and pinning are unnecessary.
  27. The correct answer is D (Metaphyseal location with ossified tumour matrix and sunburst periosteal reaction): Osteosarcoma typically arises in the metaphysis around the knee and produces osteoid, seen as dense matrix and spiculated sunburst reaction with Codman triangles. Ewing sarcoma is more diaphyseal with onion-skin periosteal reaction. A sclerotic-rimmed lesion without periosteal response is benign.
  28. The correct answer is A (Langerhans cell histiocytosis with an eosinophilic granuloma of bone): A punched-out lytic skull lesion with a bevelled edge from uneven destruction of the inner and outer tables is classic for eosinophilic granuloma. Epidermoid cysts have sclerotic margins, neuroblastoma metastases are permeative with sutural widening, and osteomyelitis would present with fever and a rim of reactive bone.
  29. The correct answer is C (Hepatoblastoma, the commonest primary hepatic malignancy of early childhood): Very high alpha-fetoprotein with a solitary heterogeneous calcified liver mass in a toddler without liver disease indicates hepatoblastoma. Undifferentiated embryonal sarcoma affects older children, is cystic and has a normal marker. Fibrolamellar carcinoma occurs in adolescents and young adults. Metastatic neuroblastoma is multifocal with an adrenal or paraspinal primary.
  30. The correct answer is D (Recognise a normal thymus and avoid any further imaging): The infant thymus is large, soft and moulds around adjacent structures, giving a sail or wave contour without displacing the trachea or vessels. With no mass effect, no attributable symptoms and a classic shape, no further imaging is warranted. Ultrasound and follow-up films are reasonable only when the appearance is atypical, and MRI belongs to a tumour work-up that is not indicated here.
  31. The correct answer is A (Congenital lobar overinflation of the left upper lobe): Progressive overinflation of one lobe, most often the left upper lobe, with stretched vessels and mass effect, is characteristic. Sequestration has an aortic feeder, and an airway malformation is cystic. A tension pneumothorax lacks vessels and shows lung collapse toward the hilum.
  32. The correct answer is B (Narrowed posterior nasal cavity with a thickened vomer and medial pterygoid plates): Choanal atresia produces obstruction at the posterior nasal aperture, with a narrowed airway, thickened vomer and medial bowing of the pterygoid plates, and an obstructing bony or membranous plate. A cyst of the lacrimal duct or a midline glioma lies elsewhere.
  33. The correct answer is C (Non-compressible blind-ending tubular structure with outer diameter above 6 mm): A fixed, non-compressible blind-ending tube wider than about 6 mm, often with hyperaemic wall and surrounding inflamed fat, is diagnostic. A compressible appendix of normal calibre excludes appendicitis, and adenitis or terminal ileal thickening point to alternative causes of pain.
  34. The correct answer is C (Gas within the stomach and bowel despite the blind-ending proximal pouch): Gas reaches the stomach and bowel through a fistula between the trachea and distal oesophagus, so an air-filled abdomen with a coiled tube indicates the common type C variant. A gasless abdomen fits atresia without a distal fistula. Duodenal atresia and vertebral anomalies are recognised associations, but neither shows that a distal fistula is present.
  35. The correct answer is B (Echogenic periportal fibrous tissue anterior to the portal vein bifurcation): The triangular cord sign, an echogenic fibrous remnant of the extrahepatic duct anterior to the portal vein, is highly specific. A dilated duct suggests a choledochal cyst, normal contractility argues against atresia, and coarse hepatomegaly with periportal halo is nonspecific and fits hepatitis.
  36. The correct answer is A (Cystic periventricular leukomalacia, associated with later spastic diplegia): Multiple cysts in periventricular white matter, developing a few weeks after an hypoperfusion injury, indicate cystic leukomalacia, which damages corticospinal fibres to the legs and leads to spastic diplegia. Haemorrhagic infarction is echogenic initially, and subependymal pseudocysts lie at the caudothalamic groove.
  37. The correct answer is B (Avascular mass that shrinks and becomes cystic on follow-up ultrasound): Haemorrhage has no internal flow, liquefies and involutes over weeks. Neuroblastoma is vascular, may calcify, encase vessels, and grows rather than shrinks. Raised catecholamines or metastases confirm tumour.
  38. The correct answer is A (Type II endoleak from retrograde flow through lumbar and mesenteric branches): Contrast that appears only on delayed imaging near patent branch vessels, with a stable sac, is the typical type II pattern from retrograde branch filling. A type I leak would show arterial phase contrast at an attachment site and needs prompt treatment. A type III leak arises at a fabric defect or junction and also fills early. Type IV porosity leak is a brief finding at deployment and resolves.
  39. The correct answer is C (Balloon moulding of the proximal seal zone, then a proximal cuff if persistent): A type I leak at the proximal attachment site exposes the sac to systemic pressure and carries rupture risk, so it is treated at the time of detection. Balloon moulding improves apposition, and a proximal cuff or stent extends the seal if the leak persists. Observation suits many type II leaks, not this one. Branch embolisation and sac puncture do not fix a failed seal.
  40. The correct answer is A (Endotension without a demonstrable leak, sometimes termed type V): Sac growth with no leak on appropriate multiphase imaging is endotension, classified as type V. Slow type II flow is a reasonable concern but multiphase CT and contrast enhanced ultrasound argue against it here. A type III leak would be seen as early contrast at a defect. Infection needs fever, gas, or perigraft soft tissue, none of which is described.
  41. The correct answer is D (Hepatic encephalopathy from diversion of portal blood, treated medically first): New encephalopathy after TIPS reflects diversion of portal blood past the liver and is the commonest significant complication. High velocities in a patent shunt do not indicate stenosis, and stenosis would present with bleeding or ascites rather than a flapping tremor. Wernicke encephalopathy causes ophthalmoplegia and ataxia, and unchanged liver function argues against hypoxic injury. Treatment is lactulose and rifaximin, with shunt reduction if refractory.
  42. The correct answer is C (Pulmonary hypertension with right heart failure, worsened by shunting): TIPS increases venous return to the right heart, so severe pulmonary hypertension and right heart failure are the main reasons to decline. Refractory ascites is an accepted indication once paracentesis fails. Cirrhosis is the usual setting for TIPS. Advancing age raises encephalopathy risk but is a relative consideration rather than a decisive barrier.
  43. The correct answer is D (Urgent drainage of the dominant infected ducts, with minimal contrast injection): Sepsis with an obstructed biliary system requires prompt decompression, and drainage should be limited to the infected ducts. Overfilling undrained segments with contrast raises pressure and can worsen bacteraemia. Antibiotics alone do not clear an obstructed system. Definitive stenting is deferred until sepsis settles and staging is complete.
  44. The correct answer is A (Urgent nephrostomy or retrograde stent decompression with intravenous antibiotics): An infected obstructed kidney is a urological emergency, and decompression is required with antibiotic cover. Either nephrostomy or retrograde stenting is accepted. Oral therapy is inadequate in the presence of pus under pressure. Definitive stone removal is performed after sepsis has resolved.
  45. The correct answer is B (Withhold warfarin and proceed once the INR has fallen below 1.8): For high risk procedures such as solid organ biopsy, society guidance accepts an INR up to 1.8 after warfarin is withheld. The threshold of 3.0 applies to low risk procedures. Plasma carries volume and transfusion risks and is kept for urgent cases. Plugging reduces track bleeding but does not replace correction.
  46. The correct answer is C (Heat sink effect from adjacent flowing blood cooling the ablation zone): Flowing blood in a large adjacent vessel carries heat away, leaving residual tumour at that margin, which is the heat sink effect. Desiccation and carbonisation limit energy spread in general but do not account for failure localised to the vessel. Pad burns are a skin complication, not a cause of incomplete ablation.
  47. The correct answer is D (Plan retrieval, as the indication has resolved and risks rise with dwell time): Retrievable filters are intended for a temporary period when anticoagulation is contraindicated. Once therapeutic anticoagulation is established, the filter should be removed because thrombosis, fracture and caval penetration increase with dwell time. Leaving it in with surveillance or delaying retrieval accepts that risk without benefit, and waiting for thrombus ignores that complications are often silent.
  48. The correct answer is D (Reposition the tip to the lower SVC or cavoatrial junction before use): A catheter tip in the internal jugular vein is malpositioned and raises the risk of thrombosis and extravasation, so it should be repositioned to the lower SVC or cavoatrial junction. A brachiocephalic tip and a right atrial tip are no longer the preferred position for chemotherapy. Repositioning with a wire or snare is usually feasible, so a new line in the other arm is unnecessary.
  49. The correct answer is C (Falsely high index from calcified vessels; measure toe pressures or waveforms): An index above 1.3 suggests noncompressible, calcified arteries, common in diabetes, so it cannot exclude disease. Toe pressures and Doppler waveforms are not affected by medial calcification. A normal range is roughly 0.9 to 1.3. Severe ischaemia gives a low index, and venous disease does not raise it.
  50. The correct answer is A (May-Thurner syndrome with iliac vein compression predisposing to left sided thrombosis): Compression of the left common iliac vein by the overlying right common iliac artery is May-Thurner syndrome, which favours left iliofemoral thrombosis, especially with oestrogen exposure. Nutcracker involves the left renal vein. Popliteal entrapment concerns the artery or vein behind the knee. Pelvic congestion presents with pain and ovarian vein reflux.
  51. The correct answer is A (Intramuscular adrenaline 0.5 mg of 1 in 1000 into the thigh): Stridor with hypotension indicates anaphylaxis, where intramuscular adrenaline is the immediate treatment, given into the anterolateral thigh. Steroids and antihistamines are adjuncts that do not reverse airway and circulatory compromise. Salbutamol treats bronchospasm, not upper airway oedema or shock.
  52. The correct answer is B (Proceed because the study is urgent, with isotonic saline volume expansion): For an urgent diagnosis the benefit outweighs the risk, and prophylaxis with isotonic saline is the evidence based measure in patients with eGFR below 30. N-acetylcysteine lacks reliable benefit. Furosemide induced diuresis can cause volume depletion and harm. Delay in suspected mesenteric ischaemia would be dangerous.
  53. The correct answer is D (A macrocyclic group II agent at the lowest diagnostic dose): Nephrogenic systemic fibrosis is linked mostly to group I linear agents in severe renal impairment. Group II macrocyclic agents carry very low risk and are used at the lowest effective dose. Reducing the dose of a group I agent does not remove risk. Dialysis after injection is not a reliable protection, and standard use of any agent is inappropriate.
  54. The correct answer is D (Avoid gadolinium unless essential and discuss alternatives with the referrer): Gadolinium crosses the placenta and is not recommended in pregnancy unless the information is essential and cannot be obtained otherwise, as fetal effects remain uncertain. Reduced dosing does not eliminate exposure. Organogenesis is largely complete by 20 weeks, so timing is not the argument. Alternative imaging and multidisciplinary discussion are preferred.
  55. The correct answer is B (The dose is well below the level at which deterministic malformation risk arises): Deterministic effects such as malformation are not expected below about 100 mGy, and 20 mGy is well under this. There remains a small possible increase in childhood cancer risk, which is stochastic and has no safe zero threshold. Fetal MRI is not needed to exclude radiation injury at this dose. Counselling should be supported by a medical physics dose estimate.
  56. The correct answer is C (Deterministic skin injury with a dose threshold, localised to the beam entry site): A localised injury at the entry site, appearing weeks after prolonged fluoroscopy, is a deterministic effect with a dose threshold, with transient erythema from about 2 Gy. Stochastic effects have no threshold and do not cause tissue reactions. Recall needs prior radiotherapy. Pad dermatitis would follow the pad outline.
  57. The correct answer is D (Neurofibromatosis type 1 with plexiform neurofibroma and probable optic pathway glioma): Cafe au lait macules, axillary freckling, a plexiform neurofibroma with the target sign and an optic pathway glioma are features of neurofibromatosis type 1. Type 2 features bilateral vestibular schwannomas and meningiomas. Tuberous sclerosis has hypomelanotic macules and cortical tubers. McCune-Albright has irregular cafe au lait macules and fibrous dysplasia.
  58. The correct answer is A (Neurofibromatosis type 2 with bilateral schwannomas, meningiomas and spinal ependymoma): Bilateral vestibular schwannomas are diagnostic of neurofibromatosis type 2, and meningiomas and spinal ependymomas are typical associations. Type 1 features gliomas and plexiform neurofibromas. VHL has haemangioblastomas and endolymphatic sac tumours that can mimic internal auditory canal lesions on one side. Sarcoidosis causes leptomeningeal enhancement but not bilateral schwannomas.
  59. The correct answer is B (Subependymal giant cell astrocytoma, a tumour typical of this syndrome): Growth, enhancement and hydrocephalus in a subependymal nodule near the foramen of Monro in tuberous sclerosis indicate a subependymal giant cell astrocytoma. Fourth ventricular ependymoma is in a different location. Central neurocytoma attaches to the septum pellucidum and is not syndromic. A colloid cyst is non-enhancing and not growing from a nodule.
  60. The correct answer is B (Von Hippel-Lindau disease with haemangioblastoma and renal carcinoma): A cerebellar cyst with an enhancing mural nodule is the classic haemangioblastoma, and with pancreatic cysts and clear cell renal carcinoma it indicates von Hippel-Lindau. Angiomyolipomas contain fat and belong to tuberous sclerosis. Birt-Hogg-Dube has lung cysts and chromophobe or hybrid tumours. NF1 does not produce haemangioblastomas.
  61. The correct answer is A (Aortic root dilatation at the sinuses of Valsalva with risk of dissection): Dural ectasia and upward lens ectopia in a tall patient suggest Marfan syndrome, whose key cardiovascular lesion is aortic root dilatation with dissection risk. Bicuspid valve and coarctation are associated with Turner syndrome. Pulmonary artery dilatation can occur in Marfan syndrome but is not the lesion that threatens life. Non-compaction is not a typical association.
  62. The correct answer is B (Conservative management with blood pressure control and surveillance imaging): In vascular Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, arterial tissue is fragile, and invasive procedures carry high rates of tear and bleeding. A stable, contained dissection without extravasation is managed conservatively with blood pressure control and imaging follow up. Intervention is reserved for rupture or instability. Drainage of a contained haematoma risks rebleeding.
  63. The correct answer is D (Sarcoidosis, Scadding stage 1, with isolated nodal enlargement): Symmetric bilateral hilar and right paratracheal nodes with erythema nodosum make Lofgren syndrome and sarcoidosis likely, and node enlargement alone on the radiograph is stage 1. Perilymphatic nodules favour the upper zones. Lymphoma tends to be asymmetric, tuberculosis typically unilateral, and silicosis has upper zone nodules with eggshell calcification.
  64. The correct answer is D (Deauville score 3, interpreted as complete metabolic response): Uptake above mediastinum but not above liver is Deauville 3, which Lugano criteria interpret as complete metabolic response in standard treatment. Scores 4 or 5 reflect uptake moderately or markedly above liver and indicate partial response or progression depending on change. Score 2 means uptake at or below mediastinum, and stable disease is not the category here.
  65. The correct answer is B (Stage III, as nodal disease on both sides of the diaphragm includes the spleen): Nodal disease above the diaphragm with splenic involvement is stage III, since the spleen is counted as nodal tissue in Lugano staging. Stage IV would need diffuse or disseminated extranodal organ disease such as liver or marrow. Stage II requires involvement on one side of the diaphragm. Stage I is a single node group.
  66. The correct answer is D (Type 1 autoimmune pancreatitis as part of IgG4 related disease): Diffuse sausage shaped enlargement with a capsule-like rim, a smooth biliary stricture and high IgG4 indicate type 1 autoimmune pancreatitis. Adenocarcinoma typically gives a focal mass with upstream duct dilatation. Acute pancreatitis presents with pain and inflammatory stranding. Groove pancreatitis is localised to the pancreaticoduodenal groove.
  67. The correct answer is C (Medial ureteric deviation, an undisplaced aorta and no nodal mass): Idiopathic retroperitoneal fibrosis encases the aorta and ureters, usually pulling the ureters medially, and does not displace the aorta anteriorly. Malignant disease often displaces structures laterally or anteriorly and shows bulky nodes. Metastases on staging point to malignancy.
  68. The correct answer is A (Takayasu arteritis with active large vessel wall inflammation): Young women with arm pressure asymmetry and concentric, smooth wall thickening of the aorta and its branches have Takayasu arteritis. Giant cell arteritis typically occurs after 50 years. Fibromuscular dysplasia produces beading and spares wall thickening. Atherosclerosis gives eccentric calcified plaque in older patients.
  69. The correct answer is A (Granulomatosis with polyangiitis with upper airway and pulmonary involvement): Sinus disease, cavitating lung nodules, subglottic stenosis and renal impairment with cytoplasmic ANCA indicate granulomatosis with polyangiitis. Eosinophilic granulomatosis requires asthma and eosinophilia. Goodpasture disease causes alveolar haemorrhage without cavitating nodules. Septic emboli need an infective source.
  70. The correct answer is D (Cardiac amyloidosis with diffuse extracellular matrix expansion of the myocardium): Thick walls, low ECG voltage and global subendocardial enhancement with abnormal nulling are classic for cardiac amyloidosis. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy typically shows patchy enhancement at insertion points. Fabry disease enhances the basal inferolateral mid wall. Myocarditis shows subepicardial enhancement.
  71. The correct answer is D (Physiological brown adipose tissue uptake, reduced by warming before injection): Symmetrical FDG uptake in fat attenuation tissue in cold conditions in a young thin patient is brown adipose tissue. Warming the patient and pre-scan measures reduce it. Lymphoma would show soft tissue nodes. Muscle uptake corresponds to muscle attenuation. Infection gives nodal enlargement.
  72. The correct answer is C (Lepidic adenocarcinoma with low cellular density may show low FDG avidity): Lepidic predominant adenocarcinoma and ground glass nodules have low cell density and often low FDG uptake, so a negative PET does not exclude malignancy. A 2 cm nodule is well above PET spatial resolution, and no timing error or steroid use is stated. The fasting glucose is acceptable. Follow-up CT is needed.
  73. The correct answer is D (Delay until about 12 weeks to limit false positive inflammatory uptake): Radiation and chemotherapy cause inflammatory uptake that mimics tumour for weeks, so response PET is typically delayed about 12 weeks after chemoradiotherapy. Waiting 12 months is unnecessary and delays management. Uptake above liver is not a complete response.
  74. The correct answer is C (Blunt traumatic aortic injury, graded by the Society for Vascular Surgery): A flap or irregular contour at the isthmus with mediastinal haematoma after deceleration is blunt traumatic aortic injury. Spontaneous type A dissection begins in the ascending aorta. Penetrating ulcers occur in atherosclerotic disease. A ductus diverticulum is smooth without a flap or haematoma.
  75. The correct answer is A (Contained vascular injury such as a pseudoaneurysm, with washout later): A round focus that matches the aorta in arterial phase and washes out on portal phase is a contained vascular injury such as a pseudoaneurysm. Active extravasation increases in size and often remains or spreads on delayed phase. A cleft is a fat attenuation indentation without contrast. Normal arteries are linear and branching.
  76. The correct answer is D (Splenic artery angioembolisation with monitoring in a high dependency setting): In a haemodynamically stable patient with a high grade injury and a contrast blush, non-operative management with angioembolisation is appropriate and improves splenic salvage. Surgery is for instability. A blush indicates active bleeding or pseudoaneurysm and warrants intervention. Drainage does not control arterial bleeding.
  77. The correct answer is C (Pelvic angiography with embolisation of the bleeding internal iliac branches): Ongoing haemodynamic instability with arterial pelvic bleeding that persists despite binding and transfusion requires angioembolisation or pre-peritoneal packing. Repeat imaging delays treatment. Draining a tamponading haematoma worsens bleeding. Fluid alone will not control arterial haemorrhage.
  78. The correct answer is B (Bronchial artery embolisation, avoiding the anterior spinal artery origin): Massive haemoptysis in bronchiectasis usually arises from hypertrophied bronchial arteries at systemic pressure, and embolisation is first line. Spinal artery origin from the intercostobronchial trunk must be identified to avoid cord ischaemia. Surgery is a later option, balloon tamponade is a temporising measure, and nebulised tranexamic acid helps mild bleeding only.
  79. The correct answer is A (Right portal vein embolisation to induce left lobe hypertrophy): A remnant under about 20 to 25 percent in normal liver is considered insufficient, and portal vein embolisation of the side to be resected diverts flow and induces hypertrophy of the remnant. Arterial chemoembolisation does not induce hypertrophy. Embolising the remnant side would shrink it. A 15 percent remnant is accepted only in selected cases and not as a routine safe margin.
  80. The correct answer is B (Quantify lung shunting and detect extrahepatic deposition of particles): Technetium macroaggregated albumin mimics the microspheres, so imaging quantifies shunting to the lungs and shows unintended deposition in gastrointestinal arteries, which would cause radiation pneumonitis or ulceration. It does not diagnose hepatopulmonary syndrome, measure liver reserve or assess response. Coiling of the gastroduodenal artery is sometimes needed afterwards.
  81. The correct answer is B (Thoracic endovascular repair to cover the entry tear and restore true lumen perfusion): Type B dissection complicated by malperfusion is treated urgently, usually with thoracic endovascular repair covering the entry tear to expand the true lumen. Medical therapy alone is for uncomplicated disease. Ascending aortic replacement is for type A dissection. Waiting three months would risk organ loss.
  82. The correct answer is D (Ultrasound guided thrombin injection into the sac with monitoring of distal flow): A pseudoaneurysm with a narrow neck is well suited to thrombin injection under ultrasound guidance, which achieves thrombosis in most cases. Compression takes much longer than a couple of minutes and is uncomfortable. Surgery is reserved for failures or wide necks. Pseudoaneurysms over about 2 cm with symptoms are not safe to observe.
  83. The correct answer is D (Use a different iodinated agent with premedication and resuscitation available): A prior reaction raises the risk of a further reaction, and options include a different agent, premedication with corticosteroid and antihistamine per local policy, and readiness for treatment. Skin testing does not reliably predict contrast reactions. A slower rate or fluids do not prevent recurrence, and prophylactic adrenaline infusion is not standard.
  84. The correct answer is D (Continue metformin without interruption, as the risk is low at this renal function): Current guidance holds metformin around intravenous contrast only when eGFR is below 30 or there is acute kidney injury. At an eGFR of 55 with no injury, it can be continued. Permanent cessation is unwarranted. Diabetes is not itself a barrier to contrast.
  85. The correct answer is C (Place the tube beneath the table and step further from the patient): Scatter is highest on the entrance side of the patient, so an under table tube places the operator on the lower scatter side, and the inverse square law means greater distance cuts dose sharply. Continuous fluoroscopy increases total exposure, and a distant intensifier raises entrance dose and scatter. A lateral projection with the tube on the operator side gives the highest scatter.
  86. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary arteriovenous malformation in HHT, treated with coil embolisation): A nodule with a feeding artery and draining vein in a patient with epistaxis and a family history suggests hereditary haemorrhagic telangiectasia with a pulmonary arteriovenous malformation. The right to left shunt explains the paradoxical cerebral abscess. Embolisation with coils or plugs is first line, so lobectomy is excessive. A varix needs cardiac disease, and Behcet aneurysms present with haemoptysis.
  87. The correct answer is A (Lymphangioleiomyomatosis associated with tuberous sclerosis and renal angiomyolipoma): Diffuse uniform thin walled cysts in a woman with pneumothorax and a fat containing renal mass indicate lymphangioleiomyomatosis, which occurs in tuberous sclerosis. Langerhans cell histiocytosis produces irregular cysts and nodules in smokers. Lymphocytic interstitial pneumonia has ground glass opacity. Birt-Hogg-Dube cysts are basal and subpleural.
  88. The correct answer is B (Urgent SVC stent placement for airway compromise, then tissue diagnosis and treatment): SVC obstruction with stridor is an emergency, and stenting gives rapid symptom relief, with tissue diagnosis obtained afterwards. Empirical radiotherapy before biopsy can compromise histology. Anticoagulation alone does not relieve a malignant obstruction. Waiting for histology delays relief of the airway.
  89. The correct answer is D (Image the whole spine, because noncontiguous metastatic deposits are common): Noncontiguous epidural or vertebral disease occurs in a substantial proportion of patients, so the entire spine should be imaged and treatment planned accordingly. Imaging only the symptomatic or adjacent levels risks missing a second site. CT myelography is invasive and is reserved for patients who cannot have MRI.
  90. The correct answer is A (Draped aorta sign, with the posterior wall unseparated from the vertebral body): The draped aorta sign, where the posterior wall drapes over the vertebral body without a fat plane, indicates contained rupture. The hyperattenuating crescent sign in mural thrombus is a sign of impending rupture, and a hypoattenuating crescent is not. Smooth calcified walls and a stable diameter do not point to rupture.
  91. The correct answer is C (Repeat ultrasound at about 12 months with cardiovascular risk management): Aneurysms below 5.5 cm in men are under surveillance, and the interval for 3.0 to 4.4 cm is generally annual (some guidance uses 12 months for 3.0 to 4.4). Repair is considered at 5.5 cm, or with rapid growth or symptoms. A 5 year interval is too long at this size, and planning for repair is premature.
  92. The correct answer is C (Post-embolisation syndrome, managed with analgesia, antiemetics and observation): Pain, low grade fever and nausea within the first days after embolisation are typical of post-embolisation syndrome and settle with supportive care. Hysterectomy is reserved for necrosis or sepsis with supporting findings. Fever beyond several days, purulent discharge or gas would raise infection.
  93. The correct answer is C (Chronic limb threatening ischaemia, needing urgent revascularisation planning): Rest pain, tissue loss and a toe pressure of about 20 mmHg define chronic limb threatening ischaemia, which requires early revascularisation with imaging to plan intervention. Claudication has no rest pain or ulcer. Acute limb ischaemia has sudden onset. Venous ulcers sit near the malleoli with normal pulses.
  94. The correct answer is B (Endovenous thermal ablation of the refluxing trunk with local anaesthesia): Truncal saphenous reflux with symptoms is first treated by endovenous thermal ablation, which has outcomes at least equal to surgery with faster recovery. Stripping is now second line. Compression is an alternative for patients unsuitable for intervention. Sclerotherapy targets superficial veins, not the deep femoral vein.
  95. The correct answer is C (Reactive marrow and splenic uptake from colony stimulating factor therapy): Growth factor therapy stimulates marrow and spleen, causing diffuse, symmetrical uptake that can mimic marrow involvement. This is reactive and usually resolves. Focal lesions or unexplained uptake in a patient not given growth factor would raise concern. Steatosis and sickle cell disease do not fit.
  96. The correct answer is D (Hepatic arteriography and embolisation of a track pseudoaneurysm): Pulsatile bloody output after biliary drainage suggests arterial injury along the track, commonly a hepatic artery pseudoaneurysm, and angiography with embolisation is the definitive treatment. Capping and waiting risks further bleeding, and upsizing may tamponade temporarily but does not treat the artery. A portal vein to bile duct fistula causes non-pulsatile bleeding.
  97. The correct answer is D (Cardiac sarcoidosis with granulomatous infiltration and myocardial scarring): Patchy mid wall and subepicardial enhancement that does not follow a coronary territory, in a patient with systemic sarcoidosis and ventricular arrhythmia, indicates cardiac sarcoidosis. Infarction enhances from the subendocardium in a vascular distribution. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy enhances at insertion points. Amyloid is diffusely subendocardial.
  98. The correct answer is A (Small amounts can be retained, though no clinical harm has been established): Trace gadolinium deposition in structures such as the dentate nucleus is documented, especially with linear agents, but no clinical adverse effect has been proven. Nephrogenic systemic fibrosis is a distinct renal complication. Clearance is not complete. Macrocyclic agents show less retention, and imaging should be used when justified.
  99. The correct answer is C (Proceed without stopping warfarin, as the INR is acceptable for low risk): Low risk procedures such as superficial fine needle aspiration can be performed with the INR up to about 3.0, so warfarin need not be interrupted. Vitamin K reversal and bridging expose a mechanical valve patient to unnecessary thromboembolic risk. Anticoagulation is not an absolute contraindication.
  100. The correct answer is D (Tumour derived parathyroid hormone related peptide, causing humoral hypercalcaemia): Humoral hypercalcaemia of malignancy from tumour-secreted parathyroid hormone related peptide is the commonest mechanism, notably in squamous cell lung cancer. Local osteolysis contributes in some patients but is less common. A primary parathyroid adenoma is unrelated. Calcitriol excess occurs in lymphoma and sarcoidosis.

Case Reporting Paper 1: Model Reports

Case 1: Short (3 marks)

History: 58 year old man, 3 weeks of cough and intermittent haemoptysis, 40 pack year smoking history.

Findings

  • Loss of the right heart border with a triangular opacity and fissural displacement towards the collapsed segment, with a preserved right hemidiaphragm outline.

Diagnosis

Right middle lobe collapse (silhouette sign), with a central obstructing endobronchial lesion such as bronchial carcinoma to be excluded.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Right middle lobe collapse
  • RML collapse
  • Right middle lobe atelectasis

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Right middle lobe collapse (2)
  • Loss of right heart border with a triangular opacity and fissural displacement (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the opacity right lower lobe collapse despite the preserved hemidiaphragm outline
  • Reporting right middle lobe consolidation without noting volume loss
  • Failing to suggest an obstructing central lesion in a smoker

Case 2: Short (3 marks)

History: 34 year old man, 2 weeks of progressive dyspnoea and dry cough, exertional desaturation. Recently diagnosed HIV infection, CD4 count 45.

Findings

  • Bilateral symmetrical perihilar interstitial and ground-glass opacities with peripheral sparing, normal volumes, no effusion or lymphadenopathy.

Diagnosis

Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) in an immunosuppressed patient.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia
  • PJP
  • Pneumocystis pneumonia
  • PCP

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (2)
  • Bilateral perihilar ground-glass or interstitial opacities with peripheral sparing and no effusion or nodes (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing pulmonary oedema without noting normal heart size and no effusion
  • Diagnosing miliary tuberculosis from a granular pattern
  • Omitting the immunocompromised context from the diagnosis

Case 3: Short (3 marks)

History: 19 year old man with asthma, acute central chest pain and neck swelling after a severe coughing episode.

Findings

  • Mediastinal and cervical gas outlining the aortic knuckle, with the continuous diaphragm sign and no pneumothorax.

Diagnosis

Pneumomediastinum with cervical subcutaneous emphysema, probably spontaneous (secondary to alveolar rupture), with oesophageal perforation to be considered.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Pneumomediastinum
  • Spontaneous pneumomediastinum
  • Mediastinal emphysema

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Pneumomediastinum (2)
  • Continuous diaphragm sign or gas outlining mediastinal structures, extending to the neck (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reporting pneumothorax from the gas lucencies
  • Missing the continuous diaphragm sign
  • Not mentioning oesophageal rupture as a cause to exclude

Case 4: Short (3 marks)

History: 72 year old woman, acute onset colicky abdominal pain, vomiting and absolute constipation. Past history of chronic constipation.

Findings

  • Solitary, markedly distended, haustra-free, kidney-shaped loop arising in the right iliac fossa with its apex towards the left upper quadrant; mildly dilated small bowel; collapsed distal colon; no free gas.

Diagnosis

Caecal volvulus causing large bowel obstruction; CT is the next step to assess viability.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Caecal volvulus
  • Cecal volvulus
  • Caecal bascule

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Caecal volvulus (2)
  • Single dilated haustra-free loop arising from the right iliac fossa pointing to the left upper quadrant (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing sigmoid volvulus, where the loop arises from the pelvis and points to the right upper quadrant
  • Not commenting on free gas or ischaemia
  • Calling it a simple ileus

Case 5: Short (3 marks)

History: 28 year old woman, twisted her foot stepping off a kerb, unable to weight bear, midfoot swelling and plantar bruising.

Findings

  • Malalignment of the second and fourth metatarsal bases with the middle cuneiform and cuboid, widened first to second intermetatarsal interval and a fleck fragment.

Diagnosis

Lisfranc (tarsometatarsal) fracture dislocation.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Lisfranc injury
  • Lisfranc fracture dislocation
  • Tarsometatarsal fracture dislocation

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Lisfranc injury (2)
  • Loss of alignment of the second metatarsal base with the middle cuneiform, or the fleck sign (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reporting an isolated metatarsal fracture
  • Not describing the alignment criteria
  • Missing the fleck sign

Case 6: Short (3 marks)

History: 35 year old motorcyclist, fall onto outstretched hand, severe wrist pain and median nerve paraesthesia.

Findings

  • Triangular lunate on the frontal film with disrupted carpal arcs; on the lateral film the lunate is volarly displaced and tilted while the capitate remains aligned with the radius.

Diagnosis

Volar lunate dislocation (stage IV perilunate instability, lesser arc injury), with acute carpal tunnel syndrome.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Lunate dislocation
  • Volar lunate dislocation
  • Spilled teacup sign

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Lunate dislocation (2)
  • Lunate displaced volarly out of the radial articulation with the capitate remaining aligned with the radius (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing perilunate dislocation, where the lunate stays in the radial fossa and the capitate is displaced
  • Missing the triangular lunate
  • Not relating the findings to median nerve symptoms

Case 21: Medium (6 marks)

History: 32-year-old woman, 3 months of dry cough and tiredness. Tender red lumps on both shins.

Findings

  • Symmetric bilateral hilar and right paratracheal lymphadenopathy without necrosis.
  • Multiple small perilymphatic nodules along bronchovascular bundles, septa and subpleural surfaces, upper and mid zone predominant.
  • No cavitation, effusion or consolidation.

Diagnosis

Pulmonary sarcoidosis with thoracic lymphadenopathy

Differential diagnosis

  • Lymphoma
  • Lymphangitic carcinomatosis
  • Silicosis
  • Tuberculosis

Further investigation and management

Clinical correlation with erythema nodosum supports sarcoidosis. Serum ACE and calcium, respiratory function tests, and tissue sampling (EBUS-guided transbronchial node biopsy) to exclude lymphoma and tuberculosis if atypical.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • sarcoidosis
  • pulmonary sarcoid
  • stage 2 sarcoidosis
