  • Symmetric bilateral hilar and mediastinal lymphadenopathy (1.5)
  • Perilymphatic nodule distribution (1.5)
  • Diagnosis of sarcoidosis (2)
  • Appropriate differential (lymphoma or tuberculosis) (0.5)
  • Next step: tissue sampling or clinical and biochemical correlation (0.5)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the nodules random or miliary rather than perilymphatic
  • Diagnosing lymphoma despite symmetry and absent necrosis
  • Omitting any distribution description

Case 22: Medium (6 marks)

History: 54-year-old man, 3 weeks of fever, productive cough with foul-smelling sputum. History of alcohol excess and a recent seizure.

  • Thick-walled 4.5 cm cavity in the posterior segment of the right upper lobe with an air-fluid level and rim enhancement.

  • Cavitating squamous cell carcinoma
  • Septic emboli

  • lung abscess

  • necrotising pneumonia with abscess formation

  • Appropriate differential (cavitating carcinoma or tuberculosis) (0.75)
  • Next step: cultures, antibiotics, follow-up or bronchoscopy (0.75)

Case 24: Medium (6 marks)

History: 29-year-old woman on the oral contraceptive pill, incidental liver lesion on ultrasound. Normal liver function and no chronic liver disease.

Case 26: Medium (6 marks)

Case 27: Medium (6 marks)

Case 29: Medium (6 marks)

History: 28-year-old woman, 7 weeks amenorrhoea, left iliac fossa pain and light vaginal bleeding. Serum beta-hCG 2,800 IU/L, haemodynamically stable.

Findings

  • No intrauterine pregnancy with thickened endometrium.
  • Left adnexal ring with yolk sac separate from the ovary.
  • Echogenic free fluid in the pelvis extending to Morison pouch, indicating haemoperitoneum.

Diagnosis

Left tubal ectopic pregnancy with haemoperitoneum

Differential diagnosis

  • Corpus luteum cyst
  • Pseudosac of an early intrauterine pregnancy
  • Ruptured haemorrhagic cyst

Further investigation and management

Urgent gynaecology review. Moderate haemoperitoneum with a tubal ring containing a yolk sac indicates a high risk of rupture, so surgical management (laparoscopic salpingectomy or salpingostomy) is usually required; methotrexate is not appropriate with significant free fluid. Group and hold, large-bore intravenous access, repeat beta-hCG after intervention, and anti-D if the woman is Rh(D) negative.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • ectopic pregnancy
  • left tubal pregnancy
  • ruptured ectopic

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Empty uterus (1)
  • Adnexal ring with yolk sac, separate from ovary (1.5)
  • Free fluid and its significance (1)
  • Diagnosis (1.5)
  • Urgent management (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling it a corpus luteum
  • Missing free fluid in Morison pouch
  • Suggesting repeat scan in 48 hours despite free fluid

Case 30: Medium (6 marks)

Case 31: Long (12 marks)

History: 66 year old woman, ex-smoker, 4 months of cough and weight loss. Chest radiograph reported a right upper zone opacity.

Findings

  • Spiculated 45 mm mass in the right upper lobe, FDG avid, without chest wall or mediastinal invasion (T2b by size).
  • Enlarged, FDG avid ipsilateral paratracheal (4R) and subcarinal nodes: multiple ipsilateral mediastinal stations, N2b (TNM 9th edition).
  • Solitary 28 mm left adrenal nodule, indeterminate on CT (38 HU unenhanced) but FDG avid, suspicious for metastasis.
  • Mild background emphysema and two small densely calcified left lung granulomata (benign); no pleural effusion.

Diagnosis

Primary lung carcinoma of the right upper lobe with multistation N2b nodal disease and a solitary left adrenal metastasis, clinical stage IVA (cT2b N2b M1b, TNM 9th edition; M1b is a single extrathoracic metastasis in a single organ), pending tissue confirmation.

Differential diagnosis

  • Lung carcinoma with an incidental lipid-poor left adrenal adenoma and N2b disease: stage IIIB in the 9th edition (IIIA in the 8th edition)
  • Pulmonary lymphoma or carcinoid with nodal and adrenal spread
  • Granulomatous infection (tuberculosis, fungal) with FDG avid nodes

Further investigation and management

Dedicated MRI or CT brain with contrast to complete staging. Tissue diagnosis: biopsy of the most accessible site that would confirm highest stage, for example adrenal or the 4R/subcarinal nodes by EBUS-TBNA, with histology and molecular testing (EGFR, ALK, ROS1, PD-L1). Adrenal biopsy or MRI chemical shift is needed to prove the adrenal lesion is a metastasis before it changes treatment intent. Present at a lung cancer multidisciplinary meeting. If metastasis is confirmed, treatment is systemic (targeted therapy or immunotherapy based on biomarkers) with palliative radiotherapy as needed rather than surgery; if the adrenal nodule proves benign, the disease is cT2b N2b M0, stage IIIB (9th edition), and chemoradiotherapy with consolidation immunotherapy is considered.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • primary lung cancer stage IV
  • non-small cell lung cancer with adrenal metastasis
  • lung carcinoma cT2b N2 M1b

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: primary mass with size and local extent, N2 nodes, adrenal lesion, background chest (4)
  • Diagnosis: lung carcinoma with correct stage, M1b justified (2)
  • Differential: adrenal adenoma, granulomatous disease, lymphoma (2)
  • Further investigation: brain imaging, EBUS or adrenal tissue proof, molecular testing (2)
  • Management: MDT and treatment intent depending on adrenal proof (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the adrenal nodule an adenoma without noting it is FDG avid.
  • Omitting brain imaging from staging.
  • Staging as IIIB and ignoring the adrenal lesion.
  • Not stating that tissue confirmation of M1 disease is required before denying curative treatment.

Case 32: Long (12 marks)

History: 61 year old man, 5 weeks of progressive headache, personality change and a first generalised seizure. Non-contrast CT showed a frontal mass.

Findings

  • 5.5 cm right frontal mass crossing the genu of the corpus callosum into the contralateral hemisphere.
  • Thick irregular nodular peripheral enhancement with a non-enhancing necrotic centre showing facilitated diffusion.
  • Intralesional susceptibility from haemorrhage and extensive vasogenic oedema with 9 mm midline shift.
  • Elevated rCBV in the enhancing rim and raised choline to NAA ratio with lipid and lactate.
  • Solitary lesion, no dural attachment.

Diagnosis

High grade glioma, most likely glioblastoma (IDH wildtype), with butterfly pattern spread across the corpus callosum.

Differential diagnosis

  • Solitary cerebral metastasis
  • Primary CNS lymphoma
  • Cerebral abscess (against: facilitated not restricted diffusion in the cavity, no thin smooth rim)
  • Tumefactive demyelination

Further investigation and management

Systemic staging (CT chest, abdomen and pelvis) if histology is uncertain, to exclude an unrecognised primary. Neurosurgical referral for maximal safe resection with tissue for histology and molecular markers (IDH, MGMT promoter methylation). Postoperative MRI within 48 to 72 hours to assess extent of resection. Dexamethasone for vasogenic oedema and an antiepileptic drug given the presenting seizure. Neuro-oncology multidisciplinary meeting, then radiotherapy with concurrent and adjuvant temozolomide.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • glioblastoma
  • glioblastoma multiforme
  • grade 4 astrocytoma
  • high grade glial tumour

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: location and crossing the corpus callosum, enhancement pattern, necrosis, haemorrhage, oedema and shift, perfusion and spectroscopy (4)
  • Diagnosis: glioblastoma or high grade glioma (2)
  • Differential: metastasis, lymphoma, abscess, demyelination with discriminating feature (2)
  • Further investigation: systemic staging, histology and molecular markers, postoperative MRI (2)
  • Management: surgery, steroids, MDT, chemoradiotherapy (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing abscess without noting the diffusion in the cavity is facilitated.
  • Omitting midline shift or mass effect.
  • Not recommending postoperative MRI timing.
  • Missing that the lesion crosses the corpus callosum.

Case 33: Long (12 marks)

History: 68 year old man, painless jaundice for 2 weeks, 8 kg weight loss and new diabetes. Bilirubin 210 micromol/L.

Findings

  • 30 mm hypoenhancing mass in the pancreatic head and uncinate process.
  • Dilated common bile duct (14 mm) and main pancreatic duct (7 mm) with abrupt cut-off, the double duct sign; distended gallbladder; upstream pancreatic atrophy.
  • Contact with the SMV over about 120 degrees with contour irregularity but patent lumen; no contact with the SMA, coeliac axis or hepatic artery.
  • Subcentimetre portocaval node only; no para-aortic or aortocaval adenopathy; no liver metastases, ascites or peritoneal disease.

Diagnosis

Pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma of the head with biliary and pancreatic duct obstruction, borderline resectable because of SMV involvement of 180 degrees or less with contour irregularity.

Differential diagnosis

  • Pancreatic neuroendocrine tumour
  • Mass-forming (autoimmune IgG4) pancreatitis
  • Distal cholangiocarcinoma or ampullary carcinoma
  • Pancreatic lymphoma

Further investigation and management

Staging CT chest, serum CA 19-9 (interpreted with bilirubin), and MRI liver if any equivocal lesion. EUS with fine needle biopsy for tissue before neoadjuvant therapy. Hepatobiliary multidisciplinary meeting. For a borderline resectable tumour neoadjuvant chemotherapy is usually offered before surgery (pancreaticoduodenectomy). Biliary drainage by ERCP with a metal stent is used if surgery is delayed or cholangitis or neoadjuvant therapy is planned.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • pancreatic adenocarcinoma
  • pancreatic head cancer
  • periampullary mass pancreatic head cancer

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: mass, double duct sign, vessel contact in degrees for SMV and SMA, liver and nodal disease (4)
  • Diagnosis: pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma, borderline resectable (2)
  • Differential: neuroendocrine tumour, autoimmune pancreatitis, cholangiocarcinoma (2)
  • Further investigation: chest CT, CA 19-9, EUS biopsy (2)
  • Management: MDT, neoadjuvant therapy, biliary stenting, surgery (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Describing vessel contact without degrees.
  • Calling the mass unresectable on the basis of any SMV contact.
  • Missing the double duct sign.
  • Stenting before tissue or MDT planning without reason.

Case 34: Long (12 marks)

History: 54 year old man with hypertension, sudden tearing chest pain radiating to the back and a blood pressure difference of 40 mmHg between arms. Troponin mildly raised.

Findings

  • Intimal flap beginning above the aortic valve and extending from the ascending aorta through the arch to the left common iliac artery, with a 46 mm ascending aorta.
  • Compressed true lumen distally and a larger, later enhancing false lumen; brachiocephalic involvement with reduced flow.
  • Left renal artery arising from the false lumen with reduced left renal enhancement (malperfusion); coeliac axis and SMA from the true lumen.
  • Flap near the right coronary ostium, but ostium arising from true lumen.
  • Small high attenuation pericardial effusion (haemopericardium) with no mediastinal rupture.

Diagnosis

Acute aortic dissection, Stanford type A (DeBakey type I), with branch vessel malperfusion and a haemopericardium.

Differential diagnosis

  • Intramural haematoma (crescentic wall thickening without flap)
  • Penetrating atherosclerotic ulcer
  • Contained aortic rupture
  • Pulsation or motion artefact mimicking a flap in the ascending aorta

Further investigation and management

Immediate escalation: this is a surgical emergency and the patient should not be delayed for further imaging. Urgent cardiothoracic surgical referral for ascending aortic and hemiarch repair. Intravenous beta blockade for impulse control, then vasodilator, aiming for systolic pressure 100 to 120 mmHg. Transfer to a cardiac surgical centre with cross-matching, and transthoracic or transoesophageal echocardiography to assess the aortic valve and tamponade if the patient is unstable. Pericardiocentesis is avoided unless there is peri-arrest tamponade. Renal malperfusion and cerebral perfusion are assessed during the repair, and follow-up CT surveillance is arranged postoperatively.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • type A aortic dissection
  • Stanford A dissection
  • DeBakey I aortic dissection

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: flap origin and extent, lumen sizes, branch vessels and malperfusion, aortic diameter, pericardial effusion, absence of rupture (4)
  • Diagnosis: Stanford type A dissection with complications (2)
  • Differential: IMH, PAU, rupture, artefact (2)
  • Further investigation: echocardiography, communicate and no further delay (2)
  • Management: surgery, blood pressure control, avoid pericardiocentesis, transfer (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Failing to state the Stanford type.
  • Missing renal malperfusion.
  • Recommending anticoagulation or thrombolysis.
  • Not communicating urgently.

Case 35: Long (12 marks)

History: 15 year old boy, 6 weeks of worsening pain and swelling above the right knee, worse at night. Alkaline phosphatase raised.

Findings

  • Aggressive distal femoral metaphyseal lesion with mixed sclerosis and lysis and a wide zone of transition.
  • Codman triangle, sunburst periosteal reaction and cloud-like osteoid matrix within a soft tissue mass.
  • MRI: 11 cm intramedullary extent, 5 cm extra-osseous mass, physeal and epiphyseal extension, popliteal artery not encased, separate skip lesion in the same bone.
  • CT chest: multiple subpleural nodules (one calcified), suspicious for pulmonary metastases.

Diagnosis

Conventional osteosarcoma of the distal femur with a skip lesion and probable pulmonary metastases.

Differential diagnosis

  • Ewing sarcoma (diaphyseal, permeative, no osteoid matrix)
  • Osteomyelitis
  • Telangiectasic osteosarcoma or aneurysmal bone cyst
  • Lymphoma of bone

Further investigation and management

Do not biopsy at a peripheral hospital: refer to a sarcoma service first. Image-guided or open biopsy planned by the surgeon so the track can be excised at resection, before any chemotherapy. Whole body staging with PET-CT or bone scintigraphy to find other skeletal lesions, and MRI of the whole femur has already shown the skip lesion. Biopsy of a lung nodule is generally not needed if imaging is typical. Sarcoma multidisciplinary meeting. Neoadjuvant chemotherapy (usually methotrexate, doxorubicin and cisplatin), then limb-salvage surgery with wide resection and reconstruction, and resection of pulmonary metastases where possible.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • osteosarcoma
  • conventional osteosarcoma
  • high grade osteogenic sarcoma

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: radiographic aggressiveness and matrix, MRI extent and physis, neurovascular status, skip lesion, chest CT (4)
  • Diagnosis: osteosarcoma, with metastatic disease (2)
  • Differential: Ewing sarcoma, osteomyelitis, telangiectatic, lymphoma (2)
  • Further investigation: biopsy planning with surgeon, systemic staging (2)
  • Management: neoadjuvant chemotherapy, limb salvage, metastasectomy (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Recommending biopsy before sarcoma service involvement.
  • Not describing the skip lesion.
  • Failing to mention the physis crossing for surgical planning.
  • Calling it Ewing sarcoma despite matrix mineralisation.

Case Reporting Paper 2: Model Reports

Case 36: Short (3 marks)

History: 38-year-old woman of Afro-Caribbean background, 3 months of dry cough and tender red lumps on the shins. Chest radiograph.

Findings

  • Symmetrical bilateral hilar and right paratracheal lymphadenopathy with bilateral upper and mid zone reticulonodular opacities.

Diagnosis

Sarcoidosis, radiographic stage 2

Differential diagnosis

  • Lymphoma
  • Tuberculosis
  • Pneumoconiosis

Further investigation and management

High resolution CT chest; serum ACE and calcium; consider EBUS or bronchoscopic sampling if atypical.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Sarcoid
  • Pulmonary sarcoidosis
  • Sarcoidosis stage II

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Sarcoidosis, radiographic stage 2 (2)
  • Identifies bilateral symmetrical hilar lymphadenopathy with parenchymal opacities. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the appearance lymphoma despite symmetry, well patient and parenchymal disease
  • Omitting the staging
  • Missing the right paratracheal node enlargement

Case 37: Short (3 marks)

History: 17-year-old male with hypertension in the arms and weak femoral pulses. Chest radiograph.

Findings

  • Bilateral inferior rib notching of the 3rd to 8th ribs and a figure of 3 contour of the descending aorta.

Diagnosis

Coarctation of the aorta

Differential diagnosis

  • Pseudocoarctation
  • Neurofibromatosis rib erosion
  • Takayasu arteritis

Further investigation and management

CT or MR angiography of the thoracic aorta; echocardiography; referral for endovascular or surgical repair.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Aortic coarctation
  • Coarctation

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Coarctation of the aorta (2)
  • Describes inferior rib notching with the figure of 3 sign. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Attributing notching to neurofibromatosis without the aortic contour
  • Describing superior rather than inferior rib margin erosion
  • Missing that notching spares the 1st and 2nd ribs

Case 38: Short (3 marks)

History: 72-year-old man with orthopnoea and bilateral leg swelling, acutely short of breath overnight. Chest radiograph.

Findings

  • Cardiomegaly, upper lobe diversion, perihilar alveolar opacity, septal lines and small bilateral effusions.

Diagnosis

Cardiogenic pulmonary oedema

Differential diagnosis

  • Non-cardiogenic pulmonary oedema
  • Atypical pneumonia
  • Pulmonary haemorrhage

Further investigation and management

Echocardiography, BNP, ECG; diuretic therapy and treatment of the underlying cardiac cause.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Pulmonary oedema
  • Left heart failure
  • Acute cardiac failure with pulmonary oedema

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Cardiogenic pulmonary oedema (2)
  • Identifies septal lines at the costophrenic angles. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the appearance pneumonia
  • Reporting only the airspace change and omitting cardiomegaly and effusions
  • Misnaming septal lines as air bronchograms

Case 39: Short (3 marks)

History: 29-year-old man with 5 days of bloody diarrhoea, fever 38.9 C, heart rate 124 and a distended tender abdomen. Supine abdominal radiograph.

Findings

  • Transverse colonic dilatation to 8 cm with loss of haustra and mucosal irregularity.

Diagnosis

Toxic megacolon complicating severe colitis

Differential diagnosis

  • Sigmoid or caecal volvulus
  • Colonic pseudo-obstruction
  • Infective colitis without dilatation

Further investigation and management

Urgent surgical review, intravenous steroids and fluids; avoid barium enema and colonoscopy; serial radiographs and CT if perforation is suspected.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Toxic megacolon
  • Fulminant colitis with colonic dilatation
  • Toxic dilatation of the colon

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Toxic megacolon complicating severe colitis (2)
  • Identifies colonic dilatation exceeding 6 cm with loss of haustra. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing large bowel obstruction rather than colitis
  • Failing to look for free gas
  • Suggesting contrast enema

Case 40: Short (3 marks)

History: 64-year-old woman with sudden severe epigastric pain and a rigid abdomen, on long-term NSAIDs. Supine abdominal radiograph.

Findings

  • Gas outlining both sides of the bowel wall with a visible falciform ligament.

Diagnosis

Pneumoperitoneum from a perforated hollow viscus

Differential diagnosis

  • Gas filled dilated bowel loops
  • Subphrenic abscess gas
  • Post laparotomy free gas

Further investigation and management

Erect chest radiograph or CT abdomen with oral contrast to localise the perforation; urgent surgical review.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Pneumoperitoneum
  • Perforated viscus
  • Free intraperitoneal gas
  • Perforated peptic ulcer

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Pneumoperitoneum from a perforated hollow viscus (2)
  • Identifies the Rigler sign of gas on both sides of the bowel wall. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reporting normal gas distribution on a supine film
  • Missing the falciform ligament sign
  • Not recommending an erect chest film or CT

Case 41: Short (3 marks)

History: 74-year-old man with hip and groin pain and an elevated serum alkaline phosphatase. Pelvis radiograph.

Findings

  • Left hemipelvic bone expansion with coarse trabeculae, cortical thickening and a thickened iliopectineal line.

Diagnosis

Paget disease of bone affecting the left hemipelvis

Differential diagnosis

  • Sclerotic metastases
  • Osteosclerotic lymphoma
  • Fibrous dysplasia

Further investigation and management

Bone scintigraphy to assess extent; bisphosphonate treatment if symptomatic; watch for sarcomatous change if a lytic lesion or soft tissue mass develops.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Paget disease
  • Osteitis deformans
  • Paget disease of bone

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Paget disease of bone affecting the left hemipelvis (2)
  • Identifies bone expansion with coarse trabeculae and cortical thickening. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing sclerotic metastases without noting bone expansion
  • Failing to describe unilateral hemipelvic involvement
  • Omitting the sarcomatous transformation risk

Case 42: Short (3 marks)

History: 15-year-old boy with 4 months of leg pain worse at night and relieved by ibuprofen. Tibia radiograph.

Findings

  • Fusiform cortical thickening and sclerosis around a small round lucent nidus measuring 8 mm.

Diagnosis

Osteoid osteoma

Differential diagnosis

  • Brodie abscess
  • Stress fracture
  • Osteoblastoma

Further investigation and management

CT to confirm the nidus; radiofrequency ablation or surgical excision if symptoms persist despite NSAIDs.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Osteoid osteoma of tibia

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Osteoid osteoma (2)
  • Identifies a lucent nidus under 1.5 cm surrounded by sclerosis. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the sclerosis osteosarcoma
  • Missing the central nidus calcification
  • Diagnosing stress fracture without a lucent focus

Case 43: Short (3 marks)

History: 36-year-old man with 10 years of inflammatory back pain and morning stiffness. Lumbar spine radiograph.

Findings

  • Continuous marginal syndesmophytes, squared vertebrae and fused sacroiliac joints.

Diagnosis

Ankylosing spondylitis with bamboo spine

Differential diagnosis

  • Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis
  • Psoriatic spondyloarthropathy
  • Reactive arthritis

Further investigation and management

MRI of the sacroiliac joints for active inflammation; HLA B27; rheumatology referral for NSAIDs and biologic therapy; screen for osteoporosis and fracture.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Ankylosing spondylitis
  • Bamboo spine
  • Axial spondyloarthritis with bamboo spine

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Ankylosing spondylitis with bamboo spine (2)
  • Identifies thin marginal syndesmophytes with sacroiliac fusion. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing DISH without noting thin marginal syndesmophytes and sacroiliac fusion
  • Describing osteophytes instead of syndesmophytes
  • Omitting sacroiliac joint involvement

Case 44: Short (3 marks)

History: 58-year-old woman with a palpable lump in the upper outer left breast. Mammogram.

Findings

  • Spiculated high density mass with microcalcifications and architectural distortion, with an axillary node.

Diagnosis

Malignant spiculated mass, BI-RADS 5, most likely invasive carcinoma of no special type (ductal)

Differential diagnosis

  • Invasive lobular carcinoma
  • Radial scar
  • Fat necrosis

Further investigation and management

Targeted ultrasound with core biopsy of the mass and axillary node; staging and multidisciplinary meeting.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Breast carcinoma
  • Breast cancer
  • Invasive carcinoma of no special type
  • BI-RADS 5 mass

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: malignant spiculated mass, BI-RADS 5, most likely invasive carcinoma (2)
  • Identifies the spiculated mass with microcalcifications. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the mass benign or BI-RADS 4
  • Omitting the axillary node
  • Diagnosing radial scar without a palpable mass or calcification

Case 45: Short (3 marks)

History: Premature infant born at 29 weeks, day 8 of life, abdominal distension and bloody stools after feeds. Abdominal radiograph.

Findings

  • Dilated bowel with intramural gas and branching gas over the liver.

Diagnosis

Necrotising enterocolitis with pneumatosis intestinalis and portal venous gas

Differential diagnosis

  • Meconium ileus
  • Intestinal atresia
  • Spontaneous isolated ileal perforation

Further investigation and management

Nil by mouth, broad-spectrum antibiotics and surgical review; serial radiographs including a lateral decubitus film for perforation.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Necrotising enterocolitis
  • NEC
  • Pneumatosis intestinalis in a premature infant

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Correct diagnosis: Necrotising enterocolitis with pneumatosis intestinalis and portal venous gas (2)
  • Identifies intramural gas and portal venous gas. (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Mistaking portal venous gas for biliary gas
  • Calling the appearance obstruction without bowel wall gas
  • Omitting a decubitus film for perforation

Case 46: Short (3 marks)

History: 34 year old woman with exertional dyspnoea, mild cyanosis and recurrent epistaxis. Oxygen saturation 90 percent on room air.

Findings

  • Well-defined lobulated right lower zone nodule with a tubular feeding vessel and a draining vessel linking it to the hilum.

Diagnosis

Pulmonary arteriovenous malformation (right lower lobe), best confirmed with CT pulmonary angiography.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Pulmonary AVM
  • Pulmonary arteriovenous fistula
  • Pulmonary arteriovenous malformation

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Pulmonary arteriovenous malformation (2)
  • Nodule with feeding and draining vessels to the hilum (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the lesion a bronchogenic carcinoma or metastasis without noting the connecting vessels
  • Diagnosing a hamartoma or granuloma and missing the vascular link
  • Not relating the findings to hypoxia and epistaxis

Case 47: Short (3 marks)

History: 46 year old woman born overseas with progressive exertional dyspnoea and haemoptysis. Irregularly irregular pulse.

Findings

  • Left atrial enlargement with straight left heart border, double right heart border and splayed carina, with upper lobe venous diversion and Kerley B lines.

Diagnosis

Mitral stenosis with left atrial enlargement and pulmonary venous hypertension (likely rheumatic).

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Mitral valve stenosis
  • Rheumatic mitral stenosis
  • Mitral stenosis

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Mitral stenosis (2)
  • Left atrial enlargement with pulmonary venous hypertension signs (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reporting only cardiomegaly with no specific chamber
  • Diagnosing mitral regurgitation, which usually gives a large left ventricle
  • Calling it simple heart failure without identifying left atrial enlargement

Case 48: Short (3 marks)

History: 72 year old retired boilermaker, ex-shipyard worker, for review before elective surgery. No respiratory symptoms.

Findings

  • Bilateral calcified pleural and diaphragmatic plaques with sharp costophrenic angles and clear lungs.

Diagnosis

Asbestos-related calcified pleural plaques, with asbestos exposure as the cause.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Asbestos-related pleural plaques
  • Calcified pleural plaques due to asbestos exposure
  • Asbestos related pleural disease

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Asbestos-related calcified pleural plaques (2)
  • Bilateral calcified plaques on chest wall and diaphragm (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing mesothelioma although there is no effusion, nodular pleural thickening or volume loss
  • Calling the plaques old tuberculosis
  • Failing to link the findings to the occupational history

Case 49: Short (3 marks)

History: 81 year old woman with 4 days of colicky abdominal pain, vomiting and absolute constipation. Previous episodes of biliary colic.

Findings

  • Small bowel obstruction with pneumobilia and an ectopic calcified gallstone in the right iliac fossa.

Diagnosis

Gallstone ileus: small bowel obstruction from a gallstone passed through a cholecystoenteric fistula.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Gallstone ileus
  • Small bowel obstruction due to impacted gallstone
  • Biliary enteric fistula with gallstone obstruction

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Gallstone ileus (2)
  • Pneumobilia with small bowel obstruction and ectopic gallstone (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reporting simple adhesional small bowel obstruction and ignoring the biliary gas
  • Mistaking the gallstone for a phlebolith or ureteric calculus
  • Describing air in the biliary tree as portal venous gas

Case 50: Short (3 marks)

History: 78 year old woman with postprandial chest pain, early satiety and dysphagia. Mild iron deficiency anaemia.

Findings

  • Retrocardiac air-fluid level; barium shows fundus herniated through the hiatus beside a normally positioned gastro-oesophageal junction.

Diagnosis

Paraoesophageal (type II) hiatus hernia, with risk of gastric volvulus.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Paraoesophageal hernia
  • Type II hiatus hernia
  • Rolling hiatus hernia
  • Para-oesophageal hiatus hernia

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Paraoesophageal hiatus hernia (2)
  • Fundus herniated with the gastro-oesophageal junction below the diaphragm (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Labelling it a sliding hiatus hernia although the junction is in its normal position
  • Diagnosing a lung abscess or mediastinal mass because of the air-fluid level
  • Not mentioning the risk of volvulus or strangulation

Case 51: Short (3 marks)

History: 58 year old woman with renal calculi, bone pain and fatigue. Serum calcium raised, phosphate low, parathyroid hormone raised.

Primary hyperparathyroidism with subperiosteal resorption and a brown tumour of the third metacarpal.

  • Primary hyperparathyroidism (2)
  • Subperiosteal resorption and brown tumour (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing metastasis or myeloma from the lytic lesion alone
  • Calling the lesion an enchondroma or giant cell tumour without noting the resorption
  • Missing the radial-side subperiosteal pattern of the middle phalanges

Case 52: Short (3 marks)

Case 53: Short (3 marks)

Case 54: Short (3 marks)

History: 2 year old child with delayed walking, bowed legs and swelling at the wrists. Prolonged breastfeeding with a limited diet, dark-skinned mother, little sun exposure.

Findings

  • Widened physes with cupped, frayed, splayed metaphyses at wrists and knees, generalised osteopenia and bowing of the lower limbs.

Rickets, most likely nutritional vitamin D deficiency.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Nutritional rickets
  • Vitamin D deficiency rickets
  • Rickets due to vitamin D deficiency
  • Rickets

  • Rickets (2)
  • Widened physes with cupped frayed metaphyses (1)

  • Diagnosing scurvy, where sclerosis of the metaphysis and a Wimberger ring help to distinguish it
  • Calling the bowing physiological and the changes normal

Case 55: Short (3 marks)

History: 62 year old woman attending BreastScreen, asymptomatic. Previous nipple discharge years ago.

Findings

  • Bilateral coarse smooth rod-like calcifications with lucent centres, oriented to the nipple, without mass or distortion.

Diagnosis

Duct ectasia with secretory (plasma cell mastitis) calcifications, a benign finding (BI-RADS 2).

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Secretory calcifications
  • Duct ectasia
  • Benign secretory calcification
  • Plasma cell mastitis calcifications

Marking template (3 marks)

  • Benign secretory calcifications of duct ectasia (2)
  • Bilateral coarse smooth rod-like calcifications oriented to the nipple (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Reporting suspicious calcification and recommending biopsy
  • Confusing with fine linear branching calcification of DCIS, which is thin, irregular and often unilateral
  • Not assigning a benign BI-RADS category

Case 56: Medium (6 marks)

History: 58 year old man, sudden severe interscapular tearing pain, hypertensive, blood pressure equal in both arms.

Findings

  • Intimal flap beginning distal to the left subclavian artery and extending down the abdominal aorta to the infrarenal segment, creating true and false lumina.
  • Ascending aorta and arch are uninvolved.
  • Coeliac axis, superior mesenteric artery and right renal artery arise from the true lumen; left renal artery arises from the false lumen with delayed left renal enhancement, indicating possible malperfusion.
  • Small left pleural effusion; no pericardial effusion or periaortic haematoma suggesting rupture.

Diagnosis

Acute Stanford type B (descending) aortic dissection with left renal artery origin from the false lumen

Differential diagnosis

  • Intramural haematoma
  • Penetrating atherosclerotic ulcer
  • Aortic aneurysm with mural thrombus

Further investigation and management

Urgent vascular or cardiothoracic surgical referral, intravenous beta blockade and analgesia to control heart rate and blood pressure, admission to high dependency, and close monitoring. Uncomplicated type B dissection is managed medically; evidence of branch vessel malperfusion (here the delayed left renal enhancement, to be correlated with renal function and urine output), rupture or refractory pain makes it complicated and is an indication for thoracic endovascular repair.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Stanford type B aortic dissection
  • DeBakey type III dissection
  • descending aortic dissection

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes intimal flap with correct extent from distal to the left subclavian to the infrarenal abdominal aorta (2)
  • States ascending aorta and arch are not involved (1)
  • Describes branch vessel origin and renal perfusion (1)
  • Diagnosis of Stanford type B dissection (1)
  • Differential or management (urgent vascular referral, blood pressure control) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling it type A because of tearing chest pain
  • Omitting branch vessel involvement
  • Not excluding rupture signs such as haemothorax or periaortic haematoma

Case 57: Medium (6 marks)

History: 46 year old woman, 8 months of progressive exertional dyspnoea and dry cough, keeps pet birds at home, no smoking history.

Findings

  • Bilateral upper and mid zone ground glass opacity and poorly defined centrilobular nodules.
  • Mosaic attenuation with three-density pattern, where lucent lobules show air trapping on expiratory imaging.
  • Mild upper zone subpleural reticulation and traction bronchiectasis, indicating early fibrosis; no honeycombing.
  • Basal sparing; no significant lymphadenopathy or pleural disease.

Diagnosis

Fibrotic hypersensitivity pneumonitis (probable avian exposure) with a mosaic, air-trapping and nodular non-fibrotic component

Differential diagnosis

  • Non-specific interstitial pneumonia
  • Sarcoidosis
  • Respiratory bronchiolitis interstitial lung disease

Further investigation and management

Detailed exposure history (birds, mould, humidifiers), antigen avoidance, respiratory function tests, bronchoalveolar lavage showing lymphocytosis, multidisciplinary discussion, and corticosteroids if progressive.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Hypersensitivity pneumonitis
  • Extrinsic allergic alveolitis
  • Fibrotic hypersensitivity pneumonitis

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes ground glass opacity and centrilobular nodules with zonal distribution (2)
  • Identifies mosaic attenuation with air trapping on expiratory images (1)
  • Notes early fibrosis (reticulation, traction bronchiectasis) without honeycombing (1)
  • Diagnosis of hypersensitivity pneumonitis (1)
  • Differential or management including antigen avoidance (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing sarcoidosis despite absent lymphadenopathy
  • Missing the air trapping because expiratory images were not assessed
  • Calling the pattern usual interstitial pneumonia

Case 58: Medium (6 marks)

History: 67 year old man with painless haematuria and a 6 kg weight loss.

Findings

  • 7.5 cm heterogeneously enhancing right upper pole renal mass with central necrosis and no macroscopic fat.
  • Enhancing tumour thrombus in the right renal vein extending into the IVC above the renal veins, with the cranial tip below the hepatic vein confluence (Mayo level II).
  • Perinephric fat and Gerota fascia intact (no T3a perinephric invasion); small 8 mm paracaval nodes of uncertain significance (cN0 versus cN1, below size criteria).
  • Left kidney, adrenal glands and liver are normal.

Diagnosis

Right renal cell carcinoma, cT3b (7.5 cm; tumour thrombus in the renal vein and in the IVC below the diaphragm, Mayo level II, below the hepatic veins)

Differential diagnosis

  • Transitional cell carcinoma of the renal pelvis
  • Renal lymphoma
  • Bland thrombus

Further investigation and management

Staging CT of the chest and discussion at a urology multidisciplinary meeting; MRI can confirm the cranial extent of thrombus. Radical nephrectomy with thrombectomy is the usual treatment, so the cranial level of thrombus relative to the hepatic veins should be reported for surgical planning.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Renal cell carcinoma with IVC thrombus
  • right renal malignancy with venous invasion

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes mass size, enhancement and absence of fat (1)
  • Describes venous extension and its cranial level (2)
  • Comments on perinephric invasion and nodes (1)
  • Diagnosis of renal cell carcinoma with tumour thrombus (1)
  • Next step (chest staging, urology MDM) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling it angiomyolipoma
  • Missing the IVC extension
  • Not reporting the thrombus level relative to the hepatic veins

Case 59: Medium (6 marks)

History: 52 year old woman, incidental liver lesion on ultrasound during assessment of abdominal discomfort, normal liver function tests, no known liver disease or malignancy.

Findings

  • 3.2 cm well defined lobulated segment 7 lesion, markedly T2 hyperintense and T1 hypointense.
  • Peripheral nodular discontinuous arterial enhancement with progressive centripetal fill-in on later phases, following blood pool.
  • No restricted diffusion, washout, capsule or vascular invasion.
  • Background liver normal.

Diagnosis

Hepatic haemangioma (benign)

Differential diagnosis

  • Hypervascular metastasis
  • Hepatocellular carcinoma
  • Focal nodular hyperplasia

Further investigation and management

No treatment or follow-up required for a typical haemangioma in an asymptomatic patient; further imaging only if atypical features or enlargement.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Cavernous haemangioma
  • Liver haemangioma

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes markedly T2 hyperintense lesion with correct signal on T1 (1)
  • Describes peripheral nodular discontinuous enhancement with centripetal fill-in (2)
  • States absence of washout, capsule or restricted diffusion (1)
  • Diagnosis of hepatic haemangioma (1)
  • Differential or statement that no treatment is needed (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing hepatocellular carcinoma because of arterial enhancement
  • Confusing with focal nodular hyperplasia that has a central scar and uniform arterial enhancement
  • Recommending biopsy of a typical haemangioma

Case 60: Medium (6 marks)

History: 61 year old man, 4 months of right shoulder pain at night and weakness of abduction, no definite trauma.

Findings

  • Full-thickness supraspinatus tear with fluid-filled gap, 1.8 cm in anteroposterior width, with tendon retraction to the level of the humeral head.
  • Subacromial-subdeltoid bursal fluid.
  • Moderate fatty infiltration of the supraspinatus muscle belly without marked atrophy.
  • Infraspinatus, subscapularis and biceps tendons intact; acromioclavicular osteophyte contributing to impingement.

Diagnosis

Full-thickness supraspinatus tendon tear with retraction and moderate fatty infiltration

Differential diagnosis

  • Supraspinatus tendinosis or partial-thickness tear
  • Calcific tendinitis
  • Adhesive capsulitis

Further investigation and management

Orthopaedic referral; repair is considered by tear size, retraction and fatty infiltration, since advanced infiltration lowers the chance of successful repair; physiotherapy for those unsuitable for surgery.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Full-thickness rotator cuff tear
  • Supraspinatus tear

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes full-thickness fluid-filled defect with size and retraction (2)
  • Reports muscle fatty infiltration or atrophy (1)
  • Comments on other cuff tendons, biceps and bursa (1)
  • Diagnosis of full-thickness supraspinatus tear (1)
  • Differential or surgical relevance of findings (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the tear partial thickness
  • Omitting retraction and fatty infiltration, which determine repairability
  • Not checking the other rotator cuff tendons

Case 61: Medium (6 marks)

History: 19 year old woman, chronic right hip pain and a limp, pain worse after an incidental minor fall.

Findings

  • Expansile lytic lesion in the proximal right femur with ground glass matrix and a sclerotic rim.
  • Cortical thinning with endosteal scalloping; no periosteal reaction or soft tissue mass.
  • Varus deformity of the femoral neck giving a shepherd's crook appearance.
  • No fracture and no joint involvement; remainder of the pelvis normal.

Diagnosis

Fibrous dysplasia of the proximal right femur (monostotic) with shepherd's crook deformity

Differential diagnosis

  • Non-ossifying fibroma or simple bone cyst
  • Paget disease of bone
  • Low grade intramedullary osteosarcoma

Further investigation and management

Orthopaedic referral to assess fracture risk and need for prophylactic fixation; MRI or bone scan to assess extent; consider skeletal survey for polyostotic disease and endocrine review for McCune-Albright syndrome.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Fibrous dysplasia
  • Monostotic fibrous dysplasia

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes ground glass matrix with expansile, well defined lesion (2)
  • Identifies cortical thinning and absence of aggressive features (1)
  • Recognises shepherd's crook deformity (1)
  • Diagnosis of fibrous dysplasia (1)
  • Differential or next step (orthopaedic review, fracture risk, polyostotic survey) (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing Paget disease in a young patient with no cortical thickening
  • Calling the lesion aggressive because of cortical thinning
  • Not commenting on fracture risk

Case 62: Medium (6 marks)

History: 31 year old woman with an episode of left optic neuritis two years ago and now right leg weakness and numbness.

Findings

  • Multiple ovoid periventricular T2 and FLAIR lesions perpendicular to the ventricles, including the callososeptal interface.
  • Juxtacortical and infratentorial lesions in the right middle cerebellar peduncle.
  • Two lesions show incomplete ring enhancement, denoting active inflammation; others do not enhance, indicating dissemination in time.
  • No mass effect or haemorrhage.
  • One left corona radiata lesion shows restricted diffusion, in keeping with an acute active plaque and concordant with the right leg symptoms; no cortical infarct pattern.

Diagnosis

Multiple sclerosis with lesions disseminated in space and time

Differential diagnosis

  • Acute disseminated encephalomyelitis
  • Small vessel ischaemic change
  • Neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder

Further investigation and management

MRI of the spinal cord with contrast, CSF oligoclonal bands and clinical correlation using the McDonald criteria; aquaporin 4 antibody testing if features are atypical.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • MS
  • demyelinating disease consistent with multiple sclerosis

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes lesion morphology and distribution (2)
  • Describes enhancement and dissemination in time (1)
  • States absence of mass effect or haemorrhage (1)
  • Diagnosis of multiple sclerosis (1)
  • Spinal MRI or other next step (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing small vessel disease in a young patient
  • Failing to identify dissemination in time
  • Omitting spinal cord imaging

Case 63: Medium (6 marks)

History: 24 year old man, 5 days sore throat, trismus, drooling and a muffled voice, fever.

Findings

  • 2.5 cm rim enhancing collection with gas lateral to the right palatine tonsil.
  • Mass effect with uvular displacement and oropharyngeal narrowing.
  • Reactive right level II nodes; fat stranding.
  • Retropharyngeal and parapharyngeal spaces and the internal jugular vein are uninvolved.

  • Tonsillitis with phlegmon
  • Retropharyngeal abscess

  • peritonsillar abscess
  • quinsy

  • States deep neck spaces and vein are not involved (1)

Case 64: Medium (6 marks)

History: 26 year old woman, intermittent lower abdominal discomfort, normal tumour markers.

Findings

  • 6 cm unilocular right ovarian lesion with a densely echogenic mural nodule and posterior acoustic shadowing (dermoid plug).
  • Floating echogenic lines and dots (dermoid mesh).
  • No internal vascularity, solid component or papillary projection.
  • Left ovary and uterus normal; no free fluid.

Diagnosis

Right ovarian mature cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst), O-RADS US 2

Differential diagnosis

  • Haemorrhagic cyst
  • Endometrioma
  • Ovarian cystadenofibroma

Further investigation and management

Conservative surveillance or laparoscopic cystectomy depending on symptoms and size; warn about the risk of torsion, particularly if the lesion is larger than 5 cm.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • dermoid cyst
  • mature cystic teratoma

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Describes the dermoid plug with shadowing (2)
  • Describes absence of vascularity or solid component (1)
  • Describes contralateral ovary and fluid (1)
  • Diagnosis of mature cystic teratoma (1)
  • O-RADS category, torsion risk or management (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling the lesion an endometrioma
  • Failing to state absent vascularity
  • Missing the torsion risk

Case 65: Medium (6 marks)

History: 5 week old first-born boy, progressive projectile non-bilious vomiting after every feed, hungry after vomiting, weight loss.

Findings

  • Thickened hypoechoic pyloric muscle, 5 mm in thickness, and elongated pyloric canal of 19 mm.
  • No passage of gastric contents through the canal during the study, with distended stomach and active peristalsis.
  • Muscle bulging into the antrum; no other abnormality of liver, gallbladder or kidney.

Diagnosis

Hypertrophic pyloric stenosis

Differential diagnosis

  • Gastro-oesophageal reflux
  • Pylorospasm
  • Malrotation with midgut volvulus

Further investigation and management

Urgent paediatric surgical referral; correct dehydration and hypochloraemic hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis with intravenous fluids before pyloromyotomy.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Pyloric stenosis
  • Infantile hypertrophic pyloric stenosis

Marking template (6 marks)

  • Reports pyloric muscle thickness above 3 mm (1)
  • Reports canal length above 15 mm and failure of gastric emptying (2)
  • Notes normal adjacent organs or absence of other cause (1)
  • Diagnosis of hypertrophic pyloric stenosis (1)
  • Next step: surgical referral and metabolic correction (1)

Common candidate errors

  • Diagnosing pylorospasm when measurements exceed thresholds
  • Forgetting the metabolic alkalosis and its correction before surgery
  • Recommending barium meal as first test

Case 66: Long (12 marks)

History: 58 year old man, 3 months of rectal bleeding and change in bowel habit. Colonoscopy found an obstructing lesion; biopsy awaited.

Findings

  • Low rectal circumferential tumour 5 cm long, lower edge 4 cm from the anal verge, below the peritoneal reflection; sphincter complex and intersphincteric plane clear.
  • Extension 8 mm into the mesorectal fat (mrT3c, greater than 5 to 15 mm).
  • Tumour edge 2 mm from the mesorectal fascia on the right anterolateral aspect, so the circumferential resection margin is threatened (1 to 2 mm).
  • Extramural venous invasion present.
  • Two mesorectal nodes with irregular border and mixed signal, suspicious for metastasis (mrN1b: 2 to 3 suspicious nodes); no sidewall nodes; adjacent organs free.

Diagnosis

Locally advanced low rectal adenocarcinoma, mrT3c N1b with threatened circumferential resection margin and EMVI positive.

Differential diagnosis

  • Rectal neuroendocrine tumour
  • Rectal lymphoma or gastrointestinal stromal tumour
  • Locally invasive prostate or gynaecological primary (excluded by clear adjacent organs)

Further investigation and management

Confirm histology on the colonoscopy biopsy. CT chest, abdomen and pelvis for distant staging, with CEA. Rectal cancer multidisciplinary meeting. Because the mesorectal fascia is threatened and EMVI is present, neoadjuvant therapy is recommended, either short course radiotherapy or long course chemoradiotherapy or total neoadjuvant therapy, followed by a repeat MRI to restage and assess response before surgery (total mesorectal excision) to give the best chance of an R0 resection.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • rectal adenocarcinoma with threatened CRM
  • locally advanced rectal cancer
  • mrT3 rectal cancer with EMVI
  • low rectal cancer with threatened CRM

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: level and extent, depth of extramural spread, distance to mesorectal fascia in mm, EMVI, nodes and adjacent organs (4)
  • Diagnosis: rectal adenocarcinoma stated with mrT3 and threatened CRM (2)
  • Differential: neuroendocrine tumour, lymphoma, GIST (2)
  • Further investigation: histology, CT staging, CEA (2)
  • Management: neoadjuvant therapy, restaging MRI, TME (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Not reporting the distance to the mesorectal fascia in millimetres.
  • Counting node size alone and ignoring border and signal.
  • Not mentioning EMVI.
  • Recommending immediate surgery despite a threatened margin.

Case 67: Long (12 marks)

History: 72 year old woman, sudden right hemiparesis and expressive aphasia, onset witnessed 90 minutes ago. NIHSS 16. On apixaban for atrial fibrillation, last dose 8 hours ago.

Findings

  • Hyperdense left MCA sign and subtle left insular ribbon loss; ASPECTS 9; no haemorrhage.
  • CTA: abrupt occlusion of the left M1 segment with preserved ICA and moderate pial collaterals.
  • CT perfusion: small core (18 mL) and large hypoperfused tissue (110 mL), mismatch volume 92 mL (ratio about 6).
  • Mild carotid bifurcation atheroma; no other large vessel occlusion.

Diagnosis

Acute ischaemic stroke from left M1 middle cerebral artery occlusion with a small infarct core and large salvageable penumbra.

Differential diagnosis

  • Cardioembolic versus in situ atherothrombotic occlusion
  • Intracranial haemorrhage (excluded on CT)
  • Stroke mimic (seizure with Todd paresis, hemiplegic migraine)
  • Carotid or arch dissection (ICA patent here)

Further investigation and management

MRI or repeat CT at 24 hours to define final infarct and exclude haemorrhagic transformation; cardiac assessment (ECG monitoring, echocardiography) and review of anticoagulation. Immediate management: activate the stroke code and discuss urgent endovascular thrombectomy, which is indicated for proximal large vessel occlusion with favourable core and mismatch. Intravenous thrombolysis is considered but recent DOAC use within 48 hours is a contraindication unless levels are low or reversal is given, so decision is made with the stroke neurologist without delaying thrombectomy.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • left MCA occlusion
  • acute left MCA infarct with penumbra
  • M1 occlusion large vessel occlusion stroke

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: hyperdense vessel sign, ASPECTS, CTA occlusion site and collaterals, perfusion core and penumbra volumes (4)
  • Diagnosis: acute left M1 occlusion with salvageable penumbra (2)
  • Differential: haemorrhage, mimic, dissection (2)
  • Further investigation: follow-up imaging, cardiac work-up (2)
  • Management: thrombectomy and thrombolysis decision with DOAC caveat (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Not reporting ASPECTS.
  • Ignoring the DOAC when discussing thrombolysis.
  • Reporting the CTP mismatch without volumes.
  • Delaying thrombectomy for MRI.

Case 68: Long (12 marks)

History: 24 year old man, 8 months of intermittent right-sided abdominal pain, diarrhoea and weight loss. Faecal calprotectin raised. Colonoscopy could not reach the terminal ileum.

Findings

  • Long segment terminal ileal mural thickening with mucosal hyperenhancement, layering, comb sign and fibrofatty proliferation.
  • Short distal stricture with upstream small bowel dilatation (3.6 cm) and small bowel faeces sign.
  • Small ileocaecal peri-enteric collection with a fistulous track to an adjacent ileal loop (enteroenteric fistula).
  • Reactive mesenteric nodes; colon and solid organs normal; no perforation.

Diagnosis

Crohn disease of the terminal ileum, active inflammatory, with a stricture, small bowel obstruction features and a penetrating complication (phlegmon or small abscess with enteroenteric fistula).

Differential diagnosis

  • Ileocaecal tuberculosis
  • Small bowel lymphoma
  • Yersinia or other infective ileitis
  • Neuroendocrine tumour or ileal adenocarcinoma

Further investigation and management

MR enterography or repeat ultrasound can separate inflammatory from fibrotic stricture and assess collection size. Ileocolonoscopy with biopsy (or enteroscopy) for histological confirmation, plus stool culture, and tuberculosis testing in an at-risk patient. Gastroenterology and colorectal surgical review at an IBD multidisciplinary meeting. Treat sepsis if present: antibiotics and percutaneous drainage if the collection is greater than 3 cm. Biological therapy for active disease without a fixed fibrotic stricture; surgical resection or stricturoplasty for a symptomatic stricture with penetrating complications.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • Crohn disease
  • Crohn's disease
  • terminal ileitis with Crohn disease

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: length and wall features, comb sign and fat, stricture with upstream dilatation, collection and fistula (4)
  • Diagnosis: Crohn disease with complications (2)
  • Differential: tuberculosis, lymphoma, Yersinia, neoplasm (2)
  • Further investigation: MR enterography, endoscopic biopsy, TB testing (2)
  • Management: MDT, drainage and antibiotics, biologic or surgery (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Not distinguishing inflammatory from fibrostenotic disease.
  • Omitting the fistula.
  • Missing small bowel obstruction features.
  • No mention of tuberculosis in the differential.

Case 69: Long (12 marks)

History: 71 year old man, 18 months of progressive exertional breathlessness and dry cough. Bibasal inspiratory crackles with digital clubbing. Restrictive spirometry with reduced DLCO. Former smoker, no occupational dust exposure, no bird exposure.

Findings

  • Subpleural and basal predominant reticulation with traction bronchiectasis.
  • Honeycombing in multiple layers at the lung bases.
  • Limited ground-glass opacity within fibrotic zones; no air trapping, centrilobular nodules or upper lobe predominance.
  • No pleural plaques; reactive mediastinal nodes; 6 mm right upper lobe nodule needing follow-up per Fleischner.

Diagnosis

Fibrotic interstitial lung disease with a usual interstitial pneumonia (UIP) pattern, in this clinical context consistent with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis.

Differential diagnosis

  • Fibrotic hypersensitivity pneumonitis (would show air trapping, centrilobular nodules, upper or mid zone)
  • Connective tissue disease associated ILD
  • Asbestosis (pleural plaques absent)
  • Fibrotic NSIP

Further investigation and management

No biopsy is needed when the HRCT shows a UIP pattern in the right clinical context. Review at an ILD multidisciplinary meeting including respiratory physician, radiologist and pathologist. Exclude exposures (birds, mould, drugs such as amiodarone or nitrofurantoin) and test for connective tissue disease with autoantibodies. Full pulmonary function tests and a six minute walk test for baseline. Treat with an antifibrotic (nintedanib or pirfenidone), consider pulmonary rehabilitation and oxygen, and discuss lung transplant referral if suitable. Follow the 6 mm nodule by Fleischner guidance, and watch for lung cancer and acute exacerbation.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis
  • UIP pattern
  • usual interstitial pneumonia

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: distribution, honeycombing, traction bronchiectasis, features excluding alternatives, nodule (4)
  • Diagnosis: UIP pattern, IPF in context (2)
  • Differential: HP, CTD-ILD, asbestosis, NSIP (2)
  • Further investigation: exposures, serology, PFTs, MDT (2)
  • Management: antifibrotic, rehabilitation, transplant, nodule follow-up (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Calling fibrosis NSIP despite honeycombing.
  • Recommending lung biopsy for a UIP pattern.
  • Missing the lung nodule.
  • Not mentioning air trapping as the discriminator for HP.

Case 70: Long (12 marks)

History: 54 year old woman recalled from BreastScreen after a screening mammogram. No palpable lump, no family history.

Findings

  • 22 mm spiculated irregular mass with architectural distortion in the right upper outer quadrant on mammography.
  • Fine pleomorphic calcifications in a segmental distribution extending beyond the mass.
  • Ultrasound: irregular hypoechoic taller-than-wide mass with shadowing; axillary node with thickened cortex (5 mm) and absent hilum.
  • MRI: avid enhancement with washout, a second 6 mm enhancing focus 3 cm away (multifocal disease); chest wall and skin spared; left breast clear.

Diagnosis

Highly suspicious right breast malignancy, likely invasive carcinoma (BI-RADS 5), with suspicious axillary node and a possible second focus.

Differential diagnosis

  • Radial scar or complex sclerosing lesion (less likely with calcifications and node)
  • Fat necrosis or granulomatous mastitis
  • Phyllodes tumour or lymphoma
  • Invasive lobular carcinoma versus ductal carcinoma NST (histological question)

Further investigation and management

Ultrasound-guided core biopsy of the mass with clip placement, and core biopsy or FNA of the abnormal axillary node. Biopsy of the 6 mm MRI focus (second look ultrasound or MRI-guided) if it would alter surgery. Histology with ER, PR and HER2. Multidisciplinary meeting. Treatment depends on pathology: breast conserving surgery versus mastectomy given multifocality, axillary surgery or neoadjuvant systemic therapy for node-positive disease, followed by radiotherapy. Bilateral staging is complete with normal left breast; systemic staging imaging is not routinely needed for early disease unless symptomatic or high stage.

Also acceptable for the diagnosis mark

  • invasive ductal carcinoma
  • BI-RADS 5 breast cancer
  • breast carcinoma with axillary node involvement

Marking template (12 marks)

  • Findings: mass morphology, calcifications, axillary node, MRI extent and second focus, assessment category (4)
  • Diagnosis: BI-RADS 5 breast carcinoma, likely invasive (2)
  • Differential: radial scar, fat necrosis, phyllodes or lymphoma, ILC vs NST (2)
  • Further investigation: core biopsy with clip, node sampling, MRI-focus biopsy, receptor status (2)
  • Management: MDT and treatment depending on pathology (2)

Common candidate errors

  • Not assigning a BI-RADS category.
  • Missing the second focus on MRI.
  • Ignoring the abnormal axillary node.
  • Recommending FNA of the breast mass instead of core biopsy.
